. However, the constructed Lagrangian is a GCT invariant only if the diff field is a tensor. In fact, if it is not a tensor then its covariant derivative is not defined. This is an important flaw of the transverse method which we will try to cure later in the thesis. Again, in analogy with the YM case, the term involving
\Lambda
should represent a gauge transformation of the diff field. However, in building the interaction of the diff field with the gauge field, diff field was treated as a gauge-invariant object
[
BLR00
]
. We will handle this here since its solution is simpler.

Recall that in (
3.93
) we obtained a gauge-invariant object
\tilde{D}
from the Virasoro coadjoint element
D
. Since, in 2D, we identified
D
with the
D_{11}
component of the diff field in
4.3.1
, and
A
with the
A_{1}
component of the YM field we can rewrite (
3.93
) as

\displaystyle\tilde{D}_{11}=D_{11}+\frac{e}{2}\text{Tr}(A_{1}A_{1})

(4.104)

We can easily extend this to higher dimensions as

\displaystyle\tilde{D}_{\mu\nu}\equiv D_{\mu\nu}+\frac{e}{2}\,\text{Tr}\,(A_{\mu}A_{\nu})

(4.105)

Note that in this extension, temporal gauge
A_{0}=0
of YM theory corresponds to the full temporal gauge
D_{\mu 0}=0
of the diff field theory. So the gauge-invariant extension (
4.105
) is compatible with the gauge fixing arguments of the transverse formalism.

Recall that the point particle and spinor interactions of the diff field suggested the diff field may be a perturbation to the spacetime metric. Motivated with this, we are going to use the gauge-invariant extension (
4.105
) in a setting where the metric and gauge field meet, namely the Born-Infeld action.
A mass term included in the YM action or the Born-Infeld action breaks the gauge invariance, yet through (
4.105
), inclusion of the diff field into the picture may yield a mass term for the gauge field without breaking gauge invariance. We are going to investigate this possibility by incorporating the diff field via the nonabelian extension of the Born-Infeld action
[
tseytlinnonabelianborninfeld
]
.

The nonabelian Born-Infeld action reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\text{STr}\sqrt{\det(g_{\mu\nu}+cF_{\mu\nu})}-\sqrt{g}

(4.106)

In this expression, the determinant is taken only in the indices
\mu,\nu
whereas the symmetric trace is defined as

\displaystyle\text{STr}\,(A_{1}\cdots A_{n})=\frac{1}{n!}\text{Tr}\,(A_{1}\cdots A_{n}+\text{all permutations})

(4.107)

where the trace Tr is taken in the fundamental representation over the Lie algebra indices of the gauge field. The curvature tensor is given by

\displaystyle F_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}+e[A_{\mu},A_{\nu}]

(4.108)

Introducing
\kappa\tilde{D}_{\mu\nu}
into the determinant (
4.106
) for some constant
\kappa
, the Lagrangian becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}+\sqrt{g}

\displaystyle=\text{STr}\,\sqrt{\det(g_{\mu\nu}+cF_{\mu\nu}+\kappa\tilde{D}_{\mu\nu})}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{g}\,\text{STr}\,\sqrt{\det(\delta^{\mu}_{\ \nu}+cF^{\mu}_{\ \nu}+\kappa\tilde{D}^{\mu}_{\ \nu})}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{g}\,\text{STr}\,\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\,\text{tr}\,\ln(\delta^{\mu}_{\ \nu}+cF^{\mu}_{\ \nu}+\kappa\tilde{D}^{\mu}_{\ \nu})\right)

(4.109)

Using the Taylor series

\displaystyle\ln(1+x)=x-\frac{x^{2}}{2}+\frac{x^{3}}{3}+\cdots

(4.110)

for matrices, the logarithm can be expanded to second-order in
c
and
\kappa

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}+\sqrt{g}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{g}\,\text{STr}\,\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\,\text{tr}\left[cF^{\mu}_{\ \nu}+\kappa\tilde{D}^{\mu}_{\ \nu}-\frac{1}{2}(cF^{\mu}_{\ \nu}+\kappa\tilde{D}^{\mu}_{\ \nu})^{2}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{g}\,\text{STr}\,\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\kappa\tilde{D}^{\mu}_{\ \mu}-\frac{1}{4}\text{tr}\,(cF^{\mu}_{\ \nu}+\kappa\tilde{D}^{\mu}_{\ \nu})^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{g}\,\text{STr}\,\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\kappa\tilde{D}^{\mu}_{\ \mu}-\frac{1}{4}c^{2}F^{\mu}_{\ \nu}F^{\mu}_{\ \nu}-\frac{1}{4}\kappa^{2}\tilde{D}^{\mu}_{\ \nu}\tilde{D}^{\mu}_{\ \nu}\right)

(4.111)

where we used antisymmetry of
F
and symmetry of
\tilde{D}
.
Expanding the exponential to second-order in
c
and
\kappa
we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\sqrt{g}\,\text{STr}\left(\frac{1}{2}\kappa\tilde{D}^{\mu}_{\ \mu}-\frac{1}{4}c^{2}F^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{4}\kappa^{2}\Big(\tilde{D}^{\mu\nu}\tilde{D}_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{D}^{\mu}_{\ \mu})^{2}\Big)\right)

(4.112)

Now, if we treat the diff field part of
\tilde{D}_{\mu\nu}
as a linear perturbation to the metric, to second-order in
A
and to first-order in
D
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{L}}{\sqrt{g}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa}{2}\,D^{\mu}_{\ \mu}+\frac{e\kappa}{4}\,\text{Tr}\,(A^{\mu}A_{\mu})-\frac{e\kappa^{2}}{4}\,D^{\mu\nu}\,\text{Tr}\,(A_{\mu}A_{\nu})

\displaystyle+\frac{e\kappa^{2}}{8}\,D^{\mu}_{\ \mu}\,\text{Tr}\,(A^{\nu}A_{\nu})-\frac{c^{2}}{4}\,\text{STr}\,(F^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}F^{(0)\mu\nu})

(4.113)

where
F^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}\equiv\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}
. We can rewrite this result more symmetrically as

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{L}}{\sqrt{g}}=\frac{\kappa}{2}\,D^{\mu}_{\ \mu}

\displaystyle+\frac{e\kappa}{4}\left(g^{\mu\nu}-\kappa D^{\mu\nu}+\frac{e\kappa}{2}g^{\mu\nu}D^{\lambda}_{\ \lambda}\right)\text{Tr}\,(A_{\mu}A_{\nu})

\displaystyle-\frac{c^{2}}{4}\,\text{STr}\,(F^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}F^{(0)\mu\nu})

(4.114)

The first term on the right is a correction to the metric determinant as can be shown using the expansion

\displaystyle\det(B+\epsilon C)=\det(B)+\epsilon\,\text{Tr}\,(B^{-1}C)+O(\epsilon^{2})

(4.115)

For
B_{\mu\nu}=g_{\mu\nu}
,
\epsilon=\kappa
and
C_{\mu\nu}=D_{\mu\nu}
this yields

\displaystyle\sqrt{g+\kappa D}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{g+\kappa\,g\,g^{\mu\nu}D_{\mu\nu}+O(\kappa^{2})}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{g}\left(1+\frac{\kappa}{2}\,D^{\mu}_{\ \mu}+O(\kappa^{2})\right)

(4.116)

verifying the claim.
Finally, the second term on the right of (
4.4.4
) is a mass term for the gauge field.

4.4.5
Diff Field Interacting with Spin One Field

It is stated in
[
BLR00
]
that spin-one coupling of the diff field is a good test for the prescription (
4.80
) since it should have non-trivial contributions to the isotropy equations for both
A
and
D
fields. Upon including this interaction term, the isotropy equations for both KM and Virasoro algebras should be reproduced from the field theory upon reduction to the 1D spatial hypersurface of a 2D flat spacetime as field equations of
A_{0}
and
D_{\mu 0}
followed by the background conditions
A_{0}=0=D_{\mu 0}
.

Let us apply (
4.80
) to spin-one coupling.
The covariant YM action (with
e=1
) yields the following generalized momentum

\displaystyle\pi^{\rho\lambda}=\sqrt{g}F^{\rho\lambda}=\sqrt{g}\ (\partial^{\rho}A^{\lambda}-\partial^{\lambda}A^{\rho}+[A^{\rho},A^{\lambda}])

(4.117)

Spatial Lie derivative of the gauge field lifts to the covariant diff variation as

\displaystyle\delta A_{i}

\displaystyle=\xi^{k}\partial_{k}A_{i}+A_{k}\partial_{i}\xi^{k}

\displaystyle\rightarrow D^{k}_{\ 0}\partial_{k}A_{i}+A_{k}\partial_{i}D^{k}_{\ 0}

\displaystyle\rightarrow D^{\alpha}_{\ \rho}\nabla_{\alpha}A_{\lambda}+A_{\alpha}\nabla_{\lambda}D^{\alpha}_{\ \rho}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ =D^{\alpha}_{\ \rho}\partial_{\alpha}A_{\lambda}+A_{\alpha}\partial_{\lambda}D^{\alpha}_{\ \rho}\equiv(\delta_{D}A)^{(0)}_{\rho\lambda}

(4.118)

Then the interaction term becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{int}}=\text{Tr}\,\Big(\pi^{\rho\lambda}(\delta_{D}A)_{[\rho\lambda]}\Big)

(4.119)

where we antisymmetrized the diff variation
(\delta_{D}A)_{\rho\lambda}
since
\pi^{\rho\lambda}
is antisymmetric.

In
[
BLR00
]
the suggested covariant diff-variation is different from (
4.118
):

\displaystyle(\tilde{\delta}_{D}A)_{\rho\lambda}=D^{\alpha}_{\ \rho}\partial_{\alpha}A_{\lambda}+A_{\alpha}\partial_{\lambda}D^{\alpha}_{\ \rho}-\partial_{\rho}(D^{\alpha}_{\ \lambda}A_{\alpha})

(4.120)

We shall call the interaction obtained from the modified diff variation the BLRY interaction

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{int}}=\text{Tr}\,\Big(\pi^{\rho\lambda}(\delta_{D}A)_{[\rho\lambda]}\Big)

(4.121)

We will investigate the BLRY and unmodified interactions below. First, let us review the arguments in
[
BLR00
]
.
It is stated that when
D_{10}=0
,
A_{0}
has no conjugate momentum even in the presence of the diff field. This is true as we show below.
The argument continues as follows. This interaction term is still not gauge invariant. One may preserve gauge invariance by introducing a group-valued scalar field
V
, transforming under right multiplication by a group element
h
as
V\rightarrow Vh
. Then the interaction Lagrangian is modified to

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{int}}=\sqrt{g}\ \text{Tr}\,\Big(F^{\rho\lambda}(D^{\alpha}_{\ \rho}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{A}_{\lambda}+\tilde{A}_{\alpha}\partial_{\lambda}D^{\alpha}_{\ \rho}-\partial_{\rho}(D^{\alpha}_{\ \lambda}\tilde{A}_{\alpha}))\Big)

(4.122)

with

\displaystyle\tilde{A}_{\mu}=A_{\mu}-V^{-1}\partial_{\mu}V

(4.123)

The following is suggested as the Lagrangian of the
V
field

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{V}=m^{2}_{A}\int d^{n}x\ (V^{-1}\partial_{\mu}V-A_{\mu})(V^{-1}\partial_{\mu}V-A_{\nu})(g^{\mu\nu}+D^{\mu\nu})

(4.124)

It is claimed that the variation of the interaction Lagrangian with respect to
D_{10}
, followed by the conditions
V=1
and
A_{0}=0=D_{0\nu}
needed for reduction to the coadjoint orbits, yields the expected contribution
\text{Tr}\,(AE^{\prime})
(which is the field lift of the term
\text{Tr}\,(A\Lambda^{\prime})
in the isotropy equation) where
A=A_{1}
and
E=F^{01}=\pi^{1}
. It is also claimed that the variation with respect to
A_{0}
yields the expected contribution
(XA)^{\prime}
(which is the field lift of the term
\xi A^{\prime}+\xi^{\prime}A
in the isotropy equation).

Let us investigate the arguments provided above in 2D Minkowski spacetime with the metric convention
(+t,-x)
.
In 2D the only nonvanishing momentum component is
\pi^{01}=-\pi^{10}
as the momentum
\pi^{00}
for
A_{0}
vanishes by the definition (
4.117
). So the BLRY interaction term (
4.121
) reduces to

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{int}}=2\,\text{Tr}\,\Big(\pi^{01}(\tilde{\delta}_{D}A)_{[01]}\Big)

(4.125)

We introduce

\displaystyle A\equiv A_{1}\ \ ,\ \ A_{0}\equiv M\ \ ,\ \ D_{11}\equiv D\ \ ,\ \ D_{01}\equiv N\ \ ,\ \ D_{00}\equiv\varphi

(4.126)

Note that
A
and
M
are Lie algebra valued i.e.
A=A^{a}T^{a}
,
M=M^{a}T^{a}
in a basis
\{T^{a}\}
for the Lie algebra with the convention
\text{Tr}\,(T^{a}T^{b})=\delta^{ab}
.
We will also denote time derivative with a dot and space derivative with a prime. In this notation, the momentum reads

\displaystyle\pi^{01}=F^{01}

\displaystyle=\partial^{0}A^{1}-\partial^{1}A^{0}+[A^{0},A^{1}]

\displaystyle=-\dot{A}+M^{\prime}-[M,A]

(4.127)

Similarly the BLRY diff variation reads

\displaystyle(\tilde{\delta}_{D}A)_{[01]}

\displaystyle=\varphi\dot{A}-2(NA)^{\prime}-2\partial_{t}(NM)+DM^{\prime}

\displaystyle+2\varphi^{\prime}M+\varphi M^{\prime}+2\dot{D}A+D\dot{A}

(4.128)

and the unmodified diff variation reads

\displaystyle(\delta_{D}A)_{[01]}=\varphi\dot{A}-(NA)^{\prime}-\partial_{t}(NM)+DM^{\prime}+\varphi^{\prime}M+\dot{D}A

(4.129)

So it is true for both the BLRY and the unmodified diff variations that
A_{0}=M
do not receive momentum contributions when
D_{10}=N
(and its time derivative) vanishes.

Denoting
E\equiv\pi_{01}=-\pi^{01}
the variation of the BLRY interaction with respect to
N=D_{01}
yields

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\tilde{S}_{\text{int}}}{\delta N}=-2\,\text{Tr}\,(AE^{\prime}+M\dot{E})=-2\,\text{Tr}\,(A^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}E)

(4.130)

So in the temporal gauge
A_{0}=0
this expression yields the desired contribution up to a factor of
-2
. The same contribution follows from the unmodified interaction term (
4.118
) up to a factor of
-1
i.e.

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S_{\text{int}}}{\delta N}=-\text{Tr}\,(AE^{\prime}+M\dot{E})=-\,\text{Tr}\,(A^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}E)

(4.131)

The variation with respect to
M=A_{0}
evaluated at
D_{0\mu}=0=A_{0}
yields

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\tilde{S}_{\text{int}}}{\delta M}\bigg|_{D_{0\mu}=0=A_{0}}=-2(\dot{D}A)^{\prime}+[A,\dot{A}]D

(4.132)

The diff momentum evaluated at the background values
D_{0\mu}=0
becomes
X=\dot{D}
for both BLRY and full diff theories. Also for the temporal gauge
A_{0}=0
, YM momentum reduces to
E=\dot{A}
. So (
4.132
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S_{\text{int}}}{\delta M}\bigg|_{D_{0\mu}=0=A_{0}}=-2(XA)^{\prime}+[A,E]D

(4.133)

So at this step, our calculation yields an inconsistent result with the claim in
[
BLR00
]
due to the presence of the last term.

The unmodified interaction yields the contribution

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S_{\text{int}}}{\delta M}\bigg|_{D_{0\mu}=0=A_{0}}=-(XA)^{\prime}+(DE)^{\prime}

(4.134)

so it also contains an undesired term
-(DE)^{\prime}
.

Finally, the shift (
4.123
) yields a gauge invariant interaction only if the diff field is treated as a gauge invariant object, as the shift
V^{-1}\partial_{\mu}V
is nullifying the gauge transformation of
A_{\mu}
. Since, in our interpretation the diff field has a nontrivial gauge transformation

\displaystyle\delta_{\text{gauge}}=-\text{Tr}\,(AE^{\prime})

(4.135)

the proposed interaction Lagrangian (
4.121
) is not gauge invariant in our conventions. However, we have shown in the previous section that one can introduce the gauge-invariant combination (
4.105
)

\displaystyle\tilde{D}_{\mu\nu}\equiv D_{\mu\nu}+(e/2)\,\text{Tr}\,(A_{\mu}A_{\nu})

(4.136)

built from the diff and gauge fields. So, we need to simultaneously shift the gauge field
A\rightarrow\tilde{A}
as in (
4.123
) and the diff field
D\rightarrow\tilde{D}
as in (
4.136
).

This, however, does affect the variations with respect to
M
performed above. So the problem at hand is more complicated than it looks. Let us state the results for the unmodified interaction (
4.119
) upon the shift (
4.136
). The result (
4.131
) of the variation with respect to
D_{01}=N
does not change, which is good. The variation with respect to
A_{0}=M
, (
4.134
) changes to

\displaystyle-(\tilde{X}A)^{\prime}+(\tilde{D}E)^{\prime}-(1/2)A\,\text{Tr}\,(AE^{\prime})

(4.137)

where (
e
is set to
1
)

\displaystyle\tilde{D}

\displaystyle=D+(1/2)\,\text{Tr}\,(AA)

(4.138)

\displaystyle\tilde{X}

\displaystyle=\dot{D}+\text{Tr}\,(A\dot{A})=X+\text{Tr}\,(AE)

(4.139)

where the second equation holds in the temporal gauge
D_{\mu 0}=0=A_{0}
. So the case is unsolved. This suggests that the interaction prescription (
4.80
) may not applicable to the gauge field.
Otherwise,
the interaction prescription (
4.80
) and the gauge-invariant extension (
4.105
) should be inspected carefully, especially the last steps, namely the covariantization. We will not continue to this analysis.

4.4.6
Cocyle Motivated Spinor Interaction

In this section we are going to introduce an alternative diff-spinor interaction. For this purpose we will use the calculation in Section
2.4.6
as a guide.
Consider the following third-order covariant differential expression

\displaystyle I_{\mu\nu}=(\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}+kS_{\mu\nu})\nabla_{\rho}\xi^{\rho}

(4.140)

where
\xi^{\rho}
is a contravariant vector field,
S_{\mu\nu}
is an arbitrary rank-two tensor,
k
is a constant and
\nabla_{\mu}
is the Levi-Civita connection.
Expansion of the covariant derivatives in terms of the ordinary derivatives and connection coefficients yields

\displaystyle I_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\xi^{\rho}+(\Gamma^{\rho}_{\rho\lambda}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\xi^{\lambda}-\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\lambda}\partial_{\rho}\xi^{\rho})

\displaystyle+(\partial_{\nu}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\rho\lambda}\partial_{\mu}\xi^{\lambda}+\partial_{\mu}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\rho\lambda}\partial_{\nu}\xi^{\lambda}-\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\lambda}\xi^{\sigma}+kS_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\rho}\xi^{\rho})

\displaystyle+(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\rho\lambda}\xi^{\lambda}-\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\lambda}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\rho\sigma}\xi^{\sigma}+kS_{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\rho\lambda}\xi^{\lambda})

(4.141)

where the terms are ordered as
\xi^{\prime\prime\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime},\xi^{\prime},\xi
.
In 1D, this reduces to

\displaystyle I_{\text{1D}}=\xi^{\prime\prime\prime}+(2\Sigma+kS)\xi^{\prime}+(\Sigma^{\prime}+kS\Gamma)\xi

(4.142)

where we defined

\displaystyle\Sigma\equiv\Gamma^{\prime}-\Gamma^{2}/2

(4.143)

We showed in Section
2.4.4
that
\Sigma
Lie-transforms as a Virasoro coadjoint element with central charge one. We also showed that adding a rank-two tensor to a coadjoint element yields another coadjoint element with the same central charge, so the combination
kD\equiv 2\Sigma+kS
is a coadjoint element with central charge two. Then

\displaystyle D=(2/k)\Sigma+S

(4.144)

is a Virasoro coadjoint element with central charge
2/k
.

The last term in (
4.142
) is problematic since it does not transform in any good way. In the calculation of the cocyle in Section
2.4.6
, however, this term was not present since
I_{\mu\nu}
is just part of the cocyle (
2.122
); we have to contract it with
\eta^{\mu}dx^{\nu}
and antisymmetrize the expression in
\eta
and
\xi
. Namely,

\displaystyle J=\int dx^{\mu}\,\eta^{\nu}(\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}+kS_{\mu\nu})\nabla_{\rho}\xi^{\rho}-\int dx^{\mu}\,\xi^{\nu}(\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}+kS_{\mu\nu})\nabla_{\rho}\eta^{\rho}

(4.145)

Indeed the 1D reduction of the cocyle
J
has no
\xi\eta
term

\displaystyle J_{\text{1D}}

\displaystyle=\int dx\,\Big[(\eta\xi^{\prime\prime\prime}-\xi\eta^{\prime\prime\prime})+(2\Lambda+kS)(\eta\xi^{\prime}-\xi\eta^{\prime})\Big]

\displaystyle=\int dx\,\Big[(\eta\xi^{\prime\prime\prime}-\xi\eta^{\prime\prime\prime})+kD(\eta\xi^{\prime}-\xi\eta^{\prime})\Big]

(4.146)

Now, if we can form a GCT-vector out of the spinor field then we can use the analysis above to obtain a diff-spinor interaction. For this purpose, consider the spacetime Dirac matrix

\displaystyle\gamma^{\mu}(x)=e^{\mu}_{a}(x)\gamma^{a}

(4.147)

so it does transform as a vector under GCT. Then the field

\displaystyle\varphi^{\mu}(x)=\gamma^{\mu}(x)\psi(x)

(4.148)

also transforms as a vector under GCT.
Under a Lorentz transformation
e^{\mu}_{a}\rightarrow e^{\mu}_{b}\Lambda^{b}_{\ a}
of the frame fields we have the following

\displaystyle\psi\rightarrow\rho\psi\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \gamma^{\mu}\rightarrow\rho\gamma^{\mu}\rho^{-1}

(4.149)

where
\rho\equiv\rho(\Lambda)
is the Lorentz transformation matrix for the spinor representation (see e.g.
[
weldon
]
). Then
\varphi^{\mu}
transforms as

\displaystyle\varphi^{\mu}\equiv\gamma^{\mu}\psi\rightarrow\rho\gamma^{\mu}\rho^{-1}\rho\psi=\rho\gamma^{\mu}\psi=\rho\varphi^{\mu}

(4.150)

So,
\varphi^{\mu}
is also a Lorentz spinor.
Similarly we have

\displaystyle\overline{\varphi}^{\mu}\equiv\overline{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\rightarrow\overline{\psi}\rho^{-1}\rho\gamma^{\mu}\rho^{-1}=\overline{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\rho^{-1}=\overline{\varphi}^{\mu}\rho^{-1}

(4.151)

This also shows that objects of the form
\overline{\varphi}^{\mu}\varphi^{\nu}
are local Lorentz invariant.

Now we are ready to introduce the interaction,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\overline{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}(\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}+S_{\mu\nu})\nabla_{\rho}\varphi^{\rho}

(4.152)

Consider just the differential expression

\displaystyle I_{\mu\nu}=(\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}+S_{\mu\nu})\nabla_{\rho}\varphi^{\rho}

(4.153)

It is expanded as

\displaystyle I_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\varphi^{\rho}+(\Gamma^{\rho}_{\rho\lambda}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\varphi^{\lambda}-\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\lambda}\partial_{\rho}\varphi^{\rho})

\displaystyle+(\partial_{\nu}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\rho\lambda}\partial_{\mu}\varphi^{\lambda}+\partial_{\mu}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\rho\lambda}\partial_{\nu}\varphi^{\lambda}-\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\lambda}\varphi^{\sigma}+kS_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\rho}\varphi^{\rho})

\displaystyle+(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\rho\lambda}\varphi^{\lambda}-\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\lambda}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\rho\sigma}\varphi^{\sigma}+kS_{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\rho\lambda}\varphi^{\lambda})

(4.154)

So, in 1D it reduces to

\displaystyle I_{\text{1D}}

\displaystyle=\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}+(2\Sigma+kS)\varphi^{\prime}+(\Sigma^{\prime}+kS\Gamma)\varphi

(4.155)

We can write the interaction Lagrangian (
4.152
) in a more symmetric way as follows. The hermitian conjugate of
\varphi^{\mu}
becomes

\displaystyle(\varphi^{\mu})^{\dagger}=\psi^{\dagger}(\gamma^{\mu})^{\dagger}=\psi^{\dagger}\gamma^{0}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{0}=\overline{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{0}

(4.156)

which implies

\displaystyle\overline{\varphi}^{\mu}:=(\varphi^{\mu})^{\dagger}\gamma^{0}=\overline{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}

(4.157)

Thus (
4.152
) can be simplifed to (with the added symmetrization condition)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\overline{\varphi}^{(\mu}\gamma^{\nu)}(\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}+S_{\mu\nu})\nabla_{\lambda}\varphi^{\lambda}

(4.158)

The field
\varphi^{\mu}
satisfies the following

\displaystyle\overline{\varphi}^{\mu}\varphi^{\nu}=g^{\mu\nu}\overline{\psi}\psi+2e^{\mu}_{a}e^{\nu}_{b}\overline{\psi}\sigma^{ab}\psi

(4.159)

which implies

\displaystyle\overline{\varphi}^{\mu}g_{\mu\nu}\varphi^{\nu}=n\overline{\psi}\psi

(4.160)

where
n
is the spacetime dimension. Since the diff field
D_{\mu\nu}
is symmetric we also have

\displaystyle\overline{\varphi}^{\mu}D_{\mu\nu}\varphi^{\nu}=\overline{\psi}\mathcal{D}\psi

(4.161)

where
\mathcal{D}=g_{\mu\nu}D^{\mu\nu}
.

What would be the analog of (
4.145
) in order to get rid of the unwanted last term in (
4.155
)? One may employ a modification used in 2D covariant Dirac theory for showing the vanishing of the spin connection (see e.g.
[
nakahara
]
, Section 7.10.3), namely, hermitianizing the action. For this purpose, we may simply define the interaction Lagrangian as
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}=\mathcal{L}+\mathcal{L}^{*}
.

4.5
Supersymmetric Extension

This section is based on the analysis in
[
delius90
]
and
[
GR01
]
. Also
[
friedan1986conformal
]
is used as a main reference. The supersymmetric expressions for the pieces of the superdiffeomorphism action are the same though the resulting action is modified in accordance with the modified momentum ansatz in Section
4.3
.

4.5.1
Superdiffeomorphism Field

The superVirasoro algebra contains (in the NS sector) the bosonic Virasoro generators
L_{m},m\in\mathbb{Z}
, the fermionic generators
G_{\mu},\mu\in\mathbb{Z}+1/2
, and the center generator
I
. The commutation relations are given by

\displaystyle[L_{m},L_{n}]

\displaystyle=(m-n)L_{m+n}+\frac{1}{8}\hat{c}(m^{3}-m)\delta_{m+n}I

\displaystyle[L_{m},G_{\mu}]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}m-\mu\right)G_{m+\mu}

\displaystyle\{G_{\mu},G_{\nu}\}

\displaystyle=-4L_{\mu+\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\hat{c}\left(\mu^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\right)\delta_{\mu+\nu}I

(4.162)

A generic algebra (or adjoint) element takes the form

\displaystyle\hat{A}=A^{m}L_{m}+A^{\mu}G_{\mu}+\frac{1}{8}a\hat{c}I

(4.163)

We can introduce a superfield
A(z,\theta)
corresponding to the non-central part of
\hat{A}
as

\displaystyle A(z,\theta)=A^{m}z^{m+1}+2\theta A^{\mu}z^{\mu+1/2}\ \ \ \leftrightarrow\ \ \ A^{m}L_{m}+A^{\mu}G_{\mu}

(4.164)

where
\theta
is the Grassmann variable.
Then the complete generic element
\hat{A}
corresponds to the doublet

\displaystyle\hat{A}\ \leftrightarrow\ (A(z,\theta),a)

(4.165)

We also introduce fields corresponding to the generators
L_{n}
and
G_{\mu}
as

\displaystyle L(z)=z^{-n-2}L_{n}\hskip 14.45377pt\text{and}\hskip 14.45377ptG(z)=z^{-\mu-3/2}G_{\mu}

(4.166)

We can combine these into a superfield

\displaystyle T(z,\theta)=\frac{1}{2}G(z)+\theta L(z)

(4.167)

With all these, the correspondence (
4.165
) solidifies by the equality

\displaystyle\oint\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\,d\theta\,A(z,\theta)\,T(z,\theta)+\frac{1}{8}a\hat{c}I=\hat{A}

(4.168)

For convenience let us abbreviate
(z,\theta)\equiv Z
so that we have
A(z,\theta)=A(Z)
and also

\displaystyle dZ\equiv\frac{dz}{2\pi i}d\theta

(4.169)

The commutator of two generic elements
(A(Z),a)
and
(B(Z),b)
becomes

\displaystyle[(A,a),(B,b)]=\left((\partial A)B-A\partial B-\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{D}A)(\mathcal{D}B)\ ,\ \oint dZ\ (\partial^{2}\mathcal{D}A)B\right)

(4.170)

where
\partial\equiv\partial_{z}
and we introduced the superderivative

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\equiv\partial_{\theta}+\theta\partial

(4.171)

The equality (
4.170
) can be directly verified by performing the
z
and
\theta
integrations. The definition (
4.171
) of the superderivative implies
\mathcal{D}^{2}=\partial
.

Elements of superDiff(
S^{1}
) are diffeomorphisms
(z,\theta)\mapsto(\tilde{z}(z,\theta),\tilde{\theta}(z,\theta))
such that the supersymmetric line element scales by a superfield

\displaystyle dz+\theta d\theta\mapsto d\tilde{z}+\tilde{\theta}d\tilde{\theta}=\phi(z,\theta)(dz+\theta d\theta)

(4.172)

This is the generalization of the case of Diff(
S^{1}
) where
dz\mapsto d\tilde{z}=f(z)dz
. Necessary and sufficient condition for (
4.172
) is

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\tilde{z}-\tilde{\theta}\mathcal{D}\tilde{\theta}=0

(4.173)

This condition implies that the superderivative
\mathcal{D}
transforms as

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{D}}=(\mathcal{D}\tilde{\theta})^{-1}\mathcal{D}

(4.174)

and
dzd\theta
transforms as

\displaystyle d\tilde{z}d\tilde{\theta}=(\mathcal{D}\tilde{\theta})dzd\theta

(4.175)

One can define an
h
-differential
A
as

\displaystyle A=A(z,\theta)(dzd\theta)^{2h}

(4.176)

This induces the transformation property of the superfield
A
as

\displaystyle\tilde{A}(\tilde{z},\tilde{\theta})=A(z,\theta)(\mathcal{D}\tilde{\theta})^{-2h}

(4.177)

An infinitesimal transformation generated by an adjoint vector
F
becomes

\displaystyle\delta_{F}A=-F\partial A-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}F\mathcal{D}A-h(\partial F)A

(4.178)

Comparison with (
4.170
) suggests that adjoint elements transform as
-1
differentials.

The adjoint representation of the centrally extended group is given by

\displaystyle(A(Z),a)\overset{g}{\mapsto}\left(A_{g}(Z),a+2\oint dZS(Z,\tilde{Z})A(Z)\right)

(4.179)

where

\displaystyle A_{g}(\tilde{Z})=A(Z)(\mathcal{D}\tilde{\theta}(Z))^{2}

(4.180)

and the superSchwarzian
S(Z,\tilde{Z})
is given by

\displaystyle S(Z,\tilde{Z})=\frac{\mathcal{D}^{4}\tilde{\theta}}{\mathcal{D}\tilde{\theta}}-2\frac{\mathcal{D}^{3}\tilde{\theta}\ \mathcal{D}^{2}\tilde{\theta}}{(\mathcal{D}\tilde{\theta})^{2}}

(4.181)

An infinitesimal transformation
g
generated by an adjoint vector
F
becomes

\displaystyle\delta_{F}A

\displaystyle=-F\mathcal{D}^{2}A-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}F\mathcal{D}A+(\mathcal{D}^{2}F)A

\displaystyle=-F\partial A-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}F\mathcal{D}A+(\partial F)A

(4.182)

Thus the finite adjoint action agrees with the infinitesimal adjoint transformation given in (
4.170
). It is also straightforward to show the representation property

\displaystyle(A_{g},a_{g})\overset{h}{\mapsto}((A_{g})_{h},(a_{g})_{h})=(A_{gh},a_{gh})

(4.183)

Coadjoint vectors
(B^{*},b^{*})
can be introduced via the following pairing

\displaystyle\left<(B^{*},b^{*})|(A,a)\right>\equiv b^{*}a+\oint dZ\ B^{*}(Z)A(Z)

(4.184)

and the coadjoint transformation is defined by requiring the group invariance of the pairing

\displaystyle\left<(B^{*}_{g},b^{*}_{g})|(A_{g},a_{g})\right>\overset{!}{=}\left<(B^{*},b^{*})|(A,a)\right>

(4.185)

Using (
4.179
), (
4.180
) and (
4.175
) we get

\displaystyle(B^{*}(Z),b^{*})\overset{g}{\mapsto}(B^{*}_{g}(Z),b^{*}_{g})

(4.186)

where

\displaystyle B^{*}_{g}(\tilde{Z})=\Big(B^{*}(Z)-2b^{*}S(Z,\tilde{Z})\Big)(\mathcal{D}\tilde{\theta})^{-3}\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ b^{*}_{g}=b^{*}

(4.187)

Comparing this with (
4.177
) we see that for
b^{*}=0
,
B^{*}
transforms as a
3/2
-differential.

Performing an infinitesimal transformation generated by
F
we get

\displaystyle\delta_{F}B^{*}

\displaystyle=-F\mathcal{D}^{2}B^{*}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}F\mathcal{D}B^{*}-\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{D}^{2}FB^{*}-b^{*}\mathcal{D}^{5}F

\displaystyle=-F\partial B^{*}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}F\mathcal{D}B^{*}-\frac{3}{2}\partial FB^{*}-b^{*}\mathcal{D}\partial^{2}F

(4.188)

Comparing this with the infinitesimal transformation (
4.178
) we again see that for
b^{*}=0
,
B^{*}
transforms as a
3/2
-differential. If we introduce the decomposition
F(z,\theta)=\xi(z)+\theta\epsilon(z)
with bosonic
\xi
, Grassmann
\epsilon
, and
B^{*}(z,\theta)=u(z)+\theta D(z)
with bosonic
D
and Grassmann
u
, the infinitesimal transformation reduces to
5
5

5

The second equation here, is different from the equations in
[
GR01
]
and
[
delius90
]
. In each source the second equation was

\displaystyle\delta_{F}D

\displaystyle=-\xi\partial D-2\partial\xi D-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon\partial u-\frac{3}{2}\partial\epsilon u+b^{*}\partial^{3}\xi

(4.189)

\displaystyle\delta_{F}u

\displaystyle=-\xi\partial u-\frac{3}{2}\partial\xi u-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon D+b^{*}\partial^{2}\epsilon

\displaystyle\delta_{F}D

\displaystyle=-\xi\partial D-2\partial\xi D-\frac{3}{2}\partial(\epsilon u)+b^{*}\partial^{3}\xi

(4.190)

Note that reduction of the second equation to the Virasoro algebra (i.e. turning off supersymmetry) is exactly the coadjoint transformation of the diff field as desired. Also we introduced
D
as the component multiplying
\theta
so as to keep it bosonic.

4.5.2
2D Majorana Action

To extend the formalism developed so far to a field theory we lift the Grassmann variable
\theta
to a 2D Majorana spinor
\theta^{\alpha}
and the supersymmetric derivative to the operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mu}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{\mu}}-\frac{i}{2}\gamma^{N}_{\nu\mu}\theta^{\nu}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{N}}\equiv\partial_{\mu}-\frac{i}{2}\gamma^{N}_{\nu\mu}\theta^{\nu}\partial_{N}

(4.191)

One should be careful in calculations since Greek letters refer to the spinor indices not to the spacetime indices; capital Latin letters refer to the spacetime indices.
\mathcal{D}_{\mu}
satisfies

\displaystyle\{\mathcal{D}_{\mu},\mathcal{D}_{\nu}\}=-i\gamma^{M}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{M}

(4.192)

where we used
\{\partial_{\mu},\theta^{\nu}\}=\delta^{\nu}_{\mu}=\{\theta^{\nu},\partial_{\mu}\}
.

The Dirac-Gamma matrices satisfy

\displaystyle\{\gamma^{A},\gamma^{B}\}=2\eta^{AB}

(4.193)

We also introduce

\displaystyle[\gamma^{A},\gamma^{B}]=2\Sigma^{AB}

(4.194)

Combining these equations we get

\displaystyle\gamma^{A}\gamma^{B}=\eta^{AB}+\Sigma^{AB}

(4.195)

or, explicitly in spinor components

\displaystyle\gamma^{A}_{\alpha\beta}\gamma^{B\beta\lambda}=\delta^{\lambda}_{\alpha}\eta^{AB}+\Sigma_{\alpha}^{AB\lambda}

(4.196)

An adjoint element
F
is promoted to a vector superfield
F^{M}

\displaystyle F\rightarrow F^{M}=\xi^{M}+\theta^{\alpha}\gamma^{M}_{\alpha\beta}\epsilon^{\beta}

(4.197)

and a coadjoint element
B^{*}
is promoted to a spin 3/2 superfield
B_{\mu M}

\displaystyle B^{*}\rightarrow B_{\mu M}=\Upsilon_{\mu M}+D_{MN}\theta^{\alpha}\gamma^{N}_{\alpha\beta}+\theta^{\alpha}\theta^{\beta}\gamma^{N}_{\mu[\alpha}A_{\beta]MN}

(4.198)

To deduce the extension of the coadjoint action (
4.5.1
) to higher dimensional case, it is instructive to recall how this was done in the Virasoro case. Ignoring the central extension the quadratic differential transformation

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}D=\xi\partial D+2\partial\xi D

(4.199)

was lifted to the Lie derivative of a rank-two tensor as

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}D_{MN}=\xi^{A}\partial_{A}D_{MN}+\partial_{M}\xi^{A}D_{AN}+\partial_{N}\xi^{A}D_{MA}

(4.200)

Hence, upon lifting the term with coefficient two (the term specifying
D
as a quadratic differential) was split into two terms, one for each spacetime index of a rank two tensor. Applying the same rule for the tensoral part of (
4.5.1
) we deduce

\displaystyle\delta^{\text{tens}}_{F}B_{\mu M}=F^{N}\partial_{N}B_{\mu M}+(\partial_{M}F^{N})B_{\mu N}+\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{N}F^{N})B_{\mu M}

(4.201)

The first two terms are what we expect from a field with one lower spacetime index (covariant vector) and the last one is a correction telling us that
B_{\mu M}
carries a density of weight 1/2.
Now keeping track of the indices we can fully extend (
4.5.1
)

\displaystyle\delta_{F}B_{\mu M}

\displaystyle=F^{N}\partial_{N}B_{\mu M}+(\partial_{M}F^{N})B_{\mu N}+\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{N}F^{N})B_{\mu M}

\displaystyle\ \ +i(\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}F^{N})\gamma^{\lambda\nu}_{N}(\mathcal{D}_{\nu}B_{\mu M})+q\ \mathcal{D}_{\mu}\partial_{N}\partial_{M}F^{N}

(4.202)

At this point let us recall the general structure of the diff field Lagrangian. The diff field Lagrangian is of the form

\displaystyle 2L_{\text{diff}}=X^{LMR}(\nabla_{R}D_{LM}+Y_{LMR})

(4.203)

where
Y_{LMR}
is the covariantized and centrally extended Lie derivative of the diff field ’with respect to itself’ in analogy with equation (
4.69
). Just as in the non-super case, it turns out that
\nabla_{R}D_{LM}+Y_{LMR}=X_{LMR}
thus we get

\displaystyle 2L_{\text{diff}}=X^{LMR}X_{LMR}

(4.204)

Hence, if we get the super extension of
X_{LMR}
we get the super extension of the diff Lagrangian. For this purpose we first need to find the analog of
D_{0}^{\ N}
, the Lagrange multiplier of the diff-Gauss law. Consider the following superfield

\displaystyle F_{A}^{\ N}=E^{1/2}\gamma^{\alpha\beta}_{A}\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}B_{\beta}^{N}

(4.205)

where
E
is the superdeterminant and
the superderivative
\mathcal{D}_{\mu}
is covariantized

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}-\frac{i}{2}\gamma^{N}_{\nu\mu}\theta^{\nu}\nabla_{N}

(4.206)

A straightforward calculation shows

\displaystyle E^{-1/2}F^{\ N}_{0}=D_{0}^{N}

\displaystyle-i\theta^{\beta}\nabla_{0}\Upsilon^{N}_{\beta}-i(\Sigma_{0}^{\ N})^{\beta}_{\nu}\theta^{\nu}\nabla_{M}\Upsilon^{N}_{\beta}

\displaystyle\ -i\gamma^{M}_{\lambda\nu}\theta^{\nu}\theta^{\lambda}\nabla_{0}D_{M}^{\ N}-i(\Sigma_{0}^{\ L})^{\beta}_{\nu}\gamma^{M}_{\lambda\beta}\theta^{\nu}\theta^{\lambda}\nabla_{L}D_{M}^{\ N}

(4.207)

so that the leading order term matches up with the Virasoro case.

With
F^{N}_{A}
found, the rest is straightfoward. We introduce the analog of the covariantized Lie derivative of diff field as the superfield
Y_{A\mu M}

\displaystyle Y_{A\mu M}

\displaystyle=F^{N}_{A}\nabla_{N}B_{\mu M}+\nabla_{M}F^{N}_{A}B_{\mu N}+\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{N}F^{N}_{A})B_{\mu M}

\displaystyle+i(\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}F^{N}_{A})\gamma^{\lambda\nu}_{N}(\mathcal{D}_{\nu}B_{\mu M})+q\ \mathcal{D}_{\mu}\nabla_{N}\nabla_{M}F^{N}_{A}

(4.208)

Then the superfield corresponding to the diff momentum becomes

\displaystyle X_{A\mu M}=\nabla_{A}B_{\mu M}+Y_{A\mu M}

(4.209)

Finally the superdiff action reads

\displaystyle S=-\frac{1}{2}\int d^{2}x\,d\theta^{\mu}\,d\theta^{\nu}\,X_{A\mu M}\,X_{B\nu N}\,\eta^{AB}\,\eta^{MN}

(4.210)

Just as in the ordinary case, the previously found superdiff action
[
GR01
]

corresponds to the symplectic part of this action :

\displaystyle S_{\text{superBLRY}}=-\frac{1}{2}\int d^{2}x\,d\theta^{\mu}\,d\theta{\nu}\,X_{A\mu M}\,\nabla_{A}B_{\mu M}\,\eta^{AB}\,\eta^{MN}

(4.211)

It is straightforward to modify the remaining fermion actions discussed in
[
GR01
]
, namely, when the Grassman variable
\theta
is lifted to a 3D Majorana spinor or a 2D, 4D chiral spinor. We shall not continue this analysis.

Chapter 5
DIFFEOMORPHISM FIELD IN 2D MINKOWSKI SPACETIME

5.1
Introduction

Before analyzing the transverse action introduced in the previous section in 2D Minkowski spacetime we will first go over a slightly different approach held in order to obtain the diff field theory, that predates the transverse method, and can be said to be the origin of it.

In search of a covariant theory for the diff field, in
[
LR95
]
authors applied the methods of
[
rajeev88
]
. This is different from the transverse action in that the diff-Gauss law constraint is implicitly introduced, i.e. it is introduced as a constraint generating an equivalence relation on the phase space, not explicitly as a term in the Lagrangian or the Hamiltonian. We shall call this field theory the DX theory. Applying the methods of
[
rajeev88
]
they also obtained a reduction of the field theory to a finite-dimensional theory.

Here, we will first review the paper
[
rajeev88
]
. Then we will go over DX theory
[
LR95
]
and correct a mistake (Equation (4.10) of
[
LR95
]
) changing some of the results in the subsequent analysis. However, application of Dirac’s constraint Hamiltonian formalism shows that even with this correction the theory is invalid. This will be shown in the next chapter.

We will, then, go back to the analysis of the transverse action in 2D. There are two cases to consider, before or after the covariantization step. The former leads to the DXN theory, to be studied in the next chapter, and the latter leads to a complicated higher order theory, even in the 2D Minkowski spacetime. We will introduce a new gauge-fixing condition for the diff field components called the chiral gauge in which every aspect of the covariantized theory simplifies. Moreover, in this gauge the theory is not constrained.

5.2
Finite Reduction of YM Theory on a Cylinder

Rajeev
[
rajeev88
]
discusses solving YM theory on a cylinder in the Hamiltonian formalism, without using a gauge-fixing condition, reducing the field theory to a finite-dimensional theory and quantizing it. Let us note the main steps.

The curvature tensor and the YM equation are given in covariant form by

\displaystyle F_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}+[A_{\mu},A_{\nu}]

(5.1)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\partial^{\mu}F_{\mu\nu}+[A^{\mu},F_{\mu\nu}]

(5.2)

Introducing
E_{1}\equiv F_{01}
, the YM equation yields

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}E_{1}+[A_{1},E_{1}]

(5.3)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}E_{1}+[A_{0},E_{1}]

(5.4)

A_{0}
is nondynamical (its momentum
F^{00}
identically vanishes) and can be eliminated from the equations by introducing a variable
T(t,x)
valued in
G
as the solution to the equation

\partial_{t}T=TA_{0}

with the boundary condition

T(t=0)=1
.
This results in new variables
A,E
given by

\displaystyle A=TA_{1}T^{-1}+T\partial_{x}T^{-1}\ \ \ ,\ \ \ E=TE_{1}T^{-1}

(5.5)

Inverting these for
A_{1},E_{1}

and inserting back into (
5.1
), (
5.3
) and (
5.4
) one gets

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}A

(5.6a)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}E

(5.6b)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}E+[A,E]

(5.6c)

Thus
A_{0}
is eliminated and the new theory involves only
A
and
E
.

The equations (
5.6
) follow from a canonical formalism where the configuration space is the space of functions

Q=\{A:S^{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}\}

and
E
is canonically conjugate to
A
. The unconstrained phase space is
\Gamma=Q\oplus Q
consisting of all
(A,E)
and the Hamiltonian yielding the first two equations is

\displaystyle H=\frac{1}{2}\int\left<E,E\right>\ dx

(5.7)

where
\left<\ ,\ \right>
is a bilinear form on
\Gamma
which can be taken as the Killing form (practically the trace) on
\mathcal{G}
. The third equation will be a first-class constraint. It can be introduced by defining the true phase space,
\tilde{\Gamma}
, to be the space of pairs
(A,E)
satisfying this constraint. Then one can show that
\tilde{\Gamma}
can be defined as the quotient of
\Gamma
with respect to the equivalence relation (the gauge equivalence)

\displaystyle(A,E)\sim(gAg^{-1}+g\partial_{x}g^{-1},gEg^{-1})\equiv(A^{g},E^{g})

(5.8)

The Hamiltonian is gauge-invariant,

H(A,E)=H(A^{g}.E^{g})
,
so that
H
is well-defined on
\tilde{\Gamma}
.

The constraint (
5.6c
) can be formally solved introducing the Wilson line
S
satisfying

\displaystyle\partial_{x}S+AS=0

(5.9)

with the boundary condition

S(x=0)=1
.
Then

\displaystyle E(x)=S(x)E(0)S^{-1}(x)

(5.10)

solves the constraint. Now we define a map
\phi:\Gamma\rightarrow G\times\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\phi(A,E)=(S(2\pi),E(0))\equiv(q,p)

(5.11)

Here,
S(2\pi)
is the Wilson loop. Geometrically it is the parallel transport operator around a loop.
\phi
satisfies
(is said to be equivariant under gauge transformations)

\displaystyle\phi(A^{g},E^{g})=(g(0)\ q\ g(0)^{-1},g(0)\ p\ g(0)^{-1})

(5.12)

Therefore,
\phi
on
\Gamma
induces
\phi:\tilde{\Gamma}\rightarrow G\times\mathcal{G}/G_{\text{adj}}

i.e. the real phase space defined by the gauge constraint yields
G\times\mathcal{G}
up to the adjoint transformation given above. This map is a bijection.

The equations in the new variables
(q,p)
read

\displaystyle\dot{p}=0\ \ \ ,\ \ \ q^{-1}\dot{q}=-2\pi p

(5.13)

These equations follow from a canonical formalism with the canonical one-form,

\displaystyle\theta=-\text{Tr}\ (pq^{-1}dq)

(5.14)

and the Hamiltonian,

\displaystyle H=\pi\text{Tr}\ (pp)

(5.15)

on the space
G\times\mathcal{G}
.
Note that (
5.15
) is just the projection of the Hamiltonian (
5.7
) of the field theory. (
5.13
), (
5.14
) and (
5.15
) define a theory with a finite number of degrees. For quantization and the spectrum of this theory see
[
rajeev88
]
.

5.3
DX Gravity Theory and Its Finite Reduction

5.3.1
DX Field Theory

Let us recall the basics about the gravity theory proposed in
[
LR95
]
. The Virasoro coadjoint element
D
is lifted to a dynamical field, i.e.,
D(\theta)\rightarrow D(\theta,\tau)
. In analogy with
[
rajeev88
]
the authors proposed the action

\displaystyle S=\frac{1}{\lambda}\int d\sigma\ d\tau\ X\partial_{\tau}D-\frac{1}{2\lambda}\int d\sigma\ d\tau\ X^{2}

(5.16)

where
\lambda
is a parameter introduced for dimensional reasons and
X
is the momentum conjugate to
D
. Recall equations (
5.6
). In analogy
X
is taken as

\displaystyle X=\dot{D}

(5.17)

so that the action becomes

\displaystyle S=\frac{1}{2\lambda}\int d\sigma\ d\tau\ X^{2}=\frac{1}{2\lambda}\int d\sigma\ d\tau\ \dot{D}^{2}

(5.18)

where dot denotes
\tau
-derivative. Hamilton’s equations yield (
5.17
) and

\displaystyle\dot{X}=0

(5.19)

The Virasoro analog of the Gauss law (
5.6c
) is obtained as follows. Notice that (
5.6c
) is the pure KM isotropy equation
\Lambda^{\prime}-[\Lambda,A]=0
with
\Lambda\rightarrow E
. In other words the isotropy generator
\Lambda
lifts to the momentum
E
. This is not so by chance, but follows by
\delta A=\{A,Q\}
with
Q=\int\Lambda G
where
G
is the Gauss law operator. This ensures that the KM coadjoint transformation is a gauge transformation generated by the Gauss law. We already used this correspondence in Section
4.2
to obtain YM theory from KM algebra.

In analogy, we lift the isotropy equation (
2.91
) on the coadjoint orbits of Virasoro algebra to the constraint equation, the diff-Gauss law

\displaystyle 2X^{\prime}D+D^{\prime}X+cX^{\prime\prime\prime}=0

(5.20)

where prime denotes
\theta
-derivative and
c
is a constant. This equation ensures transversality of dynamics to the orbits, and turns the Virasoro coadjoint transformation into a local symmetry of the theory. This equation will be enforced on the phase space as in the previous section.

5.3.2
Finite Reduction

The Wilson line
v(\theta)
for the diff field is defined through

\displaystyle D(\theta)\equiv c\,S(\theta,v)=c\left(\frac{v^{\prime\prime\prime}}{v^{\prime}}-\frac{3}{2}\left[\frac{v^{\prime\prime}}{v^{\prime}}\right]^{2}\right)

(5.21)

so that the Wilson loop becomes
1
1

1

We investigate whether this is indeed the Wilson loop in Section
7.2.6
.

\displaystyle Q\equiv v(2\pi)

(5.22)

Plugging (
5.21
) in the diff-Gauss law (
5.20
) we get

\displaystyle X(\theta)=\frac{X(0)}{\partial v(\theta)}\equiv\frac{P}{v^{\prime}}

(5.23)

Taking a time derivative of (
5.21
) and using
X=\dot{D}
this yields

\displaystyle\frac{P}{c(v^{\prime})^{3}}=\partial_{v}^{3}(\dot{v})

(5.24)

where

\displaystyle\partial_{v}=\frac{1}{v^{\prime}}\partial_{\theta}

(5.25)

Equation (
5.24
) is solved by

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}v(\theta)=\frac{P}{c}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\theta}d\phi\ \frac{v^{2}(\phi)}{(\partial v(\phi))^{2}}\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \left.-v(\theta)\int_{0}^{\theta}d\phi\ \frac{v(\phi)}{(\partial v(\phi))^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}v^{2}(\theta)\int_{0}^{\theta}d\phi\ \frac{1}{(\partial v(\phi))^{2}}\right)

(5.26)

where
\tau
-dependence of
v
is suppressed. For simplification we define

\displaystyle f(\theta)\equiv\int_{0}^{\theta}d\phi\frac{v^{2}(\phi)}{2(\partial v(\phi))^{2}}\ ,\ g(\theta)\equiv\int_{0}^{\theta}d\phi\frac{v(\phi)}{(\partial v(\phi))^{2}}\ ,\ h(\theta)\equiv\int_{0}^{\theta}d\phi\frac{1}{2(\partial v(\phi))^{2}}

(5.27)

We also have

\displaystyle\dot{v}(2\pi)=\partial_{\tau}\Big(v(2\pi,\tau)\Big)=\partial_{\tau}\Big(Q(\tau)\Big)=\dot{Q}

(5.28)

Thus evaluating (
5.26
) at
\theta=2\pi
we get

\displaystyle\dot{Q}=\frac{P}{c}\Big(f(2\pi)-Qg(2\pi)+Q^{2}h(2\pi)\Big)

(5.29)

For orbits
2
2

2

We examine such orbits in Section
7.2.5
.
in which
D
is diffeomorphic to a constant
D_{0}
one can solve (
5.21
) :

\displaystyle v(\theta)=\exp\left(\pm i\alpha\theta\right)

(5.30)

where

\alpha\equiv\sqrt{2D_{0}/c}
.
If
f
,
g
,
h
are evaluated for the solution
v=\exp(i\alpha\theta)
one gets

\displaystyle\dot{Q}=\frac{2\pi^{3}P}{c(\ln Q)^{3}}\left(3-4Q+Q^{2}+2\ln Q\right)

(5.31)

The momentum equation
\dot{X}=0
reads

\displaystyle\dot{P}=\frac{P\dot{v}^{\prime}}{v^{\prime}}

(5.32)

Taking a
\tau
-derivative of (
5.29
) and evaluating at
\theta=2\pi
one can compute
\dot{v}^{\prime}(2\pi)
in terms of
Q
and
P
. Then equation (
5.32
) yields

\displaystyle\dot{P}=\frac{(4\pi)^{3}P^{2}(Q-1)^{2}}{c\,Q(\ln Q)^{3}}

(5.33)

This result differs from the one given in
[
LR95
]
:

\displaystyle\dot{P}=\frac{2\pi^{3}P^{2}}{Q(\ln Q)^{4}}(3-4Q+Q^{2}+2\ln Q)(\ln Q+1)

(5.34)

Therefore we diverge in the analysis in the rest of the paper (the Hamiltonian, the symplectic structure and the quantization of the system).

5.3.3
Underlying Symplectic Theory

We need to find a Hamiltonian
H
and a symplectic structure
\omega
which yield
\dot{Q}
and
\dot{P}
equations through the central equation

\displaystyle\omega(\zeta_{H},Y)=-dH(Y)

(5.35)

Here
\zeta_{H}
is the Hamiltonian vector field associated with
H
, and
Y
is an arbitrary vector field. Explicitly, if we denote the phase space coordinates as
z^{1}=P
and
z^{2}=Q
then we have
\zeta_{H}=\dot{z}=(\dot{P},\dot{Q})
. Denoting the single component of
\omega
with the same letter, equation (
5.35
) yields

\displaystyle\dot{P}=-\frac{1}{\omega}\frac{\partial H}{\partial Q}\hskip 21.68121pt\text{and}\hskip 21.68121pt\dot{Q}=\frac{1}{\omega}\frac{\partial H}{\partial P}

(5.36)

The Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H=\frac{\pi^{3}}{c}\frac{P^{2}}{(3+2\ln Q-4Q+Q^{2})^{2}}

(5.37)

and the symplectic two-form

\displaystyle\omega=\frac{(\ln Q)^{3}}{(3+2\ln Q-4Q+Q^{2})^{3}}

(5.38)

yield the equations (
5.31
) and (
5.33
) through (
5.36
). Also the field to particle projection argument noted after the equation (
5.15
) in the YM case seems to roughly hold in the
DX
theory:

\displaystyle H(X,D)\sim\int X^{2}\hskip 14.45377pt\text{with}\hskip 14.45377ptX\sim\frac{P}{G(Q)}\hskip 14.45377pt\rightarrow\hskip 14.45377ptH(P,Q)\sim\left(\frac{P}{F(Q)}\right)^{2}

5.3.4
Comparison of Symplectic Theories

Reconsider the Hamiltonian
H
given in (
5.37
) and the symplectic structure
\omega
given in (
5.38
).
Those proposed in
[
LR95
]
are given by

\displaystyle\tilde{H}=\ln P+\ln Q+\ln(\ln Q)

(5.39)

and

\displaystyle\tilde{\omega}=\frac{(\ln Q)^{3}}{2\pi^{3}P^{2}(3+2\ln Q-4Q+Q^{2})}

(5.40)

In this section we will compare these as functions.

First the easy part, the momentum
P
dependence.
H
is well-defined for any
P
. In particular, for all
P
, we have
H\geq 0
and
H=0
if and only if
P=0
.

On the other hand,
\tilde{H}
is not positive definite for all
P
. For
P>1
we have
\tilde{H}>0
, for
P=1
we have
\tilde{H}=0
and for
P<1
we have
\tilde{H}<0
. Also as
P\rightarrow 0
we have
\tilde{H}\rightarrow-\infty
. Furthermore
\tilde{\omega}
is singular at
P=0
, whereas
\omega
does not depend on
P
.

Now, the
Q
-dependence.
H
is well-defined for any
Q\geq 0
except at
Q=1
where
H\rightarrow+\infty
. In particular
H(0,P)=0
. Also
H\geq 0
for all
Q
and as
Q\rightarrow\infty
we have
H\rightarrow 0
.

\tilde{H}
is well-defined for only
Q>1
, i.e. it is not defined for
Q\leq 1
. It is
0
at
Q_{0}=1.4215299358831166^{\prime}
(numerical solver) and
\tilde{H}(Q<Q_{0})<0
and
\tilde{H}(Q>Q_{0})>0
. Also as
Q\rightarrow\infty
it diverges.

\omega
is well-defined for all
Q\geq 0
except at
Q=1
and
Q=0
However, it has a well defined limit at each of these points

\displaystyle\lim_{Q\rightarrow 0}\omega=\frac{1}{8}\hskip 21.68121pt\text{and}\hskip 21.68121pt\lim_{Q\rightarrow 1}\omega=1

(5.41)

\tilde{\omega}
is well-defined everywhere except at the same points
Q=0
and
Q=1
. However, it has a limit only at
Q=1
:

\displaystyle\lim_{Q\rightarrow 0}\tilde{\omega}=+\infty\hskip 21.68121pt\text{and}\hskip 21.68121pt\lim_{Q\rightarrow 1}\tilde{\omega}=0

(5.42)

Therefore, it seems that in both
Q
and
P
dependence
H
and
\omega
behave in a much nicer way than
\tilde{H}
and
\tilde{\omega}
.

5.3.5
Quantization

For convenience let us rewrite the basic ingredients. We had the equations

\displaystyle\dot{Q}=\frac{2\pi^{3}}{c}\frac{PZ(Q)}{(\ln Q)^{3}}\hskip 21.68121pt\text{and}\hskip 21.68121pt\dot{P}=\frac{4\pi^{3}}{c}\frac{P^{2}(Q-1)^{2}}{Q(\ln Q)^{3}}

(5.43)

where

\displaystyle Z(Q)\equiv 3-4Q+Q^{2}+2\ln Q

(5.44)

These equations follow from

\displaystyle H=\frac{\pi^{3}}{c}\frac{P^{2}}{Z(Q)^{2}}\hskip 21.68121pt\text{and}\hskip 21.68121pt\omega=\frac{(\ln Q)^{3}}{Z(Q)^{3}}

(5.45)

The Poisson bracket (PB)
\{\ ,\ \}
is given by
\omega^{-1}
and we apply Dirac’s rule
\{\ ,\ \}\rightarrow-i[\ ,\ ]
to quantize. For a 2D phase space with coordinates
(z^{1},z^{2})=(P,Q)
, the PB of two dynamical variables
F(P,Q)
and
G(P,Q)
becomes

\displaystyle\{F,G\}

\displaystyle=(\omega^{-1})^{ij}\frac{\partial F}{\partial z^{i}}\frac{\partial G}{\partial z^{j}}

\displaystyle=(\omega^{-1})^{12}\left(\frac{\partial F}{\partial P}\frac{\partial G}{\partial Q}-\frac{\partial F}{\partial Q}\frac{\partial G}{\partial P}\right)

(5.46)

For our theory this reads

\displaystyle\{F,G\}=\frac{Z(Q)^{3}}{(\ln Q)^{3}}\left(\frac{\partial F}{\partial P}\frac{\partial G}{\partial Q}-\frac{\partial F}{\partial Q}\frac{\partial G}{\partial P}\right)

(5.47)

Applying Dirac’s rule we get

\displaystyle[\hat{F},\hat{G}]=i\frac{Z(\hat{Q})^{3}}{(\ln\hat{Q})^{3}}\left(\frac{\partial\hat{F}}{\partial\hat{P}}\frac{\partial\hat{G}}{\partial\hat{Q}}-\frac{\partial\hat{F}}{\partial\hat{Q}}\frac{\partial\hat{G}}{\partial\hat{P}}\right)

(5.48)

where hatted variables are corresponding quantum operators.
In particular, we have

\displaystyle[\hat{Q},\hat{P}]=-i\frac{Z(\hat{Q})^{3}}{(\ln\hat{Q})^{3}}

(5.49)

Acting on a wavefunction
f
we get

\displaystyle[\hat{Q},\hat{P}]f=\hat{P}(Q)f

(5.50)

so that

\displaystyle\hat{P}(Q)=-i\frac{Z(Q)^{3}}{(\ln Q)^{3}}

(5.51)

Therefore we have

\displaystyle\hat{P}=-i\frac{Z(Q)^{3}}{(\ln Q)^{3}}\frac{\partial}{\partial Q}

(5.52)

We will suppress hats from now on, the distinction should be clear from the context.

The Schrodinger equation is

\displaystyle H\psi=E\psi

(5.53)

There is an ambiguity in the ordering of operators in the Hamiltonian. We will choose the simplest ordering for which

\displaystyle H=\frac{\pi^{3}}{c}\frac{1}{Z(Q)^{2}}P^{2}

(5.54)

We need to find the action of
P^{2}
on
\psi

\displaystyle P^{2}\psi

\displaystyle=-i\frac{Z(Q)^{3}}{(\ln Q)^{3}}\frac{\partial}{\partial Q}\left(-i\frac{Z(Q)^{3}}{(\ln Q)^{3}}\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial Q}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{Z^{6}}{(\ln Q)^{6}}\left[3\left(\frac{Z^{\prime}}{Z}-\frac{1}{Q\ln Q}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial Q}+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial Q^{2}}\right]\psi

(5.55)

where
Z^{\prime}=dZ/dQ
. Inserting this into the Schrodinger equation we obtain

\displaystyle-\frac{\pi^{3}}{c}\frac{Z^{4}}{(\ln Q)^{6}}\left[\frac{1}{Q}\left(\frac{2(Q-1)^{2}}{Z}-\frac{1}{\ln Q}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial Q}+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial Q^{2}}\right]\psi=E\psi

(5.56)

This equation is not solvable in closed form for an arbitrary
E
. On the other hand, if we consider the simplest case of
E=0
the equation simplifies to

\displaystyle\left[\frac{1}{Q}\left(\frac{2(Q-1)^{2}}{3-4Q+Q^{2}+2\ln Q}-\frac{1}{\ln Q}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial Q}+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial Q^{2}}\right]\psi=0

(5.57)

which is of the form

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime\prime}+f(Q)\psi^{\prime}=0

(5.58)

with

\displaystyle f(Q)=-\frac{1}{Q}\left(\frac{2(Q-1)^{2}}{3-4Q+Q^{2}+2\ln Q}-\frac{1}{\ln Q}\right)

(5.59)

Then one gets the nonlocal solution

\displaystyle\psi(Q)=C_{1}+C_{2}\int_{1}^{Q}dq\ \frac{\ln q}{2\ln q+q^{2}-4q+3}

(5.60)

where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are constants.

The problem here is that the integrand of (
5.60
) blows up at
Q=1
which is due to
f(Q)
being divergent at
Q=1
. However,

\displaystyle\lim_{Q\rightarrow 1}(Q-1)f(Q-1)=-2

(5.61)

so that the singularity is regular and we can apply Frobenius method to get a series solution. These problems seem to stem from the singularity
Q=1
of the Hamiltonian.

We will not continue this analysis since we later realized that the DX theory is inconsistent as other constraints need to be provided on the phase space for consistency of the diff-Gauss law. This will be shown in the next chapter where we introduce the DXN theory which is equivalent to the DX theory on the constraint surface defined by the diff-Gauss law. In fact the source of singularities encountered in the analysis above may be due to these missing contraints.

5.4
Covariantized Transverse Action and Chiral Gauge

In this section we will show that every aspect (momenta, field equations, etc.) of the covariantized transverse action evaluated in 2D Minkowski spacetime simplifies in an ansatz that we call chiral ansatz (or chiral gauge).

This suggests that the underlying gauge condition for the transverse action before covariantization is
D_{01}=N=0
(which can be extended to
D_{0\mu}=0
as
D_{00}=\varphi
is completely invisible), but it becomes the chiral ansatz for the covariantized transverse action. This ansatz soldifies the change of character of the theory after covariantization as we will see in a variety of aspects below.

5.4.1
Momenta

After covariantization the nondynamical diff field component
N=D_{01}
becomes dynamical, which implies that the diff-Gauss law is not a constraint any more.
To remedy this problem we first set
N
to zero. However, for this to be a consistent condition we need to make the conjugate momentum to
N
,
X^{010}
vanish as well. It turns out, however, that there is a symmetry between
X^{010}
and
X^{011}
:

\displaystyle X^{010}=-\beta\varphi^{\prime}-\alpha\varphi\varphi^{\prime}-6q\ddot{\varphi}^{\prime\prime}

(5.62)

\displaystyle X^{011}=+\beta\dot{D}-\alpha D\dot{D}-6q\dot{D}^{\prime\prime}

(5.63)

These are symmetric under
\beta\leftrightarrow-\beta
,
\varphi\leftrightarrow D
and
t\leftrightarrow x
. So, instead of requiring vanishing of just the conjugate momentum
X^{010}
if we require vanishing of the generalized momentum
X^{01\mu}
of
N
we see that the required conditions also become symmetric:

\displaystyle N=0\ \ ,\ \ \frac{d}{dx}\varphi(x,t)=0=\frac{d}{dt}D(x,t)

(5.64)

We call this the chiral gauge. The motivation of the name is the following. Suppose we take
x\leftrightarrow x^{-}
,
t\leftrightarrow x^{+}
as in the geometric actions. Then the chiral gauge reads

\displaystyle D_{-+}=0\ \ ,\ \ \partial_{-}D_{++}=0=\partial_{+}D_{--}

(5.65)

These are precisely the conditions we used to build the geometric action on the coadjoint orbits of the direct product of two Virasoro algebras in Section
3.6
.
They represent the chiral splitting of the energy momentum tensor and field equations in 2D conformal field theories.

What do these conditions imply for the momenta of the remaining fields? Since we made the diff component
D
time-independent, its consistency requires the vanishing of its conjugate momentum. This is indeed the case, we automatically get
X^{110}=0
in the chiral ansatz. The remaining component of its generalized momentum reads

\displaystyle X^{111}=-D^{\prime}-2\beta D^{\prime}+3\alpha DD^{\prime}+6qD^{\prime\prime\prime}

(5.66)

As an interesting side note, vanishing of
X^{111}
yields the KdV equation (with free parameters
\beta,\alpha,q
) as time derivative of
D
vanishes by hypothesis. Alternatively, consider a generic KdV equation of the form

\displaystyle aD^{\prime}+bDD^{\prime}+cD^{\prime\prime\prime}=d\dot{D}

(5.67)

for constants
a,b,c,d
. Introducing wave solutions
\tilde{D}(z)\equiv D(x,t)
with
z=x+et
with a constant
e
, (
5.67
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle(a-de)\partial_{z}\tilde{D}+b\tilde{D}\partial_{z}\tilde{D}+c\partial_{z}^{3}\tilde{D}=0

(5.68)

Similarly, the spatial component of the generalized momentum of
\varphi
vanishes,
X^{001}=0
and its conjugate momentum reads

\displaystyle X^{000}=\dot{\varphi}+2\beta\dot{\varphi}+3\alpha\varphi\dot{\varphi}+6q\dddot{\varphi}

(5.69)

5.4.2
Acyclicity

We take the full diff Lagrangian as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=X_{abc}X^{abc}

(5.70)

where
X_{abc}
is the diff momentum and is given by the covariant expression

\displaystyle X_{abc}=D_{abc}

\displaystyle+\beta(D_{acb}+D_{bca})

\displaystyle+g^{de}\Big(\alpha(D_{ec}D_{abd}+D_{db}D_{eca}+D_{da}D_{ecb})+qD_{ec(abd)}\Big)

(5.71)

Here,
D_{abc}\equiv\nabla_{c}D_{ab}
,
D_{abcd}\equiv\nabla_{d}\nabla_{c}D_{ab}
etc and

\displaystyle D_{(abd)}=(D_{abd}+D_{adb}+D_{bad}+D_{bda}+D_{dab}+D_{dba})/6

(5.72)

The acyclic Lagrangian is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{ac}}=(X_{abc}+X_{bca}+X_{cab})X^{abc}/3

(5.73)

The acyclicity condition can be stated as

\displaystyle\Delta\equiv\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{L}_{c}\overset{!}{=}0

(5.74)

The first requirement is to set
\beta=1
in the Lagrangian. Then the difference
\Delta
at the covariant level reduces to terms with coefficients
\alpha q
,
\alpha^{2}
and
q^{2}
. If we ignore these terms we get back the BLRY Lagrangian.
Hence, the BLRY action is the acyclic part of the diff action (when
\beta=1
) at the covariant level.

We flat-reduce the difference from acyclicity, i.e. we replace covariant derivatives with ordinary ones, then evaluate the expression in the temporal gauge
N=0
. As mentioned above, the temporal gauge is necessary for obtaining the diff-Gauss law as a (constraint) field equation from the theory as
N
is the Lagrange multiplier of the diff-Gauss law introduced in building the transverse action.

So far we have tried to analyze the action with the full temporal gauge

\displaystyle N=0=\varphi

(5.75)

For instance, in order to recover the diff-Gauss law as the field equation for
D_{01}=N
we need
N=0
and
\varphi
is not at all present before covariantization. In this gauge the difference from acycility does not vanish for the full diff Lagrangian.

On the other hand, the difference from acyclicity vanishes for the full, covariantized theory in the chiral ansatz (
5.64
).

5.4.3
Field Equations

For simplicity we are going to display equations only for the BLRY theory whereas for the full theory we are going to state the general features.
The field equations of the BLRY theory evaluated in 2D Minkowski spacetime with the full temporal gauge
\varphi=N=0
read

\displaystyle\text{FE}_{11}\ :\ \ 0

\displaystyle=2\ddot{D}-6\alpha D^{\prime 2}-2D^{\prime\prime}-8\beta D^{\prime\prime}-12\alpha DD^{\prime\prime}-24qD^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}

(5.76)

\displaystyle\text{FE}_{12}\ :\ \ 0

\displaystyle=2\alpha\dot{D}D^{\prime}+4\beta\dot{D}^{\prime}+4\alpha D\dot{D}^{\prime}+24q\dot{D}^{\prime\prime\prime}

(5.77)

\displaystyle\text{FE}_{22}\ :\ \ 0

\displaystyle=2\alpha\dot{D}^{2}-24q\ddot{D}^{\prime\prime}

(5.78)

On the other hand, in the chiral gauge we get

\displaystyle\text{FE}_{11}\ :\ \ 0

\displaystyle=24qD^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}-12\alpha DD^{\prime\prime}-8\beta D^{\prime\prime}-2D^{\prime\prime}-6\alpha D^{\prime 2}

(5.79)

\displaystyle\text{FE}_{12}\ :\ \ 0

\displaystyle=0

(5.80)

\displaystyle\text{FE}_{22}\ :\ \ 0

\displaystyle=-24q\ddddot{\varphi}-12\alpha\varphi\ddot{\varphi}+8\beta\ddot{\varphi}+2\ddot{\varphi}-6\alpha\dot{\varphi}^{2}

(5.81)

Now, this is remarkable situation. Firstly, the consistency condition for
N=0
gauge is satisfied i.e. the field equation for
N=0
is identically satisfied, instead of yielding the diff-Gauss law. Secondly, the field equations for
D(x)
and
\varphi(t)
are decoupled, and have the same structure, and become identical for
\beta\leftrightarrow-\beta
and
q\leftrightarrow-q
. These equations are not solvable in closed form for
\alpha\neq 0
and
q\neq 0
. In the case,
\alpha=0
, for instance,
D
equation is solved by

\displaystyle D(x)=c_{1}+c_{2}x+2qe^{-x/\sqrt{2q}}(c_{3}e^{2x/\sqrt{2q}}+c_{4})

(5.82)

and in
q=0
case we have

\displaystyle D_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=f(x)\equiv\frac{2\beta+1}{3\alpha}+\left(\frac{3}{4\alpha}-c_{1}^{2}(x^{2}-2c_{2}x-c_{2}^{2})\right)^{1/3}

\displaystyle D_{2,3}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1\pm i\sqrt{3}}{2}f(x)

(5.83)

Since
\varphi
equation is of the same form with independent variable
t
and with slightly different coefficients, it is solved by similar expressions for the
\alpha=0
or
q=0
.

The same features (i.e. decoupling and spatial
D
vs temporal
\varphi
) is seen in the full theory although the equations are of higher order (sixth) and more complicated. We still have the field equation for
N
satisfied identically, i.e.,
0=0
. Similar features hold for the energy momentum tensor, and the conservation equations.

To summarize, in the chiral ansatz, there are no constraints, so there are no consistency conditions to check. The transverse action after covariantization evaluated in the 2D Minkowski spacetime reduces to the theory of two decoupled fields, satisfying the same structure of equations, one in space (so frozen), and the other in the time variable. Due to the decoupling, one can set one of the fields to zero and analyze the other one. As we will show below, the problem of symmetric criticality does not arise, so the consistency seen in momenta and the field equations is carried to the Lagrangian level.

5.4.4
Lagrangian and Problem of Symmetric Criticality

As a final note, let us mention another nice feature of the chiral ansatz. For this purpose let us introduce the problem of symmetric criticality (see, for instance,
[
bojowald
]
Chapter 2). Namely, in general, the field equations obtained from a gauge-fixed Lagrangian are not necessarily the same as the gauge-fixed field equations obtained from the non-gauge-fixed Lagrangian. In other words, variations and gauge fixing do not commute; at which step you apply the gauge fixing, on-shell or off-shell, does matter.

For instance, if the temporal gauge
N=0
is applied at the Lagrangian level, then the resulting Lagrangian will lead to
0=0
as the field equation for
N
as it does not depend on
N
. However, if the Lagrangian is first varied and the resulting field equations are subjected to
N=0
gauge, then the field equation for
N
no more yields
0=0
, it becomes a constraint equation (the diff-Gauss law). Therefore, by applying
N=0
gauge at the Lagrangian level one loses information.

Along the same line of reasoning in the full temporal gauge
N=0=\varphi
symmetric criticality problem arises as (constraint) information about
N
and
\varphi
are completely lost, and moreover,
D
field equation is altered.

Now, the problem of symmetric criticality does not appear in the chiral gauge, since in this gauge, the field equation for
N
becomes
0=0
in either case and
D
and
\varphi
equations remain exactly the same (for both the full and the BLRY actions) as can be verified. So one can work with the gauge-fixed Lagrangian. For simplicity let us consider the chiral BLRY Lagrangian given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=(1+2\beta+3\alpha\varphi)\dot{\varphi}^{2}+6q\dot{\varphi}\dddot{\varphi}-(1+2\beta-3\alpha D)D^{\prime 2}+6qD^{\prime}D^{\prime\prime\prime}

(5.84)

Hence, decoupling is apparent at the Lagrangian level and the chiral gauge is a consistent ansatz at the Lagrangian level as well.

5.5
BLRY Hamiltonian in 2D Minkowski Spacetime

Recall the BLRY Lagrangian,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=D_{abc}X^{abc}

(5.85)

In this form
\mathcal{L}
seems to be singular and third-order in time for each diff field component, since the higher derivative terms are of the form
q\,\nabla^{c}D_{ab}\,\nabla^{(d}\nabla^{b}\nabla^{a)}D_{dc}
. This is an illusion since we can partially integrate one of the derivatives in the second factor, which is equivalent to the addition of a total divergence
\nabla_{a}f^{a}
to
\mathcal{L}
, and it is well known that the field equations are invariant under this operation. Moreover, the resulting Lagrangian is second-order.

Carrying out this operation we end up with the following Lagrangian in 2D Minkowski spacetime :

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=C+B_{1}\dot{D}+B_{2}\dot{N}+B_{3}\dot{\varphi}+8qD^{\prime\prime}\dot{N}^{\prime}+16q\dot{D}^{\prime}N^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle+A_{1}\dot{D}^{2}+A_{2}\dot{N}^{2}+A_{3}\dot{\varphi}^{2}

\displaystyle+-4\alpha N\dot{N}\dot{\varphi}-16q\dot{N}^{\prime 2}-8q\dot{D}^{\prime}\dot{\varphi}^{\prime}-8qN^{\prime\prime}\ddot{N}-4q\ddot{D}\varphi^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle+8q\dot{N}^{\prime}\ddot{\varphi}+16q\ddot{N}\dot{\varphi}^{\prime}-6q\ddot{\varphi}^{2}

(5.86)

The ordering is such that the first line consists of zeroth and first-order terms in time, second and third lines consist of second-order terms in time and the last line consists of third and fourth-order terms in time. Here,

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=1+\alpha\varphi\ \ ,\ \ A_{2}=-2-2\beta+2\alpha(D-\varphi)\ \ ,\ \ A_{3}=1+2\beta+3\alpha\varphi

(5.87a)

\displaystyle B_{1}

\displaystyle=-2\alpha N(D^{\prime}-2\varphi^{\prime})+(4\beta-4\alpha D)N^{\prime}

(5.87b)

\displaystyle B_{2}

\displaystyle=8\alpha NN^{\prime}-4(\beta+\alpha\varphi)\varphi^{\prime}\ \ ,\ \ B_{3}=-2\alpha N\varphi^{\prime}

(5.87c)

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=C_{1}D^{\prime 2}+C_{2}N^{\prime 2}+C_{3}\varphi^{\prime 2}+C_{4}

(5.87d)

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle=-1-2\beta+3\alpha D\ \ ,\ \ C_{2}=2+2\beta-2\alpha(D-\varphi)

(5.87e)

\displaystyle C_{3}

\displaystyle=-1+\alpha D\ \ ,\ \ C_{4}=-4\alpha D^{\prime}NN^{\prime}-6qD^{\prime\prime 2}

(5.87f)

For a second-order field theory conjugate momenta can be defined through

\displaystyle\pi_{\phi}=\frac{\delta S}{\delta\dot{\phi}}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\dot{\phi}}-\partial_{\mu}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial(\partial_{\mu}\dot{\phi})}\ \ ,\ \ \pi_{\dot{\phi}}=\frac{\delta S}{\delta\ddot{\phi}}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\ddot{\phi}}

(5.88)

These yield

\displaystyle\pi_{D}

\displaystyle=2A_{1}\dot{D}+B_{1}+4q(3\dot{\varphi}^{\prime\prime}-4N^{\prime\prime\prime})

(5.89a)

\displaystyle\pi_{N}

\displaystyle=2A_{2}\dot{N}+B_{2}-4\alpha N\dot{\varphi}+8q(-D^{\prime\prime\prime}+5\dot{N}^{\prime\prime}-3\ddot{\varphi}^{\prime})

(5.89b)

\displaystyle\pi_{\varphi}

\displaystyle=2A_{3}\dot{\varphi}+B_{3}-4\alpha N\dot{N}+4q(2\dot{D}^{\prime\prime}-6\ddot{N}^{\prime}+3\dddot{\varphi})

(5.89c)

\displaystyle\pi_{\dot{D}}

\displaystyle=-4q\varphi^{\prime\prime}

(5.89d)

\displaystyle\pi_{\dot{N}}

\displaystyle=-8qN^{\prime\prime}+16q\dot{\varphi}^{\prime}

(5.89e)

\displaystyle\pi_{\dot{\varphi}}

\displaystyle=-12q\ddot{\varphi}+8q\dot{N}^{\prime}

(5.89f)

We see from the last three equations that the theory is singular in
D
and
N
whereas nonsingular in
\varphi
.

We can convert the higher order theory to an ordinary first-order theory by a redefinition of variables as described in Section
A.2.2
.
For this purpose we introduce the new variables,

\displaystyle Q_{1}\equiv D,\ \ Q_{2}\equiv\dot{D},\ \ Q_{3}\equiv N,\ \ Q_{4}\equiv\dot{N},\ \ Q_{5}\equiv\varphi,\ \ Q_{6}\equiv\dot{\varphi}

(5.90)

The main difference from the analysis in Section
A.2.2
is that we now have three (infinite sets of) variables, and since they are fields on spacetime, in addition to the time derivatives there are spatial derivatives. Spatial derivatives have no significance in the canonical analysis so they can be treated as additional indices.

We can then introduce the first-order (singular) Lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{T}

\displaystyle=C+B_{1}Q_{2}+B_{2}Q_{4}+B_{3}Q_{6}+8qQ_{1}^{\prime\prime}Q_{4}^{\prime}+16qQ_{2}^{\prime}Q_{3}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle+A_{1}Q_{2}^{2}+A_{2}Q_{4}^{2}+A_{3}Q_{6}^{2}-4\alpha Q_{3}Q_{4}Q_{6}-16qQ_{4}^{\prime 2}-8qQ_{2}^{\prime}Q_{6}^{\prime}

\displaystyle-8qQ_{3}^{\prime\prime}\dot{Q}_{4}-4q\dot{Q}_{2}Q_{5}^{\prime\prime}+8qQ_{4}^{\prime}\dot{Q}_{6}+16q\dot{Q}_{4}Q_{6}^{\prime}-6q\dot{Q}_{6}^{2}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{1}(\dot{Q}_{1}-Q_{2})+\lambda_{2}(\dot{Q}_{3}-Q_{4})+\lambda_{3}(\dot{Q}_{5}-Q_{6})

(5.91)

The constraints in the last line are added for correspondence with the original Lagrangian (
5.5
). The factors
A_{i}
,
B_{i}
and
C
are spatial functionals of
Q_{1},Q_{3},Q_{5}
only.

We introduce the conjugate momenta

\displaystyle P_{1}\equiv\pi_{D},\ \ P_{2}\equiv\pi_{\dot{D}},\ \ P_{3}\equiv\pi_{N},\ \ P_{4}\equiv\pi_{\dot{N}},\ \ P_{5}\equiv\pi_{\varphi},\ \ P_{6}\equiv\pi_{\dot{\varphi}}\ \ ,\ \ p_{i}\equiv\pi_{\lambda_{i}}

(5.92)

Then applying (
5.88
) to
S_{T}
we can compute the momenta

\displaystyle P_{2i-1}=\lambda_{i},\ \ i=1,2,3

(5.93a)

\displaystyle P_{2}=-4qQ_{5}^{\prime\prime}

(5.93b)

\displaystyle P_{4}=-8qQ_{3}^{\prime\prime}+16qQ_{6}^{\prime}

(5.93c)

\displaystyle P_{6}=-12q\dot{Q}_{6}+8qQ_{4}^{\prime}

(5.93d)

\displaystyle p_{i}=0\ ,\ \ i=1,2,3

(5.93e)

The equation (
5.93d
) can be inverted for
\dot{Q}_{6}
yielding

\displaystyle\dot{Q}_{6}=-\frac{1}{12q}P_{6}+\frac{2}{3}Q_{4}^{\prime}

(5.94)

so, the theory is nonsingular in
\varphi
.

Except the
P_{6}
equation (
5.93d
), all the equations (
5.93
) constitute primary constraints. Out of these, only
P_{2}
and
P_{4}
equations are essential, signifying the singularity in
D
and
N
. The remaining ones arise due to the introduction of the Lagrange multipliers to reduce the second-order theory to a first-order theory, and can be eliminated using the field equations for
\lambda_{i}
. Let us denote the main primary constraints as

\displaystyle\phi_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv P_{2}+4qQ_{5}^{\prime\prime}\approx 0

(5.95a)

\displaystyle\phi_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv P_{4}+8qQ_{3}^{\prime\prime}-16qQ_{6}^{\prime}\approx 0

(5.95b)

The canonical (or naive) Hamiltonian (density) is formed, as usual, as

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{c}=P_{I}\dot{Q}_{I}+p_{i}\dot{\lambda}_{i}-L_{T}\ \ \ ,\ \ I=1,\cdots,6\ \ ;\ \ i=1,2,3

(5.96)

This yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{c}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}Q_{2}+\lambda_{2}Q_{4}+\lambda_{3}Q_{6}-A_{1}Q_{2}^{2}-A_{2}Q_{4}^{2}-A_{3}Q_{6}^{2}

\displaystyle\ \ -B_{1}Q_{2}-B_{2}Q_{4}-B_{3}Q_{6}-C-8qQ_{1}^{\prime\prime}Q_{4}^{\prime}-16qQ_{2}^{\prime}Q_{3}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\ \ +4\alpha Q_{3}Q_{4}Q_{6}+8qQ_{2}^{\prime}Q_{6}^{\prime}-(1/24q)(P_{6}-8qQ_{4}^{\prime})^{2}+16qQ_{4}^{\prime 2}

(5.97)

In order to form the total Hamiltonian density,
\mathcal{H}_{T}
we need to add each primary constraint followed by the equations (
5.93
) with some multipliers. As mentioned above, however, some of these constraints are not essential. Hence, we will shortcut the procedure by eliminating these. For this purpose, we will not introduce the constraints
p_{i}=0
,
i=1,2,3\
into the action and we will replace
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}
by
P_{1},P_{3},P_{5}
, respectively. This gets rid of all the nonessential constraints. So we will introduce only the essential constraints
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
given by (
5.95
) into the Hamiltonian with multipliers
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
, respectively. The result is

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{T}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}_{c}+\mu_{1}\phi_{1}+\mu_{2}\phi_{2}

\displaystyle=P_{1}Q_{2}+P_{3}Q_{4}+P_{5}Q_{6}-A_{1}Q_{2}^{2}-A_{2}Q_{4}^{2}-A_{3}Q_{6}^{2}

\displaystyle\ \ -B_{1}Q_{2}-B_{2}Q_{4}-B_{3}Q_{6}-C-8qQ_{1}^{\prime\prime}Q_{4}^{\prime}-16qQ_{2}^{\prime}Q_{3}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\ \ +4\alpha Q_{3}Q_{4}Q_{6}+8qQ_{2}^{\prime}Q_{6}^{\prime}-(1/24q)(P_{6}-8qQ_{4}^{\prime})^{2}+16qQ_{4}^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle\ \ +\mu_{1}(P_{2}+4qQ_{5}^{\prime\prime})+\mu_{2}(P_{4}+8qQ_{3}^{\prime\prime}-16qQ_{6}^{\prime})

(5.98)

Next step is checking consistency conditions for the primary constraints to see whether we get secondary constraints or conditions on the functions
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
. We will not continue this analysis since the diff-Gauss law is lost as a constraint.
N
became dynamical and the theory lost its connection to its origins in 1D.

As a final note, higher order analysis of the full diff Lagrangian is no different. Since the full theory is of higher order than the BLRY theory, one merely needs to define more variables to obtain a first-order reduction.

Chapter 6
CONSTRAINT ANALYSIS IN 2D MINKOWSKI SPACETIME

6.1
Introduction

In this chapter we will first analyze the transverse action before covariantization. We call this theory the DXN theory. This theory is equivalent to the DX theory (Section
5.3
) on-shell (i.e. on the constraint surface). The diff-Gauss law arises as an explicit (secondary) constraint from Hamilton’s equations rather than being implicitly enforced through an equivalence relation on the phase space.

We will apply Dirac’s constrained Hamiltonian formalism to the DXN theory and show that consistency equation of the diff-Gauss law implies the existence of an independent constraint. The constraint algebra closes with one primary, three secondary constraints and a condition on the Lagrange multiplier. The diff-Gauss law turns out to be second-class unless the kinetic term itself is turned into a constraint.

Hence, we turn the kinetic term to a constraint. This is good in one respect, that a theory of gravitation requires time reparametrization invariance
[
DiracLectures
]
which, in turn, requires the Hamiltonian to consist purely of first-class constraints (i.e. to vanish on-shell).
However, since we chose the standard kinetic term of the form
\sim X^{2}
(both for the finite reduction of the
DX
theory and the transverse diff field theory) such a modification implies the theory is trivial.

In general relativity also the Hamiltonian consists of constraints, yet the ”kinetic term” is not standard.
Indeed, in the diff field case the higher order terms suggest that the standard kinetic term may not be suitable to build the dynamical theory. An alternative kinetic term, which may lead to a nontrivial dynamical theory, will be introduced and analyzed.

In the last section of this chapter, we review and summarize the tranverse diff theory, and its problems. Then we investigate the modifications required to maintain covariance of the diff Lagrangian and its interactions.

6.2
Transverse Action Before Covariantization - DXN Theory

6.2.1
Lagrangian

Recall that the momentum (
4.67
) of the diff field in flat spacetime of arbitrary dimension, and in lower indices is given by

\displaystyle X_{ij0}=\partial_{0}D_{ij}

\displaystyle-\Big[D_{k0}\partial_{k}D_{ij}+D_{kj}\partial_{i}D_{k0}+D_{ik}\partial_{j}D_{k0}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ +q\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\partial_{k}D_{k0}-\beta(\partial_{i}D_{0j}+\partial_{j}D_{i0})\Big]

(6.1)

In 2D, before covariantization, the diff field action involves only two degrees of freedom,
D_{01}\equiv N
and
D_{11}\equiv D
. Only the latter is dynamical, having a conjugate momentum
X\equiv X^{110}
. For the Minkowski metric with the sign convention
(+t,-x)
we have
X_{110}=X^{110}=X
. As usual we will denote
\partial_{1}
by a prime and
\partial_{0}
by a dot. With these we get

\displaystyle X=\dot{D}-(ND^{\prime}+2DN^{\prime}-2\beta N^{\prime}+qN^{\prime\prime\prime})

(6.2)

This suggests that we can also shift
D
by a constant
\beta
and simplify this to

\displaystyle X=\dot{D}-(ND^{\prime}+2DN^{\prime}+qN^{\prime\prime\prime})

(6.3)

We can write the momentum compactly as

\displaystyle X=\dot{D}-\mathcal{G}[N;D]

(6.4)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}[\xi;D]:=\xi D^{\prime}+2\xi^{\prime}D+q\xi^{\prime\prime\prime}

(6.5)

is the ”coadjoint action” of a vector field
\xi
on the diff field. In the following we denote
\mathcal{G}\equiv\mathcal{G}[N;D]
. Notice that this is not the same as the diff-Gauss law operator,
G_{1}=\mathcal{G}[X;D]
.

Then the Lagrangian before covariantization, (
4.3.3
), reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{2}X^{2}=\frac{1}{2}(\dot{D}-\mathcal{G})^{2}

(6.6)

in 2D flat spacetime. We call the theory defined by this Lagrangian the DXN theory.

6.2.2
Full vs BLRY Lagrangians

Recall the BLRY Lagrangian (
4.76
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{BLRY}}=\frac{1}{2}D_{abc}X^{abc}

(6.7)

Before covariantization, in 2D Minkowski spacetime the diff action reads

\displaystyle S=\frac{1}{2}\int(\dot{D}-\mathcal{G})^{2}

(6.8)

and the BLRY action reads

\displaystyle S_{\text{BLRY}}=\frac{1}{2}\int\dot{D}(\dot{D}-\mathcal{G})

(6.9)

where
\mathcal{G}\equiv\mathcal{G}[N;D]
in (
6.5
), and the measure
d^{2}x
is suppressed.

We see from these expressions that it is not right to say that the BLRY Lagrangian is the
N=0
limit of the diff Lagrangian. Rather the
N=0
limits of these theories do match up, as
\mathcal{G}
vanishes. In particular, the field equation of
N
in
N=0
gauge yields the diff-Gauss law, in both cases. Let us show this. Varying (
6.8
) with respect to
N
we get

\displaystyle\delta S

\displaystyle=\int X\delta\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle=\int X(\delta ND^{\prime}+2\delta N^{\prime}D+q\delta N^{\prime\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=-\int\delta N(XD^{\prime}+2X^{\prime}D+qX^{\prime\prime\prime})

(6.10)

So the field equation of
N
reads

\displaystyle 0=XD^{\prime}+2X^{\prime}D+qX^{\prime\prime\prime}

(6.11)

Similarly, for the BLRY action we get

\displaystyle\delta S_{\text{BLRY}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int\dot{D}(-\delta\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle=-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int\dot{D}(\delta ND^{\prime}+2\delta N^{\prime}D+q\delta N^{\prime\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int\delta N(\dot{D}D^{\prime}+2\dot{D}^{\prime}D+q\dot{D}^{\prime\prime\prime})

(6.12)

So the field equation of
N
reads

\displaystyle 0=(\dot{D}D^{\prime}+2\dot{D}^{\prime}D+q\dot{D}^{\prime\prime\prime})

(6.13)

This is the diff-Gauss law (
6.11
) only for
N=0
.

6.2.3
Hamiltonian

The conjugate momentum to
D
is given by (
6.4
).
We also have the variable
N
, but the Lagrangian is independent of its velocity, so, denoting its conjugate momentum by
\pi
, we have the primary constraint

\displaystyle\phi_{1}\equiv\pi=\frac{\delta L}{\delta\dot{N}}=0

(6.14)

The naive Hamiltonian density is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}

\displaystyle=\dot{D}X+\dot{N}\pi-\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}X^{2}+X(ND^{\prime}+2N^{\prime}D+qN^{\prime\prime\prime})

(6.15)

Adding the primary constraint (
6.14
) with a Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
we get the total Hamiltonian density

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{T}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}+\lambda\pi_{N}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}X^{2}+X(ND^{\prime}+2N^{\prime}D+qN^{\prime\prime\prime})+\lambda\pi

(6.16)

The total Hamiltonian is defined by the space integral

\displaystyle H_{T}=\int dx\,\mathcal{H}_{T}

(6.17)

Hamilton’s equations yield

\displaystyle\dot{D}=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta H_{T}}{\delta X}=X+ND^{\prime}+2N^{\prime}D+qN^{\prime\prime\prime}

(6.18a)

\displaystyle\dot{X}=-

\displaystyle\frac{\delta H_{T}}{\delta D}=X^{\prime}N-XN^{\prime}

(6.18b)

\displaystyle\dot{N}=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta H_{T}}{\delta\pi}=\lambda

(6.18c)

\displaystyle\dot{\pi}=-

\displaystyle\frac{\delta H_{T}}{\delta N}=XD^{\prime}+2X^{\prime}D+qX^{\prime\prime\prime}

(6.18d)

The first one is the momentum
X
definition (
6.4
