) reproduced. The second one is the main dynamical equation for the diff field. The third one tells us that
N
is arbitrary as its velocity is arbitrary. Finally the last one tells us that the diff-Gauss law is obtained as a secondary constraint following from the consistency condition of the primary constraint (just as in the canonical analysis of the YM theory) i.e.

\displaystyle\dot{\pi}_{N}=0\ \ \Rightarrow\ \ XD^{\prime}+2X^{\prime}D+qX^{\prime\prime\prime}=0

(6.19)

We will continue the constraint analysis in the next section.
Let us remark here that the DXN theory, the transverse theory before covariantization, is equivalent to the DX theory introduced in Section
5.3
. The momentum reduces on-shell (i.e. for
N=0
) to
\dot{D}
and the diff-Gauss law emerges as the field (constraint) equation of the nondynamical field
N
. In this equivalence, Rajeev’s theory reviewed in Section
5.2
corresponds to the DX theory and the original YM theory in 2D corresponds to the DXN theory.

Let us check the solutions to Hamilton’s equations.
Consider the dynamical equation (
6.18b
). To avoid confusion let us use
(s,t)
instead of
(x,t)
so that
X=X(s,t)
and
N=N(s)
(recall that
N
is not dynamical). Then,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial X}{\partial t}=N\frac{\partial X}{\partial s}-\frac{\partial N}{\partial s}X

(6.20)

The solution for
X
is

\displaystyle X(s,t)=N(s)F(z)

(6.21)

where

\displaystyle z\equiv t+\int_{1}^{s}\frac{d\sigma}{N(\sigma)}

(6.22)

Plugging this solution into (
6.18a
) and solving for
D
we get

\displaystyle D(s,t)=\frac{1}{N^{2}(s)}\left[G(z)-F(z)\int_{1}^{s}d\sigma\ N^{2}(\sigma)-q\int_{1}^{s}d\sigma\ N(\sigma)N^{\prime\prime\prime}(\sigma)\right]

(6.23)

6.3
Constraint Analysis of DXN Theory

Let us complete the constraint analysis started in the previous section according to Dirac’s formalism
[
DiracLectures
]
which we reviewed in Appendix
A.1
.

Using the primary constraint (
6.14
) we formed the total Hamiltonian and computed Hamilton’s equations. There we saw that the consistency equation (
6.18d
) of the primary constraint
\phi_{1}
implied the diff-Gauss law as a secondary constraint. In this section we will switch to the language of Poisson brackets (PB).

The standard PB for a field theory with phase space variables
\varphi^{a},\pi_{b}
reads

\displaystyle\{F(x)\ ,\ G(y)\}=\int dz\left(\frac{\delta F(x)}{\delta\varphi^{a}(z)}\frac{\delta G(y)}{\delta\pi_{a}(z)}-\frac{\delta F(x)}{\delta\pi^{a}(z)}\frac{\delta G(y)}{\delta\phi_{a}(z)}\right)

(6.24)

From this, for instance, follows

\displaystyle\{\varphi^{c}(x)\ ,\ \pi_{d}(y)\}=\int dz\,\Big(\delta_{a}^{c}\delta(x-z)\delta^{a}_{d}\delta(y-z)-0\Big)=\delta^{c}_{d}\delta(x-y)

(6.25)

The PBs for the DXN theory are taken to be in standard form

\displaystyle\{D(x),X(y)\}=\delta(x-y)\ \ ;\ \ \{N(x),\pi(y)\}=\delta(x-y)\ \ ;\ \ \text{the rest vanish}

(6.26)

where
t
-dependence is suppressed as usual. This implies, for instance,

\displaystyle\{D(y),X^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)\}=\int dz\,\delta(y-z)\,\partial_{x}^{3}\delta(x-z)=\partial^{3}_{x}\delta(x-y)

(6.27)

Recall the total Hamiltonian density

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{T}=\frac{1}{2}X^{2}+X\mathcal{G}+\lambda\pi

(6.28)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}=ND^{\prime}+2N^{\prime}D+qN^{\prime\prime\prime}

(6.29)

For convenience below we shall denote space dependence by a subindex, e.g.,
D_{x}\equiv D(x)
. In the language of PBs, consistency condition for the primary constraint
\phi_{1}
reads

\displaystyle 0\approx\dot{\phi_{1}}=\{\phi_{1},H_{T}\}

(6.30)

Let us show this explicitly

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\approx\left\{\pi_{y}\ ,\int_{x}\frac{1}{2}X^{2}+X_{x}(N_{x}D^{\prime}_{x}+2N^{\prime}_{x}D_{x}+qN^{\prime\prime\prime}_{x})+\lambda_{x}\pi_{x}\right\}

\displaystyle=\int_{x}X_{x}\left\{\pi_{y},N_{x}D^{\prime}_{x}+2N^{\prime}_{x}D_{x}+qN^{\prime\prime\prime}_{x}\right\}

\displaystyle=\int_{x}X_{x}\Big(\partial_{x}D_{x}(-\delta_{xy})+2D_{x}\partial_{x}(-\delta_{xy})+q\partial_{x}^{3}(-\delta_{xy})\Big)

\displaystyle=-\int_{x}\delta_{xy}\Big(X_{x}\partial_{x}D_{x}-2\partial_{x}(X_{x}D_{x})-q\partial_{x}^{3}X_{x}\Big)

\displaystyle=X_{y}D^{\prime}_{y}+2X^{\prime}_{y}D_{y}+qX^{\prime\prime\prime}_{y}

(6.31)

Since this does not involve the Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
, it must be a secondary constraint. Thus we have

\displaystyle\phi_{2}=XD^{\prime}+2X^{\prime}D+qX^{\prime\prime\prime}\approx 0

(6.32)

i.e. the-diff Gauss law is obtained
1
1

1

This is in analogy with YM theory. Vanishing of the momentum
\pi^{0}=F^{00}
conjugate to
A_{0}
implies the Gauss law constraint. The Gauss law is the field equation of
A_{0}
(analog of (
6.18d
)) and it can also be derived by the PB equation
\dot{\pi}^{0}=\{\pi^{0},H_{\text{YM}}\}
(analog of (
6.30
)).
as a secondary constraint that follows from the vanishing of the momentum of
N
.

Next, we need to check for the consistency condition of the secondary constraint to see whether we get another secondary constraint. This yields

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\approx\dot{\phi}_{2}=\{\phi_{2},H_{T}\}

\displaystyle=\left\{XD^{\prime}+2X^{\prime}D+qX^{\prime\prime\prime},\int\frac{1}{2}X^{2}+X\mathcal{G}+\lambda\pi\right\}

(6.33)

After a long, but straightforward calculation this yields

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\approx 3X^{\prime}X+N\phi_{2}^{\prime}+2N^{\prime}\phi_{2}

\displaystyle\approx 3X^{\prime}X

(6.34)

In the last (weak) equality we used the fact that in field theories spatial derivatives of a constraint
(\phi_{2})
do not constitute independent constraints. Thus we obtained a new secondary constraint

\displaystyle\phi_{3}\equiv X^{\prime}X

(6.35)

where we rescaled it by a factor of 3. Notice that this is the derivative of the kinetic term
T=X^{2}/2
.

Computing the consistency condition of
\phi_{3}
we get

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\approx\dot{\phi}_{3}=\{\phi_{3},H_{T}\}=N\phi_{3}^{\prime}-N^{\prime}\phi_{3}-N^{\prime\prime}X^{2}

\displaystyle\approx-N^{\prime\prime}X^{2}

(6.36)

Hence, we obtain another secondary constraint

\displaystyle\phi_{4}\equiv N^{\prime\prime}X^{2}

(6.37)

Notice that this is linear in the kinetic term, though it does not necessarily trivialize the theory as we can set
N^{\prime\prime}=0
to evade
X=0
.

Finally, the consistency condition of
\phi_{4}
reads

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\approx\dot{\phi}_{4}=\lambda^{\prime\prime}X^{2}-2N^{\prime}\phi_{4}+2NN^{\prime\prime}\phi_{3}

\displaystyle\approx\lambda^{\prime\prime}X^{2}

(6.38)

Since this involves the Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
of the primary constraint, it does not constitute a constraint, rather a condition on
\lambda
.

Next step is checking the PBs of the constraint algebra to determine the first-class constraints. For this purpose we are going to introduce the following commonly used tool in higher order field theories. Namely, if
\phi_{x}
is a local expression involving the fields and their derivatives then we define
\phi[\mu]

\displaystyle\phi[\mu]=\int_{x}\mu_{x}\phi_{x}

(6.39)

In fact, we already used it in (
4.49
) to get the diff-Gauss law operator;
Q=G[\xi]
in (
4.49
), in the notation we use here. Calculations involving smeared out fields are essentially the same as in the previous section.

Here are the results for the constraint algebra

\displaystyle\{\phi_{1},\phi_{1,2,3}\}=0

(6.40)

\displaystyle\{\phi_{1},\phi_{4}[\lambda]\}=-\lambda^{\prime\prime}X^{2}+4\lambda^{\prime}\phi_{3}+\lambda\phi_{3}^{\prime}\approx 0

(6.41)

where we used (
6.38
). This calculation shows that
\phi_{1}
is a first-class constraint.

Similarly, we compute

\displaystyle\{\phi_{4},\phi_{3,4}\}=0

(6.42)

\displaystyle\{\phi_{4},\phi_{2}[\mu]\}=2\mu^{\prime}\phi_{4}-2N^{\prime\prime}\mu\phi_{3}\approx 0

(6.43)

With the additional result (
6.41
) we see that
\phi_{4}
is also a first-class constraint.

The remaining two constraints (in particular,
\phi_{2}
, the diff-Gauss law) are second-class due to the following bracket

\displaystyle\{\phi_{3},\phi_{2}[\mu]\}=\mu^{\prime\prime}X^{2}+\mu^{\prime}\phi_{3}-\mu\phi_{3}^{\prime}\approx\mu^{\prime\prime}X^{2}

(6.44)

or, alternatively,

\displaystyle\{\phi_{2},\phi_{3}[\mu]\}=-3\mu^{\prime}\phi_{3}-\mu^{\prime\prime}X^{2}\approx-\mu^{\prime\prime}X^{2}

(6.45)

Both brackets tell us that in order for
\phi_{2}
and
\phi_{3}
to be first-class we need kinetic term
X^{2}
to be a constraint since
\mu
is an arbitrary function that we introduced, and we can not impose a condition on it.

6.4
Consistent Constraint Algebra, Yet Trivial Theory

The constraint analysis in the previous section suggested that the kinetic term

\displaystyle T=X^{2}/2

(6.46)

be turned into a constraint. Therefore, let us consider the following theory

\displaystyle H_{T}=\int(\mu_{1}\phi_{1}+\mu_{2}\phi_{2})

(6.47)

where

\displaystyle\phi_{1}\equiv X^{2}/2\ \ \ \ ,\ \ \ \ \phi_{2}\equiv XD^{\prime}+2X^{\prime}D+qX^{\prime\prime\prime}

(6.48)

and
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
are Lagrange multipliers.
The unconstrained phase space consists of field configurations
X,D
and the nondynamical variable
N
is discarded. The Hamiltonian vanishes weakly.

We need to show that the constraint algebra closes, no new constraints arise from the consistency conditions. Moreover, for time reparametrization invariance we also need to make sure that the constraints are first-class, generating gauge transformations.
We have

\displaystyle H_{T}=\phi_{1}[\mu_{1}]+\phi_{2}[\mu_{2}]

(6.49)

Here are the results for the constraint algebra defined by (
6.47
)

\displaystyle\{\phi_{1}[\mu],\phi_{1}[\lambda]\}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\{\phi_{1}[\mu],\phi_{2}[\lambda]\}

\displaystyle=-\int(2\lambda\mu^{\prime}\phi_{1}+3\mu\lambda\phi_{2}^{\prime})\approx 0

\displaystyle\{\phi_{2}[\mu],\phi_{2}[\lambda]\}

\displaystyle=\int(\mu\lambda^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime}\lambda)\phi_{2}\approx 0

(6.50)

Hence,
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
are each first-class constraints. This also implies that no new constraints arise as follows. We compute

\displaystyle\{\phi_{1}[\lambda],H_{T}\}

\displaystyle=\{\phi_{1}[\lambda],\phi_{1}[\mu_{1}]\}+\{\phi_{1}[\lambda],\phi_{2}[\mu_{2}]\}\approx 0

\displaystyle\{\phi_{2}[\lambda],H_{T}\}

\displaystyle=\{\phi_{2}[\lambda],\phi_{1}[\mu_{1}]\}+\{\phi_{2}[\lambda],\phi_{2}[\mu_{2}]\}\approx 0

(6.51)

where we used (
6.49
) and (
6.4
).

Finally we compute the gauge transformations generated by the constraints
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
. If
\phi_{i}
is a first-class constraint, a gauge transformation of an arbitrary dynamical variable
F
generated by
\phi_{i}
is computed as

\displaystyle\delta_{i}F=\{F,\phi_{i}[\xi_{i}]\}

(6.52)

It is enough to compute the gauge transformation of the basic fields
D
and
X
.
\phi_{1}
generates the following gauge transformations

\displaystyle\delta_{1}D=\xi_{1}X\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \delta_{1}X=0

(6.53)

For
X
in the isotropy algebra this would describe motion transverse to orbits. It is interesting to obtain the transverse motion as a gauge transformation rather from time evolution. In fact, there is no time evolution as we discuss below.

And finally the main gauge transformations we are interested in are generated by the diff-Gauss law
\phi_{2}
:

\displaystyle\delta_{2}D=\xi_{2}D^{\prime}+2\xi^{\prime}_{2}D+q\xi_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \delta_{2}X=\xi_{2}X^{\prime}-\xi_{2}^{\prime}X

(6.54)

This is simply invariance under the Lie-derivatives or Virasoro adjoint and coadjoint transformations.

The trouble here is that the first constraint
\phi_{1}=0
implies the vanishing of the diff momentum
X
, freezing the dynamics, i.e. trivializing the theory.
This suggests that we need to look for alternative expressions quadratic in the diff momentum, whose PB with the diff-Gauss law does not yield new constraints, yet whose vanishing does not imply a trivial theory, i.e.
X\neq 0
. In the next section we are going to introduce a candidate expression.

6.5
Alternative Hamiltonian

In this section, we are going to introduce an alternative kinetic term that transforms nicely under the gauge transformations generated by the diff-Gauss law.

Consider the following expression

\displaystyle T=\frac{1}{2}DX^{2}-\frac{q}{4}X^{\prime 2}+\frac{q}{2}XX^{\prime\prime}

(6.55)

where prime denotes spatial derivative.
It is a kinetic term because it is quadratic in the diff momentum
X
.
The corresponding smeared out expression satisfies the PBs

\displaystyle\{D,T[\mu]\}=\mu DX+\frac{q}{2}(\mu^{\prime\prime}X+3\mu^{\prime}X^{\prime}+3\mu X^{\prime\prime})

(6.56)

and

\displaystyle\{X,T[\mu]\}=-\frac{1}{2}\mu X^{2}

(6.57)

Using these it is straightforward to show that

\displaystyle\{T[\mu],T[\lambda]\}=0

(6.58)

This result holds identically not just weakly.

T
is related to the diff-Gauss law operator
G=XD^{\prime}+2X^{\prime}D+qX^{\prime\prime\prime}

as

\displaystyle T^{\prime}=XG

(6.59)

Therefore, a theory consisting of a single constraint
T=0
implies the diff-Gauss law for a nontrivial theory, i.e.
X\neq 0
. Conversely, according to (
6.59
) the diff-Gauss law constraint
G=0
implies that the kinetic term is space-independent i.e.
T=A(t)
for some function
A
.
Note that vanishing of
T
implies

\displaystyle\frac{D}{q}=\left(-\frac{X^{\prime}}{X}\right)^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}\left(-\frac{X^{\prime}}{X}\right)^{2}

(6.60)

If we define a function
f(t,x)
by

\displaystyle\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{f^{\prime}}=-\frac{X^{\prime}}{X}

(6.61)

then
T=0
is solved by

\displaystyle D(t,x)=q\,(S_{x}f)(t,x)

(6.62)

where
S_{x}
is the Schwarzian derivative with respect to the variable
x
.

Recall that in Section
5.3.2
, equation (
6.62
) was proposed as defining the diff-Wilson line
2
2

2

We will reexamine the diff-Wilson line and loop in Section
7.2
.
, and together with the ansatz

\displaystyle X(t,x)=\frac{P(t)}{f^{\prime}(t,x)}

(6.63)

for the diff momentum, the diff-Gauss law was solved. Moreover, the diff momentum given by (
6.63
) automatically satisfies the condition (
6.61
). This raises the question of whether we can apply finite reduction method to the alternative theory defined by the single constraint
T\approx 0
. The answer, however, turns out to be negative as follows. The position variable associated with the corresponding finite theory is defined as

\displaystyle Q(t)=f(t,2\pi)

(6.64)

and the momentum variable is read from (
6.63
) as

\displaystyle P(t)=X(t,x)f^{\prime}(t,x)\ \ \ \forall x

(6.65)

The trouble is that there is no natural way to couple
Q(t)
and
P(t)
given by these equations as
f(t,2\pi)
and
f^{\prime}(t,2\pi)=\partial_{x}f(t,2\pi)
are independent variables upon finite reduction.

Now let us investigate the transformation properties of
T
.
It is straightforward to compute

\displaystyle[T,G[\xi]]=-\xi T^{\prime}

(6.66)

so that
T
is a spatial scalar.

Let us consider the case
T\neq 0
, and get classical solutions for the Hamiltonian (
6.55
). First, consider a simpler theory defined by the first term of
T
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=\frac{1}{2}DX^{2}

(6.67)

Assuming standard symplectic structure Hamilton’s equations yield

\displaystyle\dot{D}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta H}{\delta X}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{H}}{\partial X}=DX

(6.68)

\displaystyle\dot{X}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\delta H}{\delta D}=-\frac{1}{2}X^{2}

(6.69)

Using the first equation we can obtain the Lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=X\dot{D}-\mathcal{H}=\frac{\dot{D}^{2}}{2D}

(6.70)

Equation (
6.69
) is solved by

\displaystyle X=\frac{2}{t+A(x)}

(6.71)

for an arbitrary function
A
of
x
.
Inserting this back into (
6.68
) we get

\displaystyle D=(t+A(x))^{2}B(x)

(6.72)

where
B
is another arbitrary function of
x
.

Now, let us analyze the full theory given by
T
. The Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=\frac{1}{2}DX^{2}+\frac{q}{4}(2XX^{\prime\prime}-X^{\prime 2})

(6.73)

leads to the equations

\displaystyle\dot{D}

\displaystyle=DX+\frac{3q}{2}X^{\prime\prime}

(6.74a)

\displaystyle\dot{X}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}X^{2}

(6.74b)

Hence, the momentum equation is unchanged, so is the momentum solution,

\displaystyle X=\frac{2}{t+A(x)}

(6.75)

The diff field solution, on the other hand, is modified to

\displaystyle D=(t+A(x))^{2}\left(B(x)-3q\left(\frac{A^{\prime}(x)^{2}}{2(t+A(x))^{4}}-\frac{A^{\prime\prime}(x)}{3(t+A(x))^{3}}\right)\right)

(6.76)

There does not seem to be an obvious way to get the Lagrangian in local form from the Hamiltonian, as (
6.74a
) can not be inverted for
X
in terms of
\dot{D}
in any obvious way.

6.6
On Possible Routes to Fix Transverse Formalism

6.6.1
Review and Summary of Problems

Throughout the analysis up to this point we tried to make apparent inconsistencies of the tranverse formalism. Let us review the transverse formalism to see the problems and their relation better. We started transverse formalism by lifting the transformation (
4.38
)

\displaystyle\delta D=\xi D^{\prime}+2\xi^{\prime}D+q\xi^{\prime\prime\prime}

(6.77)

of a Virasoro coadjoint element to higher dimensions as the Lie derivative (
4.40
)

\displaystyle\delta D_{\mu\nu}=\xi^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}D_{\mu\nu}+\partial_{\mu}\xi^{\lambda}D_{\lambda\nu}+\partial_{\nu}\xi^{\lambda}D_{\mu\lambda}+q\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\lambda}\xi^{\lambda}

(6.78)

of a rank-two object
D_{\mu\nu}
. Due to the third-order central term in the Lie derivative this rank-two object is not a tensor. We recovered (
6.77
) from this higher dimensional lift as
\delta D=\delta D_{11}
under the gauge-fixing conditions

\displaystyle\xi^{0}=0=\partial_{0}\xi^{1}

(6.79)

which restricts the GCTs to spatial and time-independent coordinate transformations. Then we introduced a conjugate momentum
X^{ij0}
and obtained the diff-Gauss law constraint (
4.3.2
) that generates the spatial part
\delta D_{ij}
of
\delta D_{\mu\nu}
. Using an ansatz for the momentum we constructed the action
\propto X^{ij0}X_{ij0}
. This action has the diff-Gauss law as a constraint (
4.59
) with Lagrange multiplier
D_{0}^{\ i}
.

So far everything looks good. However, this action is not covariant. So we chose the simplest way to deal with this problem, namely we covariantized the action. This operation has two parts: indices
i,j,0\rightarrow\mu,\nu,\lambda
and derivatives
\partial\rightarrow\nabla
. The first part changed the characteristics of the theory :
D_{00},D_{0i}
components which did not project on the coadjoint orbits, and
D_{ij}
component which is the field theory lift of the coadjoint element now became the same field
D_{\mu\nu}
.
D_{0\mu}
is now dynamical so the diff-Gauss law is no more a constraint.

The second part, namely replacing partial derivatives with covariant derivatives, is only meaningful for a tensor. Moreover, for a nontensor even the first part is problematic as contraction of covariant indices does not yield a tensor if the covariant indices are attached to a nontensor. So we required the diff field to be a tensor.

Then comes the question : What was the point of the first half of the transverse formalism? Why did we introduce
\delta D_{\mu\nu}
that is nontensorial? Why did we introduce a diff-Gauss law constraint that generates it, if it were going to be lost upon covariantization? And there is also the hidden inconsistency we discovered in Section
6.3
, that even before covariantization the diff-Gauss law is not first-class, so it does not generate the gauge transformation
\delta D_{ij}
, so it is not a ”Gauss” law.

After this cruel critique of all the work we have laid down so far let us investigate possible solution routes that would save us from the trouble of altogether abandoning the transverse formalism. We are going to investigate entirely different approaches to come up with a diff field theory in the next chapter.

From now on, we treat (
6.78
)
as the coordinate transformation of the diff field, so the diff field is not a tensor, and we abandon covariantization.

6.6.2
Spatial Covariance of Transverse Theory

For now let us give up our hopes on finding a full diffeomorphism invariant action. Do we, at least, have spatial diffeomorphism invariance? We do have, but it requires reviving a hidden quantity, the metric determinant. Recall the Gauss variation (
4.3.2
) of the diff momentum
X^{ij}
. This calculation showed that the diff momentum is a spatial rank-two tensor density of weight one.

A tensor density of weight
w
multiplied by
g^{w/2}
is a tensor, where
g
is the metric determinant. Hence, we can form a true rank-two spatial tensor out of
X^{lm}
as

\displaystyle\tilde{X}^{lm}=\sqrt{h}X^{lm}

(6.80)

where
h
is the determinant of the spatial hypersurface of the spacetime. In flat space
h=1
and there seems to be no difference between
\tilde{X}
and
X
. However, this is an illusion and another example of the problem of symmetric criticality. In order to show diffeomorphism invariance we need to compute the Lie variation of the Lagrangian. Although
\sqrt{h}
is one, we can evaluate it before or after the variation. The results are not the same. In fact, using the Lie derivative of
X^{ij}
obtained from (
4.3.2
) we can show that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}=\frac{1}{2}X^{ij}X_{ij}

(6.81)

is not a spatial scalar. However, the Lagrangian density

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\tilde{X}^{ij}\tilde{X}_{ij}

(6.82)

leads to a spatial diffeomorphism invariant action. We have
\mathcal{L}_{2}=\mathcal{L}_{1}
for
h=1
. This is an example of the difficulty of recovering a covariant theory from its background evaluated content.

Hence, although the diff field is not a tensor, the diff Lagrangian density before covariantization in the form

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{L}}=\frac{1}{2}\tilde{X}^{ij}\tilde{X}_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}hX^{ij}X_{ij}

(6.83)

is a spatial scalar. And the spatially invariant action reads

\displaystyle S=\int dt\int d^{3}x\,h^{3/2}\,X^{ij}X_{ij}

(6.84)

Note that we do not know the expression for
X^{ij}
in the action above when the metric is not flat. That is, when the gauge conditions
\xi^{0}=\partial_{0}\xi^{i}
are turned off, we do not know what happens to
X^{ij}
components. We will introduce a candidate below in Section
6.6.5
.

6.6.3
Spatially Covariant Extension of Diff Field in 2D

Since we abandoned covariantization and confined ourselves to spatial diffeomorphism invariance, is everything okay now? Not really.
We also formed interactions of diff field with other fields and for those interaction terms to make sense diff field should be a tensor. For instance, point particle and spinor couplings suggested that diff field is a perturbation to the spacetime metric, which we used to extend the nonabelian Born-Infeld action. However, a nontensorial perturbation to the metric will destroy all the good things it brought with general relativity.

How can we recover the spatial diffeomorphism invariance of diff field itself? A quick solution that comes to mind is to complement diff field with a correction that turns it into a tensor, just as diff field is complemented with a correction involving the gauge field in (
4.105
) to yield a gauge invariant object.

In particular, diff field can be complemented with a correction involving the spacetime metric or Levi-Civita connection to yield a tensor. In fact, in 1D the solution of this problem is simple. As discussed in Section
2.4.4
one may subtract a multiple of
\Sigma=\Gamma^{\prime}-\Gamma^{2}/2
from diff field
D
to obtain a rank-two tensor. This result, however, does not nicely extend to higher dimensions. In Section
2.4.5
we searched for higher dimensional extensions
\Sigma_{\mu\nu}
of
\Sigma
that transform as (
4.40
), but could not find a consistent one.

However, for spatial diffeomorphism invariance we may not need such a higher dimensional extension of
\Sigma_{\mu\nu}
. Namely, instead of finding a
\Sigma_{\mu\nu}
that transforms as (
4.40
), it is sufficient to find a
\Sigma_{\mu\nu}
whose
(11)
component reduces to a multiple of
\Sigma=\Gamma^{\prime}-\Gamma^{2}/2
. In this reduction the gauge-fixing conditions (
6.79
)
should be used. Recall the object

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\mu\nu}\equiv a\partial_{\lambda}\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}+b\partial_{\mu}\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\lambda\nu}+c\partial_{\nu}\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\lambda}+d\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\sigma}_{\sigma\lambda}+e\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\sigma}\Gamma^{\sigma}_{\nu\lambda}+f\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\lambda}\Gamma^{\sigma}_{\nu\sigma}

(6.85)

subjected to the condition

\displaystyle q\equiv a+b+c=-2(d+e+f)

(6.86)

Then the 1D reduction of
\Sigma_{\mu\nu}
yields a coadjoint element
\Sigma
with central charge
q
. Now, let us rewrite the result we found for the Lie variation of
\Sigma_{\mu\nu}
in (
6.85
):

\displaystyle\delta\Sigma_{\mu\nu}=\xi^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}\Sigma_{\mu\nu}+\partial_{\mu}\xi^{\lambda}\Sigma_{\lambda\nu}+\partial_{\nu}\xi^{\lambda}\Sigma_{\mu\lambda}+q\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\lambda}\xi^{\lambda}+\Delta_{\mu\nu}

(6.87)

where

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=(-a+d)\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\sigma}\xi^{\sigma}+(a+e)(\Gamma^{\rho}_{\sigma\nu}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\rho}\xi^{\sigma}+\Gamma^{\rho}_{\sigma\mu}\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\nu}\xi^{\sigma})

\displaystyle+f(\Gamma^{\rho}_{\rho\nu}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\sigma}\xi^{\sigma}+\Gamma^{\rho}_{\rho\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\sigma}\xi^{\sigma})+(b+c+d)\Gamma^{\rho}_{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\xi^{\sigma}

(6.88)

This expression is too complicated to deal with, so let us look at simpler subcases first.
Not every subcase works. For instance, the subcase (
2.119
) noticed for its simplicity in Section
2.4.5
does not work since the gauge conditions (
6.79
)
and the condition (
6.86
) are not compatible in that case as can be seen after a straightforward calculation.

Here is, a working subcase :
a=2,e=-1
. For this choice we get

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\mu\nu}\equiv 2\partial_{\lambda}\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}-\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\sigma}\Gamma^{\sigma}_{\nu\lambda}=\Sigma_{\nu\mu}

(6.89)

and the difference from the desired variation reduces to

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-2\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\sigma}\xi^{\sigma}+\Gamma^{\rho}_{\sigma\nu}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\rho}\xi^{\sigma}+\Gamma^{\rho}_{\sigma\mu}\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\nu}\xi^{\sigma}

(6.90)

Now, following the analysis in Section
4.3.1
, conditions (
6.79
) imply

\displaystyle\delta\Sigma_{11}

\displaystyle=\xi^{1}\partial_{1}\Sigma_{11}+2\partial_{1}\xi^{1}\Sigma_{11}+2\partial_{1}^{3}\xi^{1}+\Delta_{11}

(6.91a)

\displaystyle\delta\Sigma_{01}

\displaystyle=\xi^{1}\partial_{1}\Sigma_{01}+\Sigma_{01}\partial_{1}\xi^{1}+\Delta_{01}

(6.91b)

\displaystyle\delta\Sigma_{00}

\displaystyle=\xi^{1}\partial_{1}\Sigma_{00}+\Delta_{00}

(6.91c)

where the central charge of (
6.89
) is two. Our goal is to find the conditions that make
\Delta_{\mu\nu}=0
without imposing any additional conditions on
\xi^{\mu}
. Remarkably for our choice (
6.89
),
\Delta_{11}
automatically vanishes under (
6.79
). Vanishing of
\Delta_{01}
requires
\Gamma_{01}^{1}=0
and finally vanishing of
\Delta_{00}
requires
\Gamma^{1}_{00}=0
. So we can state the analog of (
4.46
) as

\displaystyle\xi^{0}

\displaystyle=0=\partial_{0}\xi^{1}\hskip 14.45377pt\text{and}\hskip 14.45377pt\Gamma^{1}_{01}=0=\Gamma^{1}_{00}

(6.92a)

\displaystyle\delta\Sigma_{11}

\displaystyle=\xi^{1}\partial_{1}\Sigma_{11}+2\partial_{1}\xi^{1}\Sigma_{11}+2\partial_{1}^{3}\xi^{1}

(6.92b)

\displaystyle\delta\Sigma_{01}

\displaystyle=\xi^{1}\partial_{1}\Sigma_{01}+\Sigma_{01}\partial_{1}\xi^{1}

(6.92c)

\displaystyle\delta\Sigma_{00}

\displaystyle=\xi^{1}\partial_{1}\Sigma_{00}

(6.92d)

with

\displaystyle\Sigma_{11}

\displaystyle=2\partial_{0}\Gamma^{0}_{11}+2\partial_{1}\Gamma^{1}_{11}-(\Gamma^{1}_{11})^{2}-(\Gamma^{0}_{01})^{2}

(6.93a)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{01}

\displaystyle=2\partial_{0}\Gamma^{0}_{01}-\Gamma^{0}_{00}\Gamma^{0}_{01}

(6.93b)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{00}

\displaystyle=2\partial_{0}\Gamma^{0}_{00}-(\Gamma^{0}_{00})^{2}

(6.93c)

Can we further restrict these expressions analogous to the full-temporal gauge conditions (
4.47
)? If we take, in addition,
\Gamma^{0}_{00}=0=\Gamma^{0}_{01}
we get
\Sigma_{00}=0=\Sigma_{01}
which automatically yields the consistency conditions
\delta\Sigma_{00}=0=\delta\Sigma_{01}
. These imply
\Sigma_{11}=2\partial_{0}\Gamma^{0}_{11}+2\partial_{1}\Gamma^{1}_{11}-(\Gamma^{1}_{11})^{2}
. Now, to match the 1D reduction of
\Sigma_{11}
with the Virasoro coadjoint element
2\Gamma^{\prime}-\Gamma^{2}
, we need the additional condition
\partial_{0}\Gamma^{0}_{11}=0
Hence, the analog of (
4.47
) becomes

\displaystyle\xi^{0}

\displaystyle=0=\partial_{0}\xi^{1}\hskip 14.45377pt\text{and}\hskip 14.45377pt\Gamma^{1}_{01}=0=\Gamma^{1}_{00}=\Gamma^{0}_{00}=\Gamma^{0}_{01}=\partial_{0}\Gamma^{0}_{11}

(6.94a)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{11}

\displaystyle=2\partial_{1}\Gamma^{1}_{11}-(\Gamma^{1}_{11})^{2}

(6.94b)

\displaystyle\delta\Sigma_{11}

\displaystyle=\xi^{1}\partial_{1}\Sigma_{11}+2\partial_{1}\xi^{1}\Sigma_{11}+2\partial_{1}^{3}\xi^{1}

(6.94c)

\displaystyle\delta\Sigma_{01}

\displaystyle=0=\Sigma_{01}

(6.94d)

\displaystyle\delta\Sigma_{00}

\displaystyle=0=\Sigma_{00}

(6.94e)

With the set of conditions (
6.92
) or (
6.94
), we obtain a spatial tensor in 2D from the diff field by

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}\equiv D_{\mu\nu}-\frac{q}{2}\Sigma_{\mu\nu}

(6.95)

For (
6.92
), (
6.93
) we have

\displaystyle\xi^{0}

\displaystyle=0=\partial_{0}\xi^{1}\hskip 14.45377pt\text{and}\hskip 14.45377pt\Gamma^{1}_{01}=0=\Gamma^{1}_{00}

(6.96a)

\displaystyle\delta T_{11}

\displaystyle=\xi^{1}\partial_{1}T_{11}+2\partial_{1}\xi^{1}T_{11}

(6.96b)

\displaystyle\delta T_{01}

\displaystyle=\xi^{1}\partial_{1}T_{01}+T_{01}\partial_{1}\xi^{1}

(6.96c)

\displaystyle\delta T_{00}

\displaystyle=\xi^{1}\partial_{1}T_{00}

(6.96d)

and for (
6.94
) we have

\displaystyle\xi^{0}

\displaystyle=0=\partial_{0}\xi^{1}\hskip 14.45377pt\text{and}\hskip 14.45377pt\Gamma^{1}_{01}=0=\Gamma^{1}_{00}=\Gamma^{0}_{00}=\Gamma^{0}_{01}=\partial_{0}\Gamma^{0}_{11}

(6.97a)

\displaystyle T_{11}

\displaystyle=D_{11}-\frac{q}{2}\Big(2\partial_{1}\Gamma^{1}_{11}-(\Gamma^{1}_{11})^{2}\Big)

(6.97b)

\displaystyle\delta T_{11}

\displaystyle=\xi^{1}\partial_{1}T_{11}+2\partial_{1}\xi^{1}T_{11}

(6.97c)

\displaystyle\delta T_{01}

\displaystyle=0=T_{01}

(6.97d)

\displaystyle\delta T_{00}

\displaystyle=0=T_{00}

(6.97e)

Now, it is tempting to think that this result would nicely extend to higher dimensions, but it does not. The reason is simple : for (
6.79
) it is easy to compute spatial part of
\Delta_{\mu\nu}
in higher than two dimensions

\displaystyle\Delta_{ij}

\displaystyle=-2\Gamma^{k}_{ij}\partial_{k}\partial_{m}\xi^{m}+\Gamma^{k}_{mj}\partial_{i}\partial_{k}\xi^{m}+\Gamma^{k}_{mi}\partial_{k}\partial_{j}\xi^{m}

\displaystyle=\partial_{k}\partial_{n}\xi^{m}(-2\Gamma^{k}_{ij}\delta_{m}^{n}+\Gamma^{k}_{mj}\delta_{i}^{n}+\Gamma^{k}_{mi}\delta_{j}^{n})

(6.98)

Only in the 2D case we automatically obtain
\Delta_{ij}=0
since all the spatial indices above reduce to
1
. In higher dimensional case there is no obvious way to solve
\Delta_{ij}=0
.

Now this calculation shows that the transverse theory that we took is spatially diffeomorphism invariant, but the diff field itself admits a correction to become a spatial tensor only in 2D spacetime. Extension of this result to higher dimensions requires the modification of the very first step of the transverse formalism. Namely, the higher dimensional lift (
4.40
) of the coadjoint transformation

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}D_{\mu\nu}=(\partial_{\mu}\xi^{\lambda})D_{\lambda\nu}+(\partial_{\nu}\xi^{\lambda})D_{\mu\lambda}+\xi^{\lambda}(\partial_{\lambda}D_{\mu\nu})+q\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\lambda}\xi^{\lambda}

(6.99)

We will investigate this in the next section.

6.6.4
Full Covariance Recovered in Interactions

The analysis in the previous section suggests that the higher dimensional lift (
6.99
) of the coadjoint transformation
is too strict for the diff field to be complemented with a correction making it into a spatial tensor. Although (
6.99
) is the most straightforward lift,
what if we change it to include second-order center terms as well? In other words, we can introduce the modified Lie derivative

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}D_{\mu\nu}=(\partial_{\mu}\xi^{\lambda})D_{\lambda\nu}+(\partial_{\nu}\xi^{\lambda})D_{\mu\lambda}+\xi^{\lambda}(\partial_{\lambda}D_{\mu\nu})+q\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\lambda}\xi^{\lambda}+(q/2)\Delta_{\mu\nu}

(6.100)

with

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-2\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\sigma}\xi^{\sigma}+\Gamma^{\rho}_{\sigma\nu}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\rho}\xi^{\sigma}+\Gamma^{\rho}_{\sigma\mu}\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\nu}\xi^{\sigma}

(6.101)

The modified Lie derivative (
6.100
) reduces to

\displaystyle\delta D=\xi D^{\prime}+2\xi^{\prime}D+q\xi^{\prime\prime\prime}

(6.102)

in 1D. In 2D curved spacetimes, and in flat spacetimes of any dimensions, it automatically yields
\delta D=\delta D_{ij}
.

Moreover, the object

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}\equiv D_{\mu\nu}-\frac{q}{2}\Sigma_{\mu\nu}

(6.103)

is now a spacetime tensor in any dimensions; not just a spatial tensor. Hence, with this new proposal we can recover full diffeomorphism invariance at least in interactions. We will investigate the transverse action obtained from (
6.100
) in the next section.

Now we apply the same procedure as in Section (
4.3.1
) to obtain the spatial reduction of the field components
3
3

3

Linear central extension is ignored for simplicity, though its presence does not affect the arguments below.
. In addition to the conditions

\displaystyle\xi^{0}=0=\partial_{0}\xi^{k}

(6.104)

we need
\Gamma^{m}_{k0}=0
to make
D_{0i}
a spatial covariant vector and
\Gamma^{m}_{00}=0
to make
D_{00}
a spatial scalar. Hence the analog of (
4.46
) is given by

\displaystyle\xi^{0}

\displaystyle=0=\partial_{0}\xi^{k}\ \ ,\ \ \Gamma^{m}_{k0}=0=\Gamma^{m}_{00}

(6.105)

\displaystyle\delta D_{ij}

\displaystyle=\xi^{k}\partial_{k}D_{ij}+\partial_{i}\xi^{k}D_{kj}+\partial_{j}\xi^{k}D_{ik}+q\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\xi^{k}

\displaystyle+(q/2)(-2\Gamma^{m}_{ij}\partial_{m}\partial_{k}\xi^{k}+\Gamma^{m}_{kj}\partial_{i}\partial_{m}\xi^{k}+\Gamma^{m}_{ki}\partial_{j}\partial_{m}\xi^{k})

(6.106)

\displaystyle\delta D_{0i}

\displaystyle=\xi^{k}\partial_{k}D_{0i}+\partial_{i}\xi^{k}D_{0k}

(6.107)

\displaystyle\delta D_{00}

\displaystyle=\xi^{k}\partial_{k}D_{00}

(6.108)

Finally, we can introduce the analog of the full temporal gauge set (
4.47
)

\displaystyle\xi^{0}

\displaystyle=0=\partial_{0}\xi^{k}\ \ ,\ \ \Gamma^{m}_{k0}=0=\Gamma^{m}_{00}

(6.109)

\displaystyle\delta D_{ij}

\displaystyle=\xi^{k}\partial_{k}D_{ij}+\partial_{i}\xi^{k}D_{kj}+\partial_{j}\xi^{k}D_{ik}+q\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\xi^{k}

\displaystyle+(q/2)(-2\Gamma^{m}_{ij}\partial_{m}\partial_{k}\xi^{k}+\Gamma^{m}_{kj}\partial_{i}\partial_{m}\xi^{k}+\Gamma^{m}_{ki}\partial_{j}\partial_{m}\xi^{k})

(6.110)

\displaystyle\delta D_{0i}

\displaystyle=0=D_{0i}

(6.111)

\displaystyle\delta D_{00}

\displaystyle=0=D_{00}

(6.112)

The change in
\delta_{\xi}D_{\mu\nu}
will change the expression of the diff-Gauss law and as a result the diff momentum.

6.6.5
Modified Transverse Action

We introduce a conjugate momentum
X^{ij}
to
D_{ij}
and form the diff-Gauss law. We can use the shortcut prescription (
4.55
) to get the modified diff-Gauss law

\displaystyle G^{0}_{k}

\displaystyle=X^{ij}\partial_{k}D_{ij}+\partial_{i}X^{ij}D_{kj}+\partial_{j}X^{ij}D_{ik}+q\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\partial_{k}X^{ij}

\displaystyle+(q/2)(-2\Gamma^{m}_{ij}\partial_{m}\partial_{k}X^{ij}+\Gamma^{m}_{kj}\partial_{i}\partial_{m}X^{ij}+\Gamma^{m}_{ki}\partial_{j}\partial_{m}X^{ij})

(6.113)

Since the
D
dependent terms of the diff-Gauss law are the same, the Lie derivative (
4.3.2
) of
X^{ij}
is unchanged. Hence,
X^{ij}
is still a spatial tensor density and

\displaystyle\tilde{X}^{ij}=\sqrt{h}X^{ij}

(6.114)

is a rank-two spatial tensor.
We apply the prescription (
4.58
) to find the diff-Lagrangian involving the diff momentum. We introduce the same ansatz (
4.64
) for the diff momentum. We insert this ansatz into the action and recompute the momentum. The result can be quickly found by (
4.69
)
which yields

\displaystyle X_{ij0}

\displaystyle=\partial_{0}D_{ij}+D_{0}^{\ k}\partial_{k}D_{ij}+\partial_{i}D_{0}^{\ k}D_{kj}+\partial_{j}D_{0}^{\ k}D_{ik}+q\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\partial_{k}D_{0}^{\ k}

\displaystyle+(q/2)(-2\Gamma^{m}_{ij}\partial_{m}\partial_{k}D_{0}^{\ k}+\Gamma^{m}_{kj}\partial_{i}\partial_{m}D_{0}^{\ k}+\Gamma^{m}_{ki}\partial_{j}\partial_{m}D_{0}^{\ k})

(6.115)

Finally, we get a spatially covariant Lagrangian density

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{2}hX_{ij0}X^{ij0}

(6.116)

Could this be the most general spatially covariant action (
6.84
) that we were looking for? It may be, but we can not say for sure (again due to the problem of symmetric criticality). In other words, there would be other possibilities that would yield back the same flat spacetime content. However, at least we have a candidate at hand. Moreover, we obtained a full diffeomorphism invariant extension of the diff field itself to be used in interactions, and consistency requires the modified diff variation (
6.100
), thereby the modified action.

We finalize our study of transverse formalism at this point. For future research on transverse theory and recovering full diffeomorphism invariance of the diff Lagrangian we can suggest three directions. The first is a careful investigation of ADM decomposition of general relativity with the intention of reversing it. Namely, one needs structures analogous to the extrinsic curvature and lapse and shift functions to lift the hypersurface theory to the full spacetime theory.
The second is examining the covariant proposal made in
[
lanophd
]
, and how it may be related to the tranverse formalism or how it can be modified to be compatible with the transverse formalism.
Finally, the third is application of projective connections in the transverse formalism context. This may provide a mechanism to recover the spacetime diff field theory from the theory on the spatial hypersurface.
In the next chapter we will investigate alternative geometric/topological approaches for the diff field including the projective connection proposal.

Chapter 7
ALTERNATIVE IDEAS AND FUTURE DIRECTIONS

7.1
Euler-Poincare Theory of Diff Field

Here we are going to introduce a canonical formalism called the Euler-Poincare (EP) formalism associated with Lie algebras. The main references for this section are
[
marsdenratiu
]
,
[
marsdenratiupaper
]
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie algebra and let
\xi:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}
,
\xi(t)\in\mathfrak{g}
. The Lagrangian is taken to be a function of
\xi
and the variations to be considered are of the form

\displaystyle\delta_{\eta}\xi=\dot{\eta}+[\eta,\xi]=\dot{\eta}+\text{ad}_{\eta}\xi

(7.1)

Then the principle of least action

\displaystyle 0=\delta S=\delta\int dt\ L

(7.2)

leads to the so called Euler-Poincare (EP) equations

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\delta L}{\delta\xi}=\text{ad}^{*}_{\xi}\frac{\delta L}{\delta\xi}

(7.3)

Let us prove this statement

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\delta\int dt\ L

\displaystyle=\int dt\ \frac{\delta L}{\delta\xi}\delta\xi

\displaystyle=\int dt\ \frac{\delta L}{\delta\xi}(\dot{\eta}+\text{ad}_{\eta}\xi)

\displaystyle=\int dt\ \frac{\delta L}{\delta\xi}(\dot{\eta}-\text{ad}_{\xi}\eta)

\displaystyle=\int dt\ \left(-\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\delta L}{\delta\xi}\right)\eta-\int dt\ \frac{\delta L}{\delta\xi}\text{ad}_{\xi}\eta

(7.4)

Notice that
\delta L/\delta\xi
is a coadjoint vector so the integrand is the pairing between an adjoint and a coadjoint element. Then we can use invariance of the pairing to rewrite the second term

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int dt\ \left(-\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\delta L}{\delta\xi}\right)\eta+\int dt\ \text{ad}^{*}_{\xi}\left(\frac{\delta L}{\delta\xi}\right)\eta

\displaystyle=\int dt\ \left(-\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\delta L}{\delta\xi}+\text{ad}^{*}_{\xi}\frac{\delta L}{\delta\xi}\right)\eta

(7.5)

For arbitrary
\eta
that vanishes at the end points we recover EP equations (
7.3
).

Now let us consider the Virasoro algebra and the diff field. What Lagrangian will we pick for the diff theory? Diff field is a Virasoro coadjoint element, so we need to form its pairing with an adjoint vector, as in the proof above. Which adjoint vector will we take? A clue comes from the rigid body theory where the conjugate momentum is obtained by varying the Lagrangian with respect to the main variable. Thus we may contract the diff field with its momentum
X
, which behaves in 1D as an adjoint vector, (
4.57
). Hence, we pick

\displaystyle L=\left<D|X\right>

(7.6)

Then the EP equation (
7.3
) reads

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\delta L}{\delta X}=\text{ad}^{*}_{X}\frac{\delta L}{\delta X}

(7.7)

which yields

\displaystyle\dot{D}=\text{ad}^{*}_{X}D=XD^{\prime}+2X^{\prime}D+qX^{\prime\prime\prime}

(7.8)

How to interpret (
7.8
)? Let us take
X
to be in the isotropy algebra for
D
so that

\displaystyle 0=\text{ad}^{*}_{X}D=XD^{\prime}+2X^{\prime}D+qX^{\prime\prime\prime}

(7.9)

This implies
\dot{D}=0
. This is a strange situation from the perspective of transverse formalism. When the diff momentum is taken in isotropy algebra so that it represents an infinitesimal motion that is transverse to the orbit, diff field freezes. Hence, dynamics described by the theory should be completely on the orbit. However, on the orbit
D
is fixed by diffeomorphisms. Therefore such a theory describes evolution between distinct but diffeomorphic field configurations for
D
. So this is a theory on the coadjoint orbit rather than transverse to it. A coadjoint transformation is not obtained as a gauge transformation (generated by the diff-Gauss law). Rather, it corresponds to the dynamical evolution.

If we take
X=D
as functions, the field equation (
7.8
) becomes

\displaystyle\dot{D}-3DD^{\prime}-qD^{\prime\prime\prime}=0

(7.10)

Rescaling time
\tau(t)=-t/2
, we reach KdV equation for
q=1/2

\displaystyle D_{\tau}+6DD_{\sigma}+D_{\sigma\sigma\sigma}=0

(7.11)

Finally, if instead of
X
we paired
D
with
N
, the Lagrange multiplier of the diff-Gauss law in the transverse formalism, then the field equation would read

\displaystyle\dot{D}=ND^{\prime}+2N^{\prime}D+qN^{\prime\prime\prime}

(7.12)

which corresponds to vanishing of diff momentum in the transverse theory. This is compatible with the argument above that the EP theory of diff field does not provide dynamics transverse to orbits.

7.2
Diff-Wilson Loop

Rajeev
[
rajeev88
]
discusses the finite reduction of Yang-Mills (YM) theory on a cylinder (
\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}
) using Wilson loop methods. This has been summarized in Section
5.2
. In particular, the Wilson line is used to solve the Gauss law (equation (
5.10
)).

This method has been imitated in the case of Virasoro algebra in references
[
LR95
]
and
[
hendersonrajeev
]
in a different way. The former is reviewed in Section
5.3
. Since diff field is a Virasoro coadjoint element, the Wilson loop associated with Virasoro coadjoint representation will be called the diff-Wilson loop. In this section we are going to examine diff-Wilson loop more carefully.

In the Virasoro case things are more complicated due to the higher order local transformation. Hence, we need to understand the properties of the Wilson loop in a form that is suitable for generalization to a field theory related to the Virasoro algebra. The main references for this section are
[
rajeev88
]
,
[
NairQFT
]
,
[
LR95
]
and
[
hendersonrajeev
]
.

7.2.1
Understanding Wilson Loop

In gauge theories, the configuration space is the space of gauge fields
\mathcal{A}
modulo the space of gauge transformations
\mathcal{G}
. However, the quotient is not well-defined for the full gauge group
\mathcal{G}
because, in general, it acts on
\mathcal{A}
with fixed points, i.e. there are nonidentity gauge transformations that fix an arbitrary gauge field. Only the subgroup
\mathcal{G}_{0}\subset\mathcal{G}
of gauge transformations that are equal to identity at
x=0
acts without fixed points. So the configuration space is taken as
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{G}_{0}
which is a smooth manifold and the wavefunctions of the gauge theory can be viewed as functions on this space.

On circle
S^{1}
, this quotient space is finite-dimensional; the only gauge invariant observable is the Wilson loop
W[A]
around the circle
[
rajeev88
]
. To define this object we first need to solve the parallel transport equation

\displaystyle\frac{d\psi}{dx}+eA(x)\psi(x)=0

(7.13)

or

\displaystyle\nabla_{A}\psi(x)=0

(7.14)

where
\nabla_{A}
is the covariant derivative with the gauge connection
A
.

This equation is invariant under the simultaneous transformations

\displaystyle\psi(x)\mapsto\psi^{g}(x)=g(x)\psi(x)

(7.15a)

\displaystyle A(x)\mapsto A^{g}(x)=g(x)A(x)g^{-1}(x)+e^{-1}g(x)dg^{-1}(x)

(7.15b)

as follows

\displaystyle\nabla_{A^{g}}\psi^{g}

\displaystyle=(\psi^{g})^{\prime}+eA^{g}\psi^{g}

\displaystyle=g^{\prime}\psi+g\psi^{\prime}+e\left(gAg^{-1}-e^{-1}g^{\prime}g^{-1}\right)g\psi

\displaystyle=g(\psi^{\prime}+eA\psi)=0

(7.16)

Now, although
A(x)
is periodic, the solution
\psi(x)
of (
7.14
) is, in general, not periodic (so is not a well-defined function
1
1

1

See
[
NairQFT
]
pages 183-184 for path-dependence of the Wilson loop leading to the standard path-ordered exponential definition of the Wilson loop.
on
S^{1}
). Instead the solution satisfies

\displaystyle\psi(2\pi)=W[A]\psi(0)

(7.17)

where
W:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow G
is the parallel transport operator, or the Wilson loop. For
g\in\mathcal{G}_{0}
we have
g(0)=g(2\pi)=I
. Then using (
7.15a
) we get
\psi^{g}(2\pi)=\psi(2\pi)
, and
\psi^{g}(0)=\psi(0)
. Together these imply that

\displaystyle W[A^{g}]=W[A]

(7.18)

i.e. the Wilson loop is gauge-invariant.

Note that, in the notation of Section
5.2
,
\psi=S
,
e=1
and the boundary condition
S(0)=1
implies
S(2\pi)=W[A]
.

7.2.2
Virasoro Covariant Derivatives

The main reference for this section is
[
scherer88
]
.
First, we will introduce some more structure related to the Virasoro algebra.
Let
\widehat{\text{G}}
denote the central extension of the group
\text{G}=\text{Diff}(S^{1})
of orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms of
S^{1}
. Let
\Omega_{p}(S^{1})=\{\psi=\psi(\theta)(d\theta)^{p}\}
be the space of densities of weight
p\in\mathbb{R}
on
S^{1}
. These densities form a representation
R^{(p)}
of G, with the action of a diffeomorphism
f\in\text{G}
given by

\displaystyle R^{(p)}_{f}\psi=(f^{\prime})^{p}\ \psi\circ f

(7.19)

The algebra of
\widehat{\text{G}}
is given by
\widehat{\mathbf{g}}=\text{Vect}(S^{1})\oplus\mathbb{R}
, and is called the Virasoro algebra and its (regular) dual is denoted by
\widehat{\mathbf{g}}^{*}
, which can be identified with
\Omega_{2}(S^{1})\oplus\mathbb{R}
i.e. quadratic differentials together with real center.

Recall that the (active) coadjoint action of the group is given by

\displaystyle\text{Ad}^{*}_{f}(u,b)=\Big((f^{\prime})^{2}\ u\circ f+b\ S(f)\ ,\ b\Big)

(7.20)

where
(u,b)\in\widehat{\mathbf{g}}^{*}
,
f\in\text{G}
and
S(f)
is the Schwarzian of
f
.

We would like to construct covariant derivatives associated with the coadjoint element
u
.
We may, without loss of generality, consider only elements of the form
\widehat{u}=(u,1)\in\widehat{\mathbf{g}}^{*}
.
For
k\in\mathbb{N}
we may tensor
R^{((1-k)/2)}
with the coadjoint representation to get the following representation on
\widehat{\mathbf{g}}\otimes\Omega_{(1-k)/2}(S^{1})
,

\displaystyle T^{((1-k)/2)}\equiv\text{Ad}^{*}\otimes R^{((1-k)/2)}

(7.21)

The Hill operator,

\displaystyle\nabla^{(2)}_{\hat{u}}\equiv\frac{d^{2}}{d\theta^{2}}+u(\theta)

(7.22)

maps densities of weight
-1/2
to densities of weight
3/2
for each
\widehat{u}\in\widehat{\mathbf{g}}
.
It can be viewed as part of the mapping
\nabla^{(2)}
defined as

\displaystyle\nabla^{(2)}:\widehat{\mathbf{g}}\otimes\Omega_{-1/2}(S^{1})\rightarrow\Omega_{3/2}(S^{1}):\widehat{u}\otimes\psi\mapsto\nabla^{(2)}_{\widehat{u}}\psi

(7.23)

Hence, the mapping
\nabla^{(2)}
, connecting the representations
T^{(-1/2)}=\text{Ad}^{*}\otimes R^{(-1/2)}
and
R^{(3/2)}
, is a covariant differential operator. Explicitly, we have

\displaystyle\nabla^{(2)}_{\text{Ad}^{*}_{f}\widehat{u}}=(f^{\prime})^{3/2}\left(\frac{1}{f^{\prime 2}}\frac{d^{2}}{d\theta^{2}}-\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{(f^{\prime})^{3}}\frac{d}{d\theta}+u(f(\theta))\right)(f^{\prime})^{1/2}

(7.24)

In general, we have operators

\displaystyle\nabla^{(k)}_{\widehat{u}}:\Omega_{(1-k)/2}(S^{1})\rightarrow\Omega_{(1+k)/2}(S^{1})

(7.25)

which can be seen as part of the mappings

\displaystyle\nabla^{(k)}:\widehat{\mathbf{g}}\otimes\Omega_{(1-k)/2}(S^{1})\rightarrow\Omega_{(1+k)/2}(S^{1}):\widehat{u}\otimes\psi\mapsto\nabla^{(k)}_{\widehat{u}}\psi

(7.26)

For our purposes the other interesting case is the operator for
k=3
given by
2
2

2

For generic
k\in\mathbb{N}
, see
[
scherer88
]
.

\displaystyle\nabla^{(3)}_{\widehat{u}}=\frac{d^{3}}{d\theta^{3}}+4u\frac{d}{d\theta}+2u^{\prime}\ \ :\Omega_{-1}(S^{1})\rightarrow\Omega_{2}(S^{1})

(7.27)

mapping vectors to quadratic differentials. So the operator,
\nabla^{(3)}_{\widehat{u}}:\Omega_{-1}(S^{1})\rightarrow\Omega_{2}(S^{1})
, acts on a Virasoro adjoint element and yields a Virasoro coadjoint element. This is the analog of the covariant derivative acting on the gauge potential and used to build the YM action.

Note that, one can use two vector fields (adjoint vectors)
\xi,\eta\in\text{Vect}(S^{1})
to get an invariant using the pairing,

\displaystyle(\xi,\eta)\overset{u}{\rightarrow}\left<\xi,\nabla_{u}^{(3)}\eta\right>\in\mathbb{R}

(7.28)

In fact the Kirillov form
\Omega
can be rewritten in terms of
\nabla^{(3)}
as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\left<\nabla^{(3)}_{u}\eta\,,\,\xi\right>-\left<\nabla^{(3)}_{u}\xi\,,\,\eta\right>\right)

\displaystyle=\int d\theta\,u(\xi\eta^{\prime}-\xi^{\prime}\eta)+\frac{q}{2}\int d\theta\,(\xi\eta^{\prime\prime\prime}-\xi^{\prime\prime\prime}\eta)

\displaystyle=\left<(u,0)\,,\,[(\xi,0),(\eta,0)]\right>-qc(\xi,\eta)

\displaystyle=\left<(u,q)\,,\,[(\xi,0),(\eta,0)]\right>

\displaystyle=\Omega\left((u,q)\,\,[(\xi,0),(\eta,0)]\right)

(7.29)

As a final note, let us state that the solutions of
\nabla_{u}^{(3)}g=0
can be written as a product of the solutions of
\nabla_{u}^{(2)}f=0
. In other words, if
f_{1},f_{2}
are independent solutions of
\nabla_{u}^{(2)}f=0
. Then the general solution for
\nabla_{u}^{(3)}g=0
is given by
[
hendersonrajeev
]

\displaystyle g=af_{1}^{2}+bf_{1}f_{2}+cf_{2}^{2}

(7.30)

7.2.3
Wilson Loop Associated with Hill Equation

In this section we will form the Virasoro analog of the Wilson loop operator
[
hendersonrajeev
]
discussed in Section
7.2.1
.
The analogue of the gauge field space
\mathcal{A}
is given by the space of coadjoint elements (or quadratic differentials)
\mathcal{U}=\{u:S^{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\}
. The full gauge group
\mathcal{G}
is replaced by the group Diff
(S^{1})=\{f:S^{1}\rightarrow S^{1}\}
of diffeomorphisms of the circle. It acts on
\mathcal{U}
by
3
3

3

In
[
hendersonrajeev
]
adjoint and coadjoint elements include linear center as well. We take linear centers vanishing here i.e. we use Gelfand-Fuchs cocyle.

\displaystyle u(\theta)\mapsto f\circ u(\theta)=u(f(\theta))f^{\prime 2}(\theta)+qSf(\theta)

(7.31)

where
q
is the central charge of
u
.

As in the case of the gauge field, the full gauge group acts with fixed points (i.e. there are non-identity elements that fix the coadjoint element or the isotropy group is nontrivial). Analogue of the true gauge group
\mathcal{G}_{0}
is the subset Diff
{}_{0}(S^{1})\subset\text{Diff}S^{1}
containing diffeomorphisms satisfying

\displaystyle f(0)=0\ ,\ f^{\prime}(0)=1\ ,\ f^{\prime\prime}(0)=0

(7.32)

The proof for the infinitesimal diffeomorphisms is as follows
[
hendersonrajeev
]
. For infinitesimal diffeomorphisms of the form
f(\theta)=\theta-\xi(\theta)
invariance
f\circ u=u
reduces to

\displaystyle u^{\prime}\xi+2u\xi^{\prime}+q\xi^{\prime\prime\prime}=0

(7.33)

Conditions (
7.32
) translate to the conditions
\xi(0)=\xi^{\prime}(0)=0=\xi^{\prime\prime}(0)
for the generator. Inserting these in (
7.33
) and evaluating at
\theta=0
we get
\xi^{\prime\prime\prime}(0)=0
. By taking derivatives of (
7.33
), evaluating at
\theta=0
, and using the conditions obtained repeatedly, one reaches
\xi^{(n)}(0)=0
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. This implies
\xi(\theta)=0
. Therefore, the only infinitesimal diffeomorphism
f
fixing a generic element is the identity
f(\theta)=\theta
.

Assuming this result can be generalized to finite diffeomorphisms, analog of the configuration space
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{G}_{0}
of the gauge theory becomes
\mathcal{U}/\text{Diff}_{0}(S^{1})
.

Analog of the differential equation (
7.13
) defining the Wilson loop can be taken as the Hill equation associated with the coadjoint element
(u,q)

\displaystyle\nabla^{(2)}_{u}\psi\equiv\psi^{\prime\prime}+\frac{1}{2q}u\psi=0

(7.34)

Analogous to the invariance of equation (
7.13
) under the combined transformations (
7.15
) of the gauge field and the Wilson line, Hill equation (
7.34
) is invariant if
u
transforms as (
7.31
) and
\psi
transforms as a density of weight
-1/2
(equation (
7.19
) with
p=-1/2
). This is implied by (
7.24
) when we set
\nabla_{u}^{(2)}\psi=0
.

We have shown in Section
7.2.2
that Hill operator is the first nontrivial member of a sequence of covariant derivatives, and it can be shown
[
hendersonrajeev
]
that the solutions of the equation
\nabla^{(2s+1)}_{u}\psi=0
are just products of the
2s
solutions of
\nabla^{(2s)}_{u}\psi=0
, so
-1/2
densities are the analogues of the fundamental representation and the
\nabla^{(2)}_{u}
is the analog of the covariant derivative for the fundamental representation.

Just as in the case of the ordinary Wilson loop, solutions to the Hill’s equation

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime\prime}+\frac{1}{2q}u\psi=0

(7.35)

are, in general, not periodic. A basis
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
of solutions will change by a linear transformation
M[u]
as one goes from
\theta=0
to
\theta=2\pi
:

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\varphi_{1}(2\pi)\\
\varphi_{2}(2\pi)\end{array}\right)=M[u]\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\varphi_{1}(0)\\
\varphi_{2}(0)\end{array}\right)

Taking a standard basis satisfying the conditions

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(0)=0,\ \varphi^{\prime}_{1}(0)=1\ \ ,\ \ \varphi_{2}(0)=1,\ \varphi^{\prime}_{2}(0)=0

(7.40)

the matrix
M[u]
becomes

\displaystyle M[u]=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\varphi^{\prime}_{1}(2\pi)&\varphi_{1}(2\pi)\\
\varphi^{\prime}_{2}(2\pi)&\varphi_{2}(2\pi)\end{array}\right)

and it is invariant under Diff
{}_{0}(S^{1})
, as we show in the Appendix
B.4
. This matrix, also called the monodromy matrix of the Hill operator, is the analogue of the Wilson loop
W[A]
which is invariant under the action of
\mathcal{G}_{0}
.

7.2.4
Diff-Wilson Loop for
\nabla^{(3)}

In the previous section we obtained the analog of the Wilson loop associated with the Hill operator
\nabla^{(2)}_{u}
which maps
-1/2
densities to
3/2
densities. Although
\nabla^{(2)}
is considered as the analog of the covariant derivative in the fundamental representation, Hill operator is not what we are looking for to build a theory of the diff field.
In fact, the only operator that can be used to build a theory of the diff field is
\nabla^{(3)}_{D}
as the diff-Gauss law operator can be rewritten as

\displaystyle G=\delta_{X}D=\nabla^{(3)}_{D}X

(7.43)

Hence, we need to generalize the analysis of the previous section to
\nabla^{(3)}_{D}
and obtain its monodromy matrix.

Consider the equation

\displaystyle\nabla^{(3)}_{D}\psi=D^{\prime}\psi+2D\psi^{\prime}+q\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}=0

(7.44)

associated with the Virasoro covariant derivative
\nabla^{(3)}_{D}
mapping
-1
density (vector or adjoint element) to
2
density (quadratic differential or coadjoint element ). Explicitly,

\displaystyle\nabla^{(3)}_{D}\overset{\phi}{\rightarrow}(\phi^{\prime})^{-2}\,\nabla^{(3)}_{D_{\phi}}\,(\phi^{\prime})^{-1}

(7.45)

where
\phi\in\text{Diff}_{0}(S^{1})
, and

\displaystyle D_{\phi}(x)=\phi^{\prime}(x)^{2}D(\phi(x))+q\,S\phi(x)

(7.46)

Introduce a basis for the solution

\displaystyle\psi(x)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\varphi_{1}(x)\\
\varphi_{2}(x)\\
\varphi_{3}(x)\end{array}\right)

satisfying the conditions

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(0)=1\ ,\ \varphi_{1}^{\prime}(0)=0\ ,\ \varphi^{\prime\prime}(0)=0

(7.50a)

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}(0)=0\ ,\ \varphi_{2}^{\prime}(0)=1\ ,\ \varphi_{2}^{\prime\prime}(0)=0

(7.50b)

\displaystyle\varphi_{3}(0)=0\ ,\ \varphi_{3}^{\prime}(0)=0\ ,\ \varphi_{3}^{\prime\prime}(0)=1

(7.50c)

The analog of (
7.2.3
) can be written as

\displaystyle\psi(2\pi)

\displaystyle=M[D]\psi(0)

(7.51)

Using the initial conditions (
7.50
) we recover

\displaystyle M[D]=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\varphi_{1}(2\pi)&\varphi_{1}^{\prime}(2\pi)&\varphi_{1}^{\prime\prime}(2\pi)\\
\varphi_{2}(2\pi)&\varphi_{2}^{\prime}(2\pi)&\varphi_{2}^{\prime\prime}(2\pi)\\
\varphi_{3}(2\pi)&\varphi_{3}^{\prime}(2\pi)&\varphi_{3}^{\prime\prime}(2\pi)\end{array}\right)

Now,
\psi
transforms in the adjoint

\displaystyle\phi\circ\psi(x)=\psi(\phi(x))[\phi^{\prime}(x)]^{-1}

(7.55)

and
\phi\in\text{Diff}_{0}(S^{1})
so

\displaystyle\phi(0)=0\ ,\ \phi^{\prime}(0)=1\ ,\ \phi^{\prime\prime}(0)=0\ ,\ \phi^{\prime\prime\prime}(0)=0

(7.56)

and

\displaystyle\phi(x+2\pi)=\phi(x)+2\pi\ \ ,\ \ \ \phi^{(n)}(x+2\pi)=\phi^{(n)}(x)

(7.57)

Evaluating (
7.55
) at
x=0
and
x=2\pi
and using (
7.57
) it is straightforward to show

\displaystyle(\phi\circ\psi)(2\pi)=M[D](\phi\circ\psi)(0)

(7.58)

i.e.

\displaystyle M[D^{\phi}]=M[D]

(7.59)

7.2.5
First Type Coadjoint Orbits of Virasoro Algebra

Classification of coadjoint orbits of the Virasoro algebra has been done in
[
lazutkin
]
,
[
segalunitary
]
, and
[
witten88
]
. Here we will follow
[
witten88
]
. It turns out that classification is achieved by solving the isotropy equation
4
4

4

This is because a coadjoint orbit is obtained as the quotient of the group by the isotropy group for the orbit.
for a coadjoint element
(D(\theta),c)
,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\delta_{f}D=fD^{\prime}+2f^{\prime}D+qf^{\prime\prime\prime}

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\equiv-c/24\pi

(7.60)

given
D(\theta)
, with the requirement of periodicity of
f(\theta)
and its derivatives, and up to the action of diffeomorphisms for both
f
and
D
. We will restrict our attention to the simplest types of orbits, namely, for
D
diffeomorphic to a constant. These are sometimes called the first type orbits
[
LR95
]
.

For
D=D_{0}=\text{constant}\neq 0
, (
7.2.5
) reduces to

\displaystyle 0=2f^{\prime}D_{0}+qf^{\prime\prime\prime}

(7.61)

Defining
g\equiv f^{\prime}
and

\displaystyle\omega^{2}\equiv\frac{2D_{0}}{q}=-\frac{48\pi D_{0}}{c}

(7.62)

(
7.61
) becomes

\displaystyle g^{\prime\prime}=-\omega^{2}g

(7.63)

whose solution is

\displaystyle g=a\cos(\omega\theta)+b\sin(\omega\theta)

(7.64)

This implies

\displaystyle f(\theta)=c_{0}+c_{1}\cos(\omega\theta)+c_{2}\sin(\omega\theta)

(7.65)

for arbitrary constants
c_{0},c_{1}
and
c_{2}
.

Periodicity,
f(2\pi)=f(0)
implies the condition

\displaystyle c_{2}\sin(2\pi\omega)=c_{1}(1-\cos(2\pi\omega))

(7.66)

This condition motivates the following distinct cases :

i)
\omega=n\in\mathbb{Z}-0
. Then (
7.66
) is automatically satisfied and we have

\displaystyle f(\theta)=c_{0}+c_{1}\cos(n\theta)+c_{2}\sin(n\theta)

(7.67)

and the vector
f\,d/d\theta
stabilizes the coadjoint element

\displaystyle D_{0}=-\frac{nc}{48\pi}

(7.68)

The stabilizer (or isotropy group) is generated by (
7.67
) which is a linear combination of
L_{0},L_{n},L_{-n}
where
L_{m}\equiv ie^{im\theta}d/d\theta
. For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}-0
, these generate
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. Hence, in this case the coadjoint orbit is given by

\displaystyle\text{Orb}(D_{0})\cong\text{Diff}\,S^{1}/\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})^{(n)}

(7.69)

ii)
\omega\notin\mathbb{Z}-0
. Then arbitrarines of
D_{0}
up to diffeomorphisms requires
c_{1}=c_{2}=0
and we are left with

\displaystyle f=c_{0}

(7.70)

and

\displaystyle D_{0}=\text{constant}\neq-\frac{nc}{48\pi}

(7.71)

Thus the stabilizer is generated by
L_{0}
. It is the group of rigid rotations of
S^{1}
which is isomorphic to
S^{1}
itself. Hence, the coadjoint orbit for this case becomes

\displaystyle\text{Orb}(D_{0})\cong\text{Diff}\,S^{1}/S^{1}

(7.72)

7.2.6
Diff-Wilson Loop Proposed in
[
LR95
]

Recall the definition (
7.17
) of the Wilson loop. If instead of evaluating
\psi
at
x=0
and
x=2\pi
, we evaluated it at two arbitrary locations
z,y
on the circle we would get

\displaystyle\psi(z)=W[z,y;A]\psi(y)

(7.73)

W[z,y;A]
is the Wilson line. We can evaluate this equation at the gauge transformed configurations

\displaystyle\psi^{g}(z)=W[z,y;A^{g}]\psi^{g}(y)

(7.74)

Now, using (
7.15a
) we can rewrite this equation as

\displaystyle g(z)\psi(z)=W[z,y;A^{g}]g(y)\psi(y)

(7.75)

or

\displaystyle\psi(z)=g^{-1}(z)W[z,y;A^{g}]g(y)\psi(y)

(7.76)

Comparing this with (
7.73
) we see that

\displaystyle W[z,y,;A]=g^{-1}(z)W[z,y;A^{g}]g(y)

(7.77)

or

\displaystyle W[z,y;A^{g}]=g(z)W[z,y;A]g^{-1}(y)

(7.78)

Hence, the Wilson line is gauge-covariant.

In
[
LR95
]
the diff-Wilson line is proposed to be
v(x)
satisfying

\displaystyle D=c\,S(v)

(7.79)

The analog of the gauge transformation is the Virasoro coadjoint transformation

\displaystyle D\overset{f}{\mapsto}D^{f}=f^{\prime 2}D\circ f+c\,S(f)

(7.80)

Although
v
is not a diffeomorphism (nor is, in general, well-defined on the circle) we should be able to use the identity (
B.27
), as long as
v
is not the inverted map in the identity. With this assumption (
7.80
) becomes

\displaystyle D^{f}=c\,S(v\circ f)

(7.81)

Comparing this with (
7.79
) we see that

\displaystyle v^{f}=v\circ f

(7.82)

where
v^{f}
is the proposed Wilson line for the transformed field
D^{f}
.

Now, the Wilson loop is obtained by evaluation at
x=2\pi
, i.e.,
Q\equiv v(2\pi)
. We see that invariance of the diff-Wilson loop,
Q^{f}(2\pi)=Q(2\pi)
, requires
f(2\pi)=1
. Since
f
is a diffeomorphism, alternatively, we need
f(0)=1
. This is not satisfied for all the diffeomorphisms in Diff
S^{1}
nor for those in the subset Diff
{}_{0}S^{1}
defined in (
7.32
).

However, it is satisfied for all
f\in\text{Diff}S^{1}
by the solutions (
5.30
) taken. These solutions are valid for the first-type orbits, with the additional trivial solution,
v(x)=1
. The problem, however, is that finite reduction of DX theory is obtained for only a single solution, namely,
v=\exp(i\alpha x)
, with
\alpha=\sqrt{2D_{0}/c}
. It is in this sense that application of the Wilson loop method of
[
rajeev88
]
is incomplete in
[
LR95
]
.

In Section
7.2.4
we obtained the complete diff-Wilson loop
M[D]
that is invariant under Diff
{}_{0}S^{1}
extending the analysis of
[
hendersonrajeev
]
for the Hill operator
\nabla^{(2)}
to the differential operator
\nabla^{(3)}
. In the next section we evaluate
M[D]
for the first type orbits. The analysis in Section
5.3
should be extended with the results of the next section. Namely, instead of a theory on a 2D phase space
(Q,P)
, one gets a theory on 6D a phase space with
(Q_{i},P_{i})
,
i=1,2,3
.

7.2.7
Diff-Wilson Loop for
\nabla^{(3)}_{u}
on First Type Orbits

Reconsider the Wilson loop equation

\displaystyle\delta_{\psi}D=\nabla^{(3)}_{D}\psi=D^{\prime}\psi+2D\psi^{\prime}+q\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}=0

(7.83)

This is the same as the isotropy equation. Now, if we restrict
D
to first type orbits, discussed in Section
7.2.5
, the general solution reads

\displaystyle\psi(x)=c_{0}+c_{1}\cos(\omega x)+c_{2}\sin(\omega x)

(7.84)

The solution basis satisfying the initial conditions (
7.50
) is given by

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=1

(7.85a)

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=\sin(\omega x)/\omega

(7.85b)

\displaystyle\varphi_{3}(x)

\displaystyle=(1-\cos(\omega x))/\omega^{2}

(7.85c)

Hence, the Wilson loop (
7.2.4
) can be evaluated as

\displaystyle M[D]=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
\frac{\sin(2\pi\omega)}{\omega}&\cos(2\pi\omega)&-\omega\sin(2\pi\omega)\\
\frac{1}{\omega^{2}}(1-\cos(2\pi\omega))&\frac{\sin(2\pi\omega)}{\omega}&\cos(2\pi\omega)\end{array}\right)

Now consider the two subcases discussed in Section
7.2.5
. Given
\omega\neq 0
:

Case (i) :
\omega\in\mathbb{Z}
. In this case we get

\displaystyle M[D]=1_{3\times 3}\ \ \ \ \ \text{if}\ \ D\in\text{Diff}S^{1}/SL(2,\mathbb{R})^{(n)}

(7.89)

In other words, for Diff
S^{1}/SL(2,\mathbb{R})^{(n)}
orbits the diff-Wilson loop becomes trivial.

Case (ii) :
\omega\notin\mathbb{Z}
. In this case we get .

\displaystyle M[D]\neq 1_{3\times 3}\ \ \ \ \ \text{if}\ \ D\in\text{Diff}S^{1}/S^{1}\hskip 50.58878pt

(7.90)

So for Diff
S^{1}/S^{1}
orbits we have a nontrivial diff-Wilson loop.

It is interesting to consider time evolution, say, restricted to constant
D
orbits. In this case one can start with an
\omega\notin\mathbb{Z}
and end up with
\omega\in\mathbb{Z}
(as
\omega
is determined by
D
). Then how should the corresponding transition between the Wilson loops, namely, from a nontrivial matrix to the identity matrix be physically interpreted?

7.3
Diff Field as a Projective Connection

7.3.1
The Proposal

In
[
brensinger
]
authors proposed an alternative approach to obtain a dynamical theory of the diff field. By identifying the diff field
with a component of a Thomas-Whitehead (TW) projective connection
[
thomas1
]
,
[
thomas2
]
,
[
whitehead
]
, the interaction term (
3.77
)

\displaystyle S_{\text{int}}=\int d^{2}x\,D_{--}\,h_{++}

(7.91)

appearing in the Virasoro geometric action (
3.76
) can be recovered from

\displaystyle S_{\text{int}}=\int d^{3}x\ \sqrt{\det(-G)}K_{\alpha\beta}G^{\alpha\beta}

(7.92)

where
G_{\alpha\beta}
is the dimensionally-extended metric obtained from the 2D Polyakov metric, using the chiral Dirac matrix
\gamma^{3}
for defining the third dimension, and
K_{\alpha\beta}=K^{\rho}_{\ \alpha\rho\beta}

is the Ricci tensor derived from the TW projective connection.

The action governing the dynamics of the diff field is proposed to be

\displaystyle S_{D}=\int d^{3}x\ \sqrt{\det(-G)}K^{\alpha}_{\ \beta\gamma\rho}K_{\alpha}^{\ \beta\gamma\rho}

(7.93)

Our focus in this thesis will be on the very first step, namely, examining the relationship between TW projective connections and the diff field (i.e. a Virasoro coadjoint element) in Section
7.4
.

7.3.2
Projective Connections on
\mathbb{RP}^{m}

We follow the review
[
saunders
]
for the Thomas-Whitehead (TW) projective connections
[
thomas1
]
,
[
thomas2
]
,
[
whitehead
]
and the alternative version (based on volume bundle)
[
robertsTW
]
.

Given a vector space
V
the associated projective space
P(V)
is the set of one-dimensional subspaces of
V
.
P(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
is denoted
\mathbb{RP}^{n}
.

To introduce TW projective connections we refer to an alternative description of
\mathbb{RP}^{m}
.
As a manifold,
\mathbb{RP}^{m}
is the quotient of
\mathbb{R}^{m+1}_{0}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{m+1}-0
under the multiplicative action of
\mathbb{R}_{0}\equiv\mathbb{R}-0
. The infinitesimal generator of this action is the radial vector field given in Cartesian coordinates by
x^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}=\Upsilon
.

We may represent objects on
\mathbb{RP}^{m}
as objects on
\mathbb{R}^{m+1}_{0}
transforming appropriately under the
\mathbb{R}_{0}
action; this will be expressed in terms of the Lie derivative with respect to
\Upsilon
, together with invariance under the reflection map
j:x\mapsto-x
.

Functions on
\mathbb{RP}^{m}
may be represented by functions
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{m+1}_{0}
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Upsilon}f=\Upsilon f=0\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ j^{*}(f)=f

(7.94)

Let us call the set of such functions
\mathcal{F}_{\Upsilon}
.

Similarly, vector fields on
\mathbb{RP}^{m}
may be represented by equivalence classes of vector fields
X
on
\mathbb{R}^{m+1}_{0}
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Upsilon}X\propto\Upsilon\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ j_{*}(X)=X

(7.95)

with equivalence

\displaystyle Y\sim X\ \ \ \text{if}\ \ \ Y-X\propto\Upsilon

(7.96)

Let
\mathfrak{X}_{\Upsilon}
denote the set of such vector fields.

Let
X_{E}
denote the equivalence class of
X\in\mathfrak{X}_{\Upsilon}
. The set
\mathfrak{X}_{\Upsilon,E}
of equivalence classes is a Lie algebra over
\mathcal{F}_{\Upsilon}
, with

\displaystyle[\,X_{E},Y_{E}\,]=[X,Y]_{E}

(7.97)

For any
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\Upsilon}
we have
Xf\in\mathcal{F}_{\Upsilon}
if
X\in\mathfrak{X}_{\Upsilon}
and
Yf=Xf
if
Y\sim X
. Thus,
X_{E}f
is well-defined (as
Xf
). Hence,
\mathfrak{X}_{\Upsilon,E}
elements act as derivations on
\mathcal{F}_{\Upsilon}
.

We may define a covariant derivative operator on
\mathfrak{X}_{\Upsilon,E}
as a map
\nabla:\mathfrak{X}_{\Upsilon,E}\times\mathfrak{X}_{\Upsilon,E}\rightarrow\mathfrak{X}_{\Upsilon,E}
which is
\mathbb{R}
-bilinear,
\mathcal{F}_{\Upsilon}
-linear in the first variable and satisfies

\displaystyle\nabla_{X_{E}}(fY_{E})=f\nabla_{X_{E}}Y_{E}+(X_{E}f)Y_{E}

(7.98)

A covariant derivative is symmetric if

\displaystyle\nabla_{X_{E}}Y_{E}-\nabla_{Y_{E}}X_{E}=[\,X_{E},Y_{E}\,]

(7.99)

We now relate such operators to the standard covariant derivative
D
on
\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
by choosing a representative of each equivalence class. For this purpose, we introduce a one form
\omega
on
\mathbb{R}^{m+1}_{0}
such that

\displaystyle\left<\Upsilon,\omega\right>=1\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ j^{*}(\omega)=\omega

(7.100)

Then for any vector field
X
we set

\displaystyle\tilde{X}=X-\left<X,\omega\right>\Upsilon

(7.101)

\tilde{X}
satisfies
\left<\tilde{X},\omega\right>=0
.
Then
Y\sim X
implies
\tilde{Y}=\tilde{X}
, and,
X\in\mathfrak{X}_{\Upsilon}
implies that
\tilde{X}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\Upsilon}
.
Thus such a one form
\omega
enables us to select a representative of each equivalence class.

If, furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Upsilon}\omega=0

(7.102)

it follows that
\mathcal{L}_{\Upsilon}\tilde{X}=0
.
\Upsilon
is an infinitesimal affine transformation of
D
. Hence, when
\mathcal{L}_{\Upsilon}\tilde{X}=0=\mathcal{L}_{\Upsilon}\tilde{Y}
we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Upsilon}(D_{\tilde{X}}\tilde{Y})=D_{\mathcal{L}_{\Upsilon}\tilde{X}}\tilde{Y}+D_{\tilde{X}}(\mathcal{L}_{\Upsilon}\tilde{Y})=0

(7.103)

j
is an affine transformation, so when
j_{*}(\tilde{X})=\tilde{X}
and
j_{*}(\tilde{Y})=\tilde{Y}
we have
j_{*}(D_{\tilde{X}}\tilde{Y})=D_{\tilde{X}}\tilde{Y}
. So for any
\omega
satisfying the conditions given above we may define a symmetric connection
\nabla^{\omega}
on
\mathfrak{X}_{\Upsilon}
from the standard covariant derivative on
\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
as

\displaystyle\nabla^{\omega}_{X_{E}}Y_{E}=\Big(D_{\tilde{X}}\tilde{Y}\Big)_{E}

(7.104)

As a final remark, let us mention that
\Upsilon
has the property that

\displaystyle D\Upsilon=\text{id}

(7.105)

where id is the identity tensor; and this equation determines
\Upsilon
up to the addition of a constant vector field.

To generalize this construction from
\mathbb{RP}^{m}
to an arbitrary smooth manifold
M
we need the analog of
\mathbb{R}^{m+1}_{0}
in the case of
M
i.e. a smooth manifold of one higher dimension. Then we can define analog of
D
, a covariant derivative on this higher dimensional manifold, from which we can recover the analog of
\nabla
, the covariant derivative on
\mathbb{RP}^{m}
. It turns out that the larger manifold we are looking for is the volume bundle
V(M)
of
M
.

7.3.3
TW Connections over a Smooth Manifold

Over an
m
-dimensional manifold
M
with coordinates
x^{a}\equiv(x^{1},\cdots,x^{m})
one may build the volume bundle
V(M)
. An element of the fiber is a volume form i.e. an
m
-form

\displaystyle\omega=c(\omega)\,dx^{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx^{m}

(7.106)

Here,
c(\omega)
is the coordinate of
\omega
with respect to standard coordinate basis
\{\partial_{a}\}
of
M
. Let us, instead, coordinatize the fibers as

\displaystyle\lambda=|c|^{1/m+1}

(7.107)

So the coordinates for the volume bundle become
x^{\alpha}\equiv(x^{0},x^{1},\cdots,x^{m})\equiv(\lambda,x^{1},\cdots,x^{m})
.

There is a natural
\mathbb{R}_{+}
action (
\mathbb{R}_{+}\equiv\{x>0,\ x\in\mathbb{R}\}
) on this bundle that scales only the
\lambda
coordinate :

\displaystyle R_{s}:V(M)\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow V(M):(\omega,s)\mapsto s\omega

(7.108)

In coordinates this reads

\displaystyle x^{a}\mapsto x^{a}\hskip 21.68121pt\text{and}\hskip 21.68121pt\lambda\mapsto s^{1/m+1}\lambda

(7.109)

This action is generated by

\displaystyle\Upsilon=\lambda\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}

(7.110)

A TW connection is a special connection on
V(M)
, namely, one that satisfies

\displaystyle\tilde{\nabla}\Upsilon=\text{id}

(7.111)

Operators with tilde refer to the
m+1
dimensional space, and those without tilde refer to the projected
m
dimensional space.

Equation (
7.111
) implies the following

\displaystyle\tilde{\Gamma}^{\alpha}_{00}=0=\tilde{\Gamma}^{0}_{a0}\hskip 21.68121pt,\hskip 21.68121pt\tilde{\Gamma}^{b}_{a0}=\lambda^{-1}\delta^{b}_{a}\ (\lambda>0)\hskip 21.68121pt,\hskip 21.68121pt\tilde{\Gamma}^{0}_{ab}=\lambda\mathcal{D}_{ab}

(7.112)

Here
\mathcal{D}_{ab}
is an object intrinsic to the manifold
M
.

The geodesic equations then read

\displaystyle\ddot{\lambda}+\lambda\mathcal{D}_{ab}\dot{x}^{a}\dot{x}^{b}=0

(7.113)

\displaystyle\ddot{x}^{c}+\tilde{\Gamma}^{c}_{ab}\dot{x}^{a}\dot{x}^{b}=\left(-2\dot{\lambda}/\lambda\right)\dot{x}^{c}

(7.114)

Here
\dot{F}\equiv dF/d\tau
.

We reparametrize the paths
\tau\mapsto f(\tau)
such that (
7.114
) becomes affine (i.e. right hand side of it becomes zero or geodesic becomes geodetic). This requires the condition

\displaystyle\ddot{f}/\dot{f}=-2\dot{\lambda}/\lambda

(7.115)

When this condition is inserted into (
7.113
) one gets

\displaystyle Sf(\tau)=2\mathcal{D}_{ab}\dot{x}^{a}\dot{x}^{b}

(7.116)

where
Sf(\tau)
is the Schwarzian (
B.24
).

Now, given a TW connection
\tilde{\nabla}
on
V(M)
, with the aid of a any one-form
v
on
V(M)
which is
\mathbb{R}_{+}
invariant and satisfies
\left<\Upsilon,v\right>=1
one can construct a symmetric affine connection
\nabla^{v}
on
M
. Such a one-form
v
is the connection form on the principal bundle
V(M)\rightarrow M
. In fact
\tilde{\nabla}
gives rise in this way to a projective equivalence class
[\nabla]
of symmetric affine connections on
M
, the different members of the class corresponding to different choices of
v
. The difference
v^{\prime}-v
of two members of the equivalence class is the pullback of a one-form on
M
, which determines the projective transformation relating the two corresponding connections
\nabla^{v},\nabla^{v^{\prime}}
on
M
. For more details, see
[
saunders
]
and
[
robertsTW
]
.

7.3.4
The Theory

To obtain a metric on
V(M)
one can use the Dirac algebra on
M
,

\displaystyle\{\gamma^{a},\gamma^{b}\}=2g^{ab}.

(7.117)

Then the chiral Dirac matrix,
\gamma^{m+1}
is given, up to a factor
k(\lambda)
involving the volume parameter, by

\displaystyle\gamma(\lambda)_{m+1}=\frac{k(\lambda)}{m!}i^{\frac{m-2}{2}}\epsilon_{a_{1}\cdots a_{m}}\gamma^{a_{1}}\cdots\gamma^{a_{m}}.

(7.118)

The new Dirac algebra, obtained with the addition of
\gamma_{m+1}
, defines a metric
G
on
V(M)
through

\displaystyle\{\gamma_{\alpha},\gamma_{\beta}\}=2G_{\alpha\beta},

(7.119)

where
\alpha,\beta=1,\cdots,m+1.

One can contract
G_{\alpha\beta}
with the projective curvature to obtain the interaction term (
7.91
) in the Virasoro geometric action. The volume factor of the extended manifold is given by

\displaystyle\sqrt{-\det(G_{\mu\nu})}=\sqrt{-\det(g_{ab})}k(\lambda)

(7.120)

The factor
k(\lambda)
is proposed to be fixed by the condition
\det G=k(\lambda)^{2}\det g^{\prime}=1
under a conformal transformation
g^{\prime}_{ab}=\exp(2\lambda)g_{ab}
.

Now, this construction can be applied to a 2D manifold with the Polyakov metric

\displaystyle g_{ab}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&2h(\theta,\tau)\end{array}\right)

where
h=\partial_{\theta}f/\partial_{\tau}f
. The extended 3D metric becomes

\displaystyle G_{\alpha\beta}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&1&0\\
1&2h(\theta,\tau)&0\\
0&0&k(\lambda)\end{array}\right)

It is straightforward to compute the projective curvature components
K^{\rho}_{\ \alpha\beta\gamma}
. In particular, the projective Ricci tensor components become

\displaystyle K_{\alpha\beta}=\begin{cases}-\lambda\,\partial_{\lambda}\mathcal{D}_{\theta\theta}&{\alpha=1,\beta=1}\\
-\lambda\,\partial_{\lambda}\mathcal{D}_{\theta\tau}-\partial^{2}_{\theta}h_{\tau\tau},&\alpha=1,\beta=2\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(7.126)

Then, using the metric
G_{\alpha\beta}
on the 3D space, the proposed interaction term reads

\displaystyle S_{\text{Diff Inter}}

\displaystyle=\int d\lambda\,d\theta\,d\tau\,\sqrt{-G}\,G^{\mu\nu}K_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\int d\lambda\,d\theta\,d\tau\,\frac{k(\lambda)}{2}(\partial_{\lambda}\mathcal{D}_{\theta\theta}\,h_{\tau\tau}-\partial_{\lambda}\mathcal{D}_{\theta\tau}-\partial^{2}_{\theta}h_{\tau\tau}).

(7.127)

The arguments provided for recovering the diff-Polyakov interaction term (
7.91
) from this integral are as follows. The last term is the scalar curvature and is a total derivative so can be dropped. The middle term is decoupled from the metric so integrates to a constant. And, using the identification
D_{ab}=\lambda\partial_{\lambda}\mathcal{D}_{ab}
on the
\lambda
boundary, the first term yields the Polyakov-diff interaction term.

Finally, the proposed action for the diff field read

\displaystyle S_{\text{ Diff}}=\int d\theta\,d\lambda\,d\tau\,\sqrt{-G}\,K_{\;\;\alpha\beta\gamma}^{\rho}K_{\;\;\mu\nu\sigma}^{\delta}G_{\alpha\mu}G_{\beta\nu}G_{\gamma\sigma}G^{\rho\delta}.

(7.128)

The action for
\Gamma^{a}_{bc}=0
will only involve the diff field components so describes the free theory. It becomes

\displaystyle S_{\text{ Diff free}}

\displaystyle=\int d\theta\,d\lambda\,d\tau\,\sqrt{2}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}k(\lambda)}\left((\Delta_{\lambda})^{2}\mathcal{D}_{\theta\theta}-2(\Delta_{\lambda}\mathcal{D}_{\theta\tau})^{2}+(\Delta_{\lambda}\mathcal{D}_{\tau\tau})^{2}\right)\right.

\displaystyle-\sqrt{2}\lambda^{2}k(\lambda)^{3}\left((\partial_{\tau}\mathcal{D}_{\theta\theta})^{2}-(\partial_{\tau}\mathcal{D}_{\theta\tau})^{2}-2(\partial_{\tau}\mathcal{D}_{\theta\theta})(\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{D}_{\theta\tau})\right)

\displaystyle-\sqrt{2}\lambda^{2}k(\lambda)^{3}\left((\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{D}_{\theta\tau})^{2}+2(\partial_{\tau}\mathcal{D}_{\theta\tau})(\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{D}_{\tau\tau})+(\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{D}_{\tau\tau})^{2}\right)\Big)

(7.129)

where

\displaystyle\Delta_{\lambda}^{2}(\mathcal{D}_{ij})\equiv\Delta_{\lambda}(\Delta_{\lambda}\mathcal{D}_{ij})\ \ ,\ \ \Delta_{\lambda}(\mathcal{D}_{ij})=\lambda^{2}k(\lambda)\partial_{\lambda}\mathcal{D}_{ij}

(7.130)

For the field equations, momenta and constraint analysis see
[
brensinger
]
.

7.4
TW Projective Connection and Virasoro Algebra

In this section we are going to review in detail the relationship between the coadjoint representation of Virasoro algebra and projective geometry.
Then we are going to move back to the Thomas-Whitehead (TW) theory.
As we have seen in the previous section, in
[
brensinger
]
, the object
\mathcal{D}
appearing in (
7.112
) as a projective TW connection component is identified with the diff field.

For this identification to be justified,
\mathcal{D}
should reduce to a Virasoro coadjoint element on a TW projective space over circle. This was not shown in
[
brensinger
]
, and the main clue at hand is the appearance of the Schwarzian derivative in (
7.116
