) as related to
\mathcal{D}
upon the reparametrization that turns the main geodesic equation (
7.114
) into a geodetic equation.
We are going to fill in this gap by showing that even in geodesic frames the equations hide Virasoro coadjoint transformation under certain conditions. These conditions require more thought.

7.4.1
Virasoro Algebra and Projective Geometry

Here we are going to lay down the mathematical motivation for the proposal in
[
brensinger
]
, namely, the relation of Virasoro algebra to projective geometry in 1D.
The main references for this section are
[
ovsienkobook
]
,
[
kirillov82
]
.

Recall the projective space
\mathbb{RP}^{m}
introduced in the previous section. Local coordinates on
\mathbb{RP}^{n}
come from
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. If
x^{\alpha}=(x^{0},\cdots,x^{n})
are local coordinates in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
then in a chart with
x^{\beta}\neq 0
the
n
affine coordinates on
\mathbb{RP}^{n}
are defined as
y^{\alpha}=x^{\alpha}/x^{\beta}
,
\alpha\neq\beta
.
In the following we restrict our attention to
\mathbb{RP}^{1}
.
If
(x,y)
are local coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
then in the region
y\neq 0
the affine coordinate on
\mathbb{RP}^{1}
is
\xi^{(1)}=x/y
, and in the region
x\neq 0
the affine coordinate on
\mathbb{RP}^{1}
is
\xi^{(2)}=y/x
. In the intersection region,
x\neq 0\neq y
the transition map
\xi^{(1)}=1/\xi^{(2)}
is a diffeomorphism.

If the affine coordinate on
\mathbb{RP}^{1}
is
y
then a projective transformation
g\in PGL(2,\mathbb{R})
is defined by

\displaystyle y\mapsto g(y)=\frac{ay+b}{cy+d}

(7.131)

Consider a nondegenerate curve
\gamma(t)
in
\mathbb{RP}^{1}
i.e.
\gamma:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{RP}^{1}
, and nondegeneracy means
\dot{\gamma}(t)\neq 0\ ,\forall t
. Two curves
\gamma_{1}(t)
and
\gamma_{2}(t)
are projectively equivalent if they are related by a projective transformation
g\in PGL(2,\mathbb{R})
:

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}\sim\gamma_{2}\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ \gamma_{2}(t)=g\circ\gamma_{1}(t)

(7.132)

The following is from
[
ovsienkobook
]
Theorem 1.3.

Theorem 7.4.1
.

There is a one to one correspondence between the equivalence classes of non-degenerate curves in
\mathbb{RP}^{1}
and Hill operators

\displaystyle L=\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}+u(t)

(7.133)

where
u(t)
is a smooth function. In the affine coordinate on
\mathbb{RP}^{1}
a curve in the equivalence class is given by a function
f(t)
. Then the corresponding Hill operator has the potential

\displaystyle u(t)=\frac{1}{2}S(f(t))

(7.134)

See
[
ovsienkobook
]
for the proof.

For arbitrary diffeomorphisms
f,g\in\text{Diff}(\mathbb{RP}^{1})
the Schwarzian derivative satisfies (
B.27
).
As a result, the action of a diffeomorphism
g\in\text{Diff}(S^{1})
on the Hill operator becomes:

\displaystyle T_{g{-1}}:u\mapsto(g^{\prime})^{2}u(g)+\frac{1}{2}S(g)

(7.135)

This is the same as the transformation (
2.92
) of a Virasoro coadjoint element of central charge
1/2
. Therefore, we have the following correspondence

\displaystyle(u(x),c)\leftrightarrow 2c\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}+u(x)

(7.136)

between a Virasoro coadjoint element of central charge
c
on the left and the Hill operator on the right. This shows that
there is a one to one correspondence between Virasoro coadjoint elements and the projective equivalence classes of curves in
\mathbb{RP}^{1}
.

The second main result is obtained by discussing the consequences of the above result for projective manifolds.
In
[
brensinger
]
it is stated that the potential
u(x)
in (
7.136
) is a projective connection, referring to
[
kirillov82
]
.
In
[
kirillov82
]
Kirillov simply states that every Virasoro coadjoint element defines a projective structure on
S^{1}
. Indeed, this result can be reached using the Theorem
7.4.1
as we show below
[
ovsienkobook
]
. To clarify the matter, let us first review the concept of a projective structure.

A projective structure is the analog of a differentiable structure on a smooth manifold in the case of a projective space. Explicitly, in 1D, a projective structure on
\mathbb{R}
is given by an atlas
\{(U_{i},\varphi_{i})\}
where
\{U_{i}\}
is an open covering of
\mathbb{R}
and the maps
\varphi_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{RP}^{1}
are local diffeomorphisms such that the transition maps
\varphi_{i}\circ\varphi_{j}^{-1}
on
\mathbb{RP}^{1}
are projective.

A projective atlas defines a smooth immersion
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{RP}^{1}
, and a projective structure gives a projective equivalence class of such immersions, in the sense of (
7.132
). The immersion
\varphi
, modulo projective equivalence (i.e.
\varphi(t)\sim g\varphi(t)
with
g\in PGL(2,\mathbb{R})
), is called the developing map. The maps
\varphi_{i}
are nondegenerate curves in
\mathbb{RP}^{1}
so that the Theorem
7.4.1
states that the developing map
\varphi
gives rise to a Hill operator (
7.133
). Therefore,
the space of projective structures on
\mathbb{RP}^{1}
is identified with the space of Hill operators.

The definition of the projective structure extends to
S^{1}
, but a new feature is needed. Identifying
S^{1}
with
\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
, the developing map satisfies the additional condition
\varphi(t+2\pi)=M(\varphi(t))
for some
M\in PGL(2,\mathbb{R})
. The projective map
M
is called the monodromy. In this case projective equivalence of
\varphi
extends to include
M
as well i.e.
(\varphi(t),M)\sim(g\varphi(t),gMg^{-1})
for
g\in PGL(2,\mathbb{R})
. The monodromy condition implies that the potential
u(t)
satisfies
u(t+2\pi)=u(t)
, which is a requirement for
u(t)
to be a Virasoro coadjoint element associated with
S^{1}
.

Now using the correspondence (
7.136
) between Hill operators and Virasoro coadjoint elements we can restate the finding above :
Every Virasoro coadjoint element defines a projective structure on
S^{1}
.
In fact, what Kirillov
[
kirillov82
]
reached was this result which seems to have nothing to do with a connection at this level.

However, it does, due to the following correspondence
[
matveev2017
]
. Namely, that a projective structure can be equivalently given by a torsion-free, projectively-flat connection. Let us open this a bit. A projectively-flat connection is a connection that is projectively equivalent to a flat connection (i.e. one for which curvature vanishes). Two connections
\tilde{\nabla}
,
\nabla
are projectively equivalent
[
levicivitaprojective
]
if there exists a one-form
\phi
such that, for arbitrary vector fields
X,Y

\displaystyle\tilde{\nabla}_{X}Y=\nabla_{X}Y+\phi(Y)X+\phi(X)Y

(7.137)

The motivation behind this definition is that projectively equivalent connections yield the same geodesics (considered as unparametrized curves).

Now, if a connection is projectively-flat then it defines locally-flat geodesics in a neighborhood of any point. If one forms an atlas from these neighborhoods then the transition map between them will be a projective transformation mapping straight lines to straight lines. In particular, in the one-dimensional case, what we obtain is a family of projectively related maps
\varphi_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{RP}^{1}
, i.e. a projective structure in the sense described above.

Therefore, we can now restate the correspondence above :
Every Virasoro coadjoint element (or every Hill operator) defines a projectively-flat connection on
\mathbb{R}
. We leave it to the researcher to investigate how this result extends to the concept of projective connection as described by Thomas, Whitehead and Roberts
[
thomas1
]
,
[
thomas2
]
,
[
whitehead
]
,
[
robertsTW
]
.

In any case, it is a brilliant idea to extend a Virasoro coadjoint element (so the diff field) to higher dimensions through a projective connection. In particular, in
[
brensinger
]
Thomas and Whitehead’s formalism is used for this extension. In the next section, we are going to investigate how and under what the conditions this extension reduces back to Virasoro coadjoint orbits.

7.4.2
TW Projective Connections and Diff Field

Now, we are ready to investigate the relationship between a TW projective connection and the diff field. In the following whenever the argument of a function is suppressed it is
\theta
, and prime denotes
d/d\theta
as usual.
Reconsider the TW projective geodesic equations (
7.113
), (
7.114
) with the circle
S^{1}
as the parameter space,

\displaystyle x^{\prime\prime c}+\Gamma^{c}_{ab}x^{\prime a}x^{\prime b}=-2(\lambda^{\prime}/\lambda)x^{\prime c}

(7.138)

\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime\prime}+\lambda\mathcal{D}_{ab}x^{\prime a}x^{\prime b}=0

(7.139)

Defining

\displaystyle\Lambda\equiv(\ln\lambda)^{\prime}=\lambda^{\prime}/\lambda

(7.140)

it is easy to compute

\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime\prime}/\lambda=\Lambda^{\prime}+\Lambda^{2}

(7.141)

Then we can rewrite the geodesic equations as

\displaystyle x^{\prime\prime c}+\Gamma^{c}_{ab}x^{\prime a}x^{\prime b}=-2\Lambda x^{\prime c}

(7.142)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{D}_{ab}x^{\prime a}x^{\prime b}=\Lambda^{\prime}+\Lambda^{2}

(7.143)

For a 1+1D projective space over a circle these equations reduce to

\displaystyle x^{\prime\prime}+\Gamma x^{\prime 2}=-2\Lambda x^{\prime}

(7.144)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{D}x^{\prime 2}=\Lambda^{\prime}+\Lambda^{2}

(7.145)

Note that in these equations
\Gamma
and
\mathcal{D}
(but not
\lambda
,
\Lambda
) depend on geodesic parameter
\theta
through the coordinate
x
of the curve i.e.
\Gamma=\Gamma(x(\theta))=(\Gamma\circ x)(\theta)
,
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{D}\circ x)(\theta)
. So these equations can be properly rewritten as

\displaystyle x^{\prime\prime}+x^{\prime 2}\Gamma\circ x=-2\Lambda x^{\prime}

(7.146)

\displaystyle-x^{\prime 2}\mathcal{D}\circ x=\Lambda^{\prime}+\Lambda^{2}

(7.147)

We can solve the first equation for
\Lambda
,

\displaystyle\Lambda=-\frac{x^{\prime\prime}}{2x^{\prime}}-\frac{x^{\prime}\Gamma\circ x}{2}

(7.148)

Inserting this into the second equation we get

\displaystyle 2\mathcal{D}\circ x=\frac{(\Gamma\circ x)^{\prime}}{x^{\prime}}-\frac{(\Gamma\circ x)^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{x^{\prime 2}}Sx

(7.149)

Using the chain rule formula

\displaystyle(f\circ g)^{\prime}=(f^{\prime}\circ g)g^{\prime}

(7.150)

we can rewrite (
7.149
) as

\displaystyle 2\mathcal{D}\circ x=\partial_{x}(\Gamma\circ x)-\frac{(\Gamma\circ x)^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{x^{\prime 2}}Sx

(7.151)

Recall the transformation (
2.94
) of the connection coefficient under
x\mapsto\bar{x}(x)
.

\displaystyle\bar{\Gamma}(\bar{x})=\frac{dx}{d\bar{x}}\Gamma(x)-\left(\frac{dx}{d\bar{x}}\right)^{2}\frac{d^{2}\bar{x}}{dx^{2}}

(7.152)

If we can take the geodesic parameter
\theta
as a coordinate, so that the geodesic curve becomes a coordinate transformation (or a circle diffeomorphism), then under
x\mapsto\bar{x}=\theta
this reads

\displaystyle\bar{\Gamma}(\theta)=\frac{dx}{d\theta}\Gamma(x(\theta))-\left(\frac{dx}{d\theta}\right)^{2}\frac{d^{2}\theta}{dx^{2}}

(7.153)

Using

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}\theta}{dx^{2}}=\frac{d\theta}{dx}\frac{d}{d\theta}\frac{d\theta}{dx}=\frac{1}{x^{\prime}}\left(\frac{1}{x^{\prime}}\right)^{\prime}

(7.154)

we get

\displaystyle\bar{\Gamma}=x^{\prime}\Gamma\circ x+\frac{x^{\prime\prime}}{x^{\prime}}

(7.155)

From this we directly compute

\displaystyle\bar{\Gamma}^{\prime}-\frac{\bar{\Gamma}^{2}}{2}=x^{\prime 2}\left(\frac{(\Gamma\circ x)^{\prime}}{x^{\prime}}-\frac{(\Gamma\circ x)^{2}}{2}\right)+Sx

(7.156)

where
Sx
is the Schwarzian.
Using the chain rule (
7.150
), this becomes

\displaystyle\bar{\Gamma}^{\prime}-\frac{\bar{\Gamma}^{2}}{2}=x^{\prime 2}\left(\partial_{x}(\Gamma\circ x)-\frac{(\Gamma\circ x)^{2}}{2}\right)+Sx

(7.157)

The object

\displaystyle\Sigma=\Gamma^{\prime}-\frac{\Gamma^{2}}{2}

(7.158)

is already familiar from section
2.4.4
.
Equation (
7.157
) is the active coadjoint transformation (
2.92
) of
(\Sigma,1)
:

\displaystyle\text{Ad}^{*}_{x}\Sigma\equiv x\circ\Sigma\equiv\bar{\Sigma}=x^{\prime 2}\Sigma\circ x+Sx

(7.159)

Note that equation (
2.102
) in Section
2.4.4
is the infinitesimal version of (
7.157
).

Comparing equations (
7.151
) with (
7.157
) we get

\displaystyle 2\mathcal{D}(x)x^{\prime 2}=\bar{\Gamma}^{\prime}-\frac{\bar{\Gamma}^{2}}{2}=\bar{\Sigma}=x^{\prime 2}\Sigma\circ x+Sx

(7.160)

This shows
5
5

5

The bars on
\Gamma
are simply notational convention; they mean
\Gamma
is evaluated in the coordinate frame
\bar{x}\equiv\theta
.
that
2\mathcal{D}(x)x^{\prime 2}
is a Virasoro coadjoint element of central charge
1
. It is the coadjoint transform of
\Sigma
under
\theta\mapsto x(\theta)
.

We can recover the same result in the geodetic frame as follows. Consider the geodesic to geodetic reparametrization
\theta\rightarrow f(\theta)
i.e. one for which equation (
7.146
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}x}{df^{2}}+\Gamma(x(\theta))\left(\frac{dx}{df}\right)^{2}=0

(7.161)

and the equation (
7.147
) becomes

\displaystyle Sf(\theta)=2\mathcal{D}(x(\theta))\left(\frac{dx}{d\theta}\right)^{2}

(7.162)

Now, once again if we can identify the geodesic curve as a circle reparametrization, and take
f(\theta)=x(\theta)
, then these equations imply

\displaystyle\Gamma(x)

\displaystyle=0

(7.163)

\displaystyle 2\mathcal{D}(x)x^{\prime 2}

\displaystyle=Sx(\theta)

(7.164)

Since the Schwarzian
S(x)
is the coadjoint transform of zero coadjoint element under
\theta\rightarrow x(\theta)
,
2\mathcal{D}(x)x^{\prime 2}
is the coadjoint transform of zero coadjoint element.
The equation (
7.163
) implies
\Sigma\circ x=0
. Hence, we again see that
2(\mathcal{D}\circ x)x^{\prime 2}
is the coadjoint transform of
\Sigma(=0)
.

To summarize, for a 1+1D projective space over circle,
if we can identify the geodesic curve as a circle diffeomorphism
then
(\mathcal{D}\circ x)x^{\prime 2}
is a Virasoro coadjoint element of central charge
1/2
. In any coordinate frame it is the coadjoint transform of
\Sigma/2
. In the geodetic frame
\Sigma=0
.
Hence TW geodesic equations underlie Virasoro coadjoint transformation in 1+1D projective space over circle.

Appendix A
Dirac-Ostrogradsky Formalism

A.1
Constraint Analysis

This note contains a summary of Dirac’s constrained Hamiltonian formalism and its application to a field theory, namely Maxwell’s theory. The main references are
[
DiracLectures
]
and
[
henneaux1992quantization
]
. In this section we denote Poisson brackets by
[\ ,\ ]
.

A.1.1
Summary of Dirac’s Constrained Hamiltonian Formalism

If the momentum definition

\displaystyle p_{n}=\partial L/\partial\dot{q}_{n}

(A.1)

does not lead to an independent function of
\dot{q}_{n}
then (
A.1
) constitutes a primary constraint. Let
\phi_{m}(q,p)=0,\ m=1,\cdots,M
denote all the primary constraints.

The (naive) Hamiltonian is defined as

\displaystyle H=p_{n}\dot{q}_{n}-L

(A.2)

However, due to the relations
\phi_{m}(q,p)=0
the function

\displaystyle H^{*}=H+c_{m}(q,p)\phi_{m}(q,p)

(A.3)

is equally good as a Hamiltonian on the constraint surface.

The equations of motion on the constraint surface become

\displaystyle\dot{q}_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial H}{\partial p_{n}}+u_{m}\frac{\partial\phi_{m}}{\partial p_{n}}

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{n}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial H}{\partial q_{n}}-u_{m}\frac{\partial\phi_{m}}{\partial q_{n}}

(A.4)

where
u_{m}
can depend on
\dot{q}
as well as
q
,
p
. Time evolution of an
arbitrary dynamical variable
g=g(q,p)
is given by

\displaystyle\dot{g}=[g,H]+u_{m}[g,\phi_{m}]

(A.5)

where
[\ ,\ ]
is the Poisson bracket (PB).

We extend the ordinary PB to include variables that may depend on
\dot{q}_{n}
. Then the evolution equation becomes

\displaystyle\dot{g}=[g,H+u_{m}\phi_{m}]=:[g,H_{T}]

(A.6)

H_{T}
is called the total Hamiltonian. We define a relation, called weak equality, denoted
\approx
, meaning equal up to constraints (i.e. on the constraint surface). When there is a weak equality one should evaluate the PBs first, only then the constraints.

Any constraint equation
\phi=0
must hold throughout time. This requires the so-called consistency conditions :

\displaystyle 0\approx\dot{\phi}_{m}

\displaystyle=[\phi_{m},H_{T}]

\displaystyle=[\phi_{m},H]+u_{n}[\phi_{m},\phi_{n}]

(A.7)

Let us consider possible outcomes of these conditions:

•

Inconsistency: e.g.
L=q

\Rightarrow
equation of motion :
0\approx 1
.

•

0\approx 0
if the right-hand side of (
A.7
) is linear in
\phi_{m}
’s.

•

It may yield an expression independent of
u_{m}
and not linear in
\phi_{m}
’s. Then the consistency condition is a new constraint
\xi(q,p)=0
called a secondary constraint. For each secondary constraint one checks the consistency condition again. One exhausts all secondaries and their consistencies following the same procedure. Let
\phi_{k}\approx 0
,
k=M+1,\cdots,M+K
, denote all (K) secondary constraints. Then the complete set of constraints become

\phi_{j}\approx 0
,
j=1,\cdots,M+K
.

•

It may yield an inhomogeneous linear set of equations
[\phi_{j},H]+u_{m}[\phi_{j},\phi_{m}]\approx 0
in unknowns
u_{m}
. These are not treated as constraints.

Consider the inhomogeneous equation in the final case. Let us define
[\phi_{j},\phi_{m}]\equiv c_{jm}(q,p)
and
[\phi_{j},H]\equiv d_{j}(q,p)
. Then the inhomogeneous equation reads
u_{m}c_{jm}=d_{j}
.
There must be a solution to this equation. Let
u_{m}=U_{m}(q,p)
be a solution. This solution is not unique;
if
V_{m}(q,p)
is an arbitrary solution to the homogeneous equation,
V_{m}c_{jm}=0
, then
U_{m}+V_{m}
is also a solution to the inhomogeneous equation. Let
V_{am}
,
a=1,\cdots,A
denote all independent solutions of the homogeneous equation. Then the general solution of the inhomogeneous equation is
u_{m}=U_{m}+\sum_{a}v_{a}V_{am}
where
v_{a}
are arbitrary, possibly time-dependent. In general A
\leq
M.

With all these we have

\displaystyle H_{T}=H+U_{m}\phi_{m}+v_{a}V_{am}\phi_{m}

(A.8)

Define
H^{\prime}=H+U_{m}\phi_{m}
and
\phi_{a}=V_{am}\phi_{a}
. Then,
H_{T}=H^{\prime}+v_{a}\phi_{a}
. Here
H^{\prime}
is fixed (by the consistency equations) whereas
v_{a}\phi_{a}
is arbitrary, since
v_{a}
is an arbitrary time-dependent coefficient. This arbitrariness implies that the evolution of any dynamical variable will involve an arbitrary piece, leading to an indeterministic theory.

Let us introduce a further decomposition of the constraints. A dynamical variable,
R(q,p)
, is said to be first-class if

\displaystyle[R,\phi_{j}]\approx 0\ ,\ \forall j\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ [R,\phi_{j}]=r_{jj^{\prime}}\phi_{j^{\prime}}

(A.9)

One can show that if
R_{1}
,
R_{2}
are first-class, then
[R_{1},R_{2}]
is also first-class using the Jacobi identity of the PB.

The fixed part,
H^{\prime}
, of the total Hamiltonian is first-class:

\displaystyle[H^{\prime},\phi_{j}]=[H,\phi_{j}]+[U_{m}\phi_{m},\phi_{j}]=[H,\phi_{j}]+U_{m}[\phi_{m},\phi_{j}]\approx 0

(A.10)

since by definition,
U_{m}
is a solution to the inhomogeneous equation.
The arbitrary part,
v_{a}\phi_{a}
, of the total Hamiltonian is also first-class:

\displaystyle[v_{a}\phi_{a},\phi_{j}]=v_{a}[V_{am}\phi_{m},\phi_{j}]=v_{a}(V_{am}[\phi_{m},\phi_{j}])

(A.11)

since by definition of
V_{am}
, the term on the right weakly vanishes. Moreover, observe that
\phi_{a}
being a linear combination of primary constraints is a primary constraint. Hence, we have a total Hamiltonian which is the sum of a first-class term and a primary, first-class term. The number of independent arbitrary functions
v_{a}
is equal to the number of first-class primary constraints. Because of indeterminacy, several choices of
q,p
correspond to the same state.

Consider the infinitesimal time evolution of a dynamical variable
g

\displaystyle g(\delta t)

\displaystyle=g_{0}+\dot{g}\delta t

\displaystyle=g_{0}+[g,H_{T}]\delta t

\displaystyle=g_{0}+\delta t([g,H^{\prime}]+v_{a}[g,\phi_{a}])

(A.12)

The arbitrariness is in the last term :
\Delta g(\delta t)=\delta t(v_{a}-v^{\prime}_{a})[g,\phi_{a}]\equiv\epsilon^{a}[g,\phi_{a}]
. This then constitutes a gauge transformation. So the gauge transformations are generated by primary first-class generators.

The Dirac conjecture is that secondary first-class constraints also do generate gauge transformations. Henneaux and Teitelboim
[
henneaux1992quantization
]
disprove this providing a counterexample, yet they also show that such counterexamples are of marginal interest so that for the theories we consider Dirac conjecture holds. See
[
henneaux1992quantization
]
for details of this argument.

One therefore extends the total Hamiltonian to the extended Hamiltonian by adding also the secondary first-class constraints i.e. all first-class constraints taken into account.

Second-class constraints are not related to gauge transformations and can be eliminated by a redefinition of the PB leading to the Dirac bracket. The procedure is straightforward yet not needed for our purposes.

A.1.2
Extended Action with Only First-Class Constraints

We are going to denote the first-class constraints (primary and secondary) by
\gamma_{a}
. Then the extended action reads

\displaystyle S_{E}[q^{n}(t),p_{n}(t),u^{a}(t)]=\int dt\ (p_{n}\dot{q}^{n}-H-u^{a}\gamma_{a})

(A.13)

where
u^{a}
are the Lagrange multipliers.
H
is also first-class and we have

\displaystyle[\gamma_{a},\gamma_{b}]

\displaystyle=C_{ab}^{c}\gamma_{c}

(A.14a)

\displaystyle[H,\gamma_{a}]

\displaystyle=V_{a}^{b}\gamma_{b}

(A.14b)

The time evolution of a dynamical variable
F[q,p]
is given by

\displaystyle\dot{F}\equiv\frac{dF}{dt}=[F,H_{E}]=[F,H]+u^{a}[F,\gamma_{a}]

(A.15)

The gauge transformation of
F
is given by

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon}F=[F,G]\equiv[F,\epsilon^{a}\gamma_{a}]=\epsilon^{a}[F,\gamma_{a}]

(A.16)

where we introduced the gauge generator
G\equiv\epsilon^{a}\gamma_{a}
where
\epsilon^{a}=\epsilon^{a}(t)
and
\epsilon^{a}
are independent of phase space coordinates
q^{n},p_{n}
. The meaning of the gauge invariance of
S_{E}
is that

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon}S_{E}=0\hskip 21.68121pt\text{if}\hskip 21.68121pt\epsilon^{a}(t_{1})=0=\epsilon^{a}(t_{2})

(A.17)

The gauge invariance of
S_{E}
requires
[
henneaux1992quantization
]

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon}u^{a}=\dot{\epsilon}^{a}+u^{c}\epsilon^{b}C_{bc}^{a}-\epsilon^{b}V_{b}^{a}

(A.18)

for the gauge transformation of the Lagrange multipliers.

A.1.3
Maxwell Theory

Here we are going to examine a field theory example of the formalism introduced above, namely Maxwell theory in 4D. The Lagrangian is

\displaystyle L=-\frac{1}{4}\int d^{3}x\,F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}

(A.19)

where
F_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}
.
The variation of
L
with respect to
\partial_{0}A_{\mu}
becomes

\displaystyle\delta L=\int d^{3}x\,F^{\mu 0}\delta(\partial_{0}A_{\mu})

(A.20)

from which we read the conjugate momenta
B^{\mu}

\displaystyle B^{\mu}=F^{\mu 0}

(A.21)

Since
F_{\mu\nu}
is by definition antisymmetric, we immediately get a primary constraint

\displaystyle\phi_{1}\equiv B^{0}_{\mathbf{x}}\approx 0

(A.22)

where we included the 3D space coordinate
\mathbf{x}
as a subscript since (
A.22
) constitutes a three-fold infinity of primary constraints.

Then we build the naive Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=\int d^{3}x\,B^{\mu}(\partial_{0}A_{\mu})-L

\displaystyle=\int d^{3}x\left(\frac{1}{4}F^{ij}F_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}B^{i}B_{i}-A_{0}(\partial_{i}B^{i})\right)

(A.23)

where a partial integration is applied and boundary terms are ignored.
The consistency equation,
[B^{0},H]\approx 0
of the primary constraint yields the following secondary constraint

\displaystyle\phi_{2}\equiv\partial_{i}B^{i}\approx 0

(A.24)

which is the Gauss Law. Its consistency condition is trivial,
0=0
; so there are no other constraints.

It is straightforward to compute the algebra of constraints and of the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle[\phi_{i}(\mathbf{x}),\phi_{j}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=0,\ \ i,j=1,2

(A.25)

\displaystyle[H,\phi_{1}(\mathbf{x})]

\displaystyle=\phi_{2}(\mathbf{x})\ ,\ [H,\phi_{2}(\mathbf{x})]=0

(A.26)

so each constraint is first-class, generating gauge transformations. Hence, we can form the extended action as

\displaystyle S_{E}[B^{\mu},A_{\mu},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}]=\int d^{4}x\ (B^{i}\dot{A}_{i}+B^{0}\dot{A}_{0}-H-\mu_{1}\phi_{1}-\mu_{2}\phi_{2})

(A.27)

where
\mu_{i}
are the Lagrange multipliers for the constraints.
As discussed in the previous section, the extended action is invariant under the gauge transformations generated by the first-class constraints. The gauge generator would read

\displaystyle Q=\int dx\ (\epsilon_{1}\phi_{1}+\epsilon_{2}\phi_{2})

(A.28)

with independent functions
\epsilon_{i}
. This generates the following gauge transformations

\displaystyle\delta A_{0}=\epsilon_{1}\ \ ,\ \ \delta B^{0}=0\ \ ,\ \ \delta A_{i}=\partial_{i}\epsilon_{2}\ \ ,\ \ \delta B^{i}=0\ \ ,\ \ \delta\mu_{1}=\dot{\epsilon}_{1}\ \ ,\ \ \delta\mu_{2}=\dot{\epsilon}_{2}-\epsilon_{1}

(A.29)

In order to recover the standard form of the gauge transformations, one imposes the gauge condition
\mu_{2}=0
under which the extended action reduces to

\displaystyle S_{E}[B^{\mu},A_{\mu},\mu_{1}]=\int d^{4}x\ (B^{i}\dot{A}_{i}+B^{0}\dot{A}_{0}-H-\mu_{1}\phi_{1})

(A.30)

Consistency of the gauge condition
\delta\mu_{2}=0
require
\epsilon_{1}=\dot{\epsilon}_{2}
. Defining
\epsilon\equiv\epsilon_{2}
the residual gauge transformations read

\displaystyle\delta A_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}\epsilon\ \ ,\ \ \delta B^{\mu}=0\ \ ,\ \ \delta\mu_{1}=\ddot{\epsilon}

(A.31)

A.2
Ostrogradsky Formalism

A.2.1
Second-Order Nonsingular Lagrangian

The main reference for this section is
[
woodard15
]
.

Consider a system governed by a second-order Lagrangian
L(q,\dot{q},\ddot{q})
depending nondegenerately on
\ddot{q}
. The Euler-Lagrange (EL) equation becomes

\displaystyle 0=\frac{\delta S}{\delta q}=\frac{\partial L}{\partial q}-\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{q}}+\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\frac{\partial L}{\partial\ddot{q}}

(A.32)

Nondegeneracy means
\partial^{2}L/\partial\ddot{q}^{2}\neq 0
which implies that (
A.32
) can be cast in the form

\displaystyle q^{(4)}=\mathcal{F}(q,\dot{q},\ddot{q},q^{(3)})\ \ \Rightarrow\ \ q(t)=\mathcal{Q}(t,q_{0},\dot{q}_{0},\ddot{q}_{0},q^{(3)}_{0})

(A.33)

Because solutions
\mathcal{Q}
depend on four initial values, there must be four canonical coordinates. Ostrogradsky takes

\displaystyle Q_{1}\equiv q\ \ ,\ \ P_{1}\equiv\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{q}}-\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial L}{\partial\ddot{q}}\ \ ,\ \ Q_{2}\equiv\dot{q}\ \ ,\ \ P_{2}\equiv\frac{\partial L}{\partial\ddot{q}}

(A.34)

The assumption of nondegeneracy implies that one can invert (
A.34
) to solve for
\ddot{q}
in terms of
Q_{1},Q_{2}
and
P_{2}
. That is, there exists an acceleration
A(Q_{1},Q_{2},P_{2})
such that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial L}{\partial\ddot{q}}\bigg|_{q=Q_{1},\dot{q}=Q_{2},\ddot{q}=A}=P_{2}

(A.35)

Note that
A(Q_{1},Q_{2},P_{2})
does not depend on
P_{1}
. The momentum
P_{1}
is only needed for the third time derivative.

Ostrogradsky’s Hamiltonian is obtained by Legendre transforming on
\dot{q}
and
\ddot{q}
,

\displaystyle H(Q_{1},Q_{2},P_{1},P_{2})

\displaystyle=P_{1}\dot{q}+P_{2}\ddot{q}-L

\displaystyle=P_{1}Q_{2}+P_{2}A(Q_{1},Q_{2},P_{2})-L(Q_{1},Q_{2},A(Q_{1},Q_{2},P_{2}))

(A.36)

Hamilton’s equations are given by

\displaystyle\dot{Q}_{i}=\frac{\partial H}{\partial P_{i}}\ \ ,\ \ \dot{P}_{i}=-\frac{\partial H}{\partial Q_{i}}

(A.37)

Let us check that these equations generate time evolution. Since
A
is independent of
P_{1}
, the first equation becomes

\displaystyle\dot{Q}_{1}=\frac{\partial H}{\partial P_{1}}=Q_{2}

(A.38)

Just as expected this equation reproduces the time evolution
\dot{q}=\ddot{q}
. Similarly, the equation for
Q_{2}
yields

\displaystyle\dot{Q}_{2}=\frac{\partial H}{\partial P_{2}}=A+P_{2}\frac{\partial A}{\partial P_{2}}-\frac{\partial L}{\partial\ddot{q}}\frac{\partial A}{\partial P_{2}}=A

(A.39)

where (
A.35
) is used. The momentum definition
P_{1}
in (
A.34
) comes from the evolution equation for
P_{2}
:

\displaystyle\dot{P}_{2}=-\frac{\partial H}{\partial Q_{2}}=-P_{1}-P_{2}\frac{\partial A}{\partial Q_{2}}+\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{q}}+\frac{\partial L}{\partial\ddot{q}}\frac{\partial A}{\partial Q_{2}}=-P_{1}+\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{q}}

(A.40)

where (
A.35
) is used again. Finally, using (
A.35
), the evolution equation for
P_{1}
read

\displaystyle\dot{P}_{1}=-\frac{\partial H}{\partial Q_{1}}=-P_{2}\frac{\partial A}{\partial Q_{1}}+\frac{\partial L}{\partial q}+\frac{\partial L}{\partial\ddot{q}}\frac{\partial A}{\partial Q_{1}}=\frac{\partial L}{\partial q}

(A.41)

which, purely in terms of
q
read

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{q}}-\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial L}{\partial\ddot{q}}\right)=\frac{\partial L}{\partial q}

(A.42)

Thus, the canonical equation for
P_{1}
reproduces the EL equation (
A.32
).

A.2.2
Second-Order Singular Lagrangian

The main reference for this section is
[
pons
]
.

Given a second-order singular Lagrangian
L(q,\dot{q},\ddot{q})
we define new variables

\displaystyle q_{1}\equiv q\ \ ,\ \ q_{2}\equiv\dot{q}\ \ ,\ \ \dot{q}_{2}\equiv\ddot{q}

(A.43)

and introduce the Lagrangian constraint

\displaystyle q_{2}=\dot{q}_{1}

(A.44)

Then we form the first-order Lagrangian
L_{T}

\displaystyle L_{T}(q_{1},q_{2},\dot{q}_{1},\dot{q}_{2},\lambda)=L(q_{1},q_{2},\dot{q}_{2})+\lambda(\dot{q}_{1}-q_{2})

(A.45)

where
\lambda
is the Lagrange multiplier for the constraint (
A.44
). The definition of momenta, then, yield

\displaystyle P_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial L_{T}}{\partial\dot{q}_{1}}=\lambda

(A.46)

\displaystyle P_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial L_{T}}{\partial\dot{q}_{2}}=\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{q}_{2}}

(A.47)

\displaystyle\pi

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial L_{T}}{\partial\dot{\lambda}}=0

(A.48)

All of these constitute a primary constraint :
P_{1}
is independent of
\dot{q}_{1}
,
\pi
is independent of
\dot{\lambda}
and since by hypothesis
L
is singular,
P_{2}
is independent of
\dot{q}_{2}
.
Hence, we can identify the primary constraints

\displaystyle\chi_{1}\equiv P_{1}-\lambda=0

(A.49)

\displaystyle\chi_{2}\equiv\pi=0

(A.50)

\displaystyle\phi(q_{1},q_{2},P_{2})\equiv P_{2}-\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{q}_{2}}=0

(A.51)

Out of these only the last one is essential, the first two followed due to the prescription applied to obtain a first-order Lagrangian. These will be eliminated below.

Let us show the equivalence of the EL equations for the first-order Lagrangian
L_{T}
and the Ostrogradsky equations for
L
:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial L_{T}}{\partial\dot{q}_{1}}-\frac{\partial L_{T}}{\partial q_{1}}=0\Leftrightarrow\dot{P}_{1}=\frac{\partial L}{\partial q_{1}}

where we used (
A.49
). Similarly, for
q_{2}
we get

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial L_{T}}{\partial\dot{q}_{2}}-\frac{\partial L_{T}}{\partial q_{2}}=0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\dot{P}_{2}=\frac{\partial L}{\partial q_{2}}-\lambda

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\dot{P}_{2}=\frac{\partial L}{\partial q_{2}}-P_{1}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow P_{1}=\frac{\partial L}{\partial q_{2}}-\dot{P}_{2}

If we insert the second equation into the first we get

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial L}{\partial q_{1}}-\frac{dP_{1}}{dt}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial L}{\partial q_{1}}-\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial L}{\partial q_{2}}+\frac{d^{2}P_{2}}{dt^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial L}{\partial q_{1}}-\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial L}{\partial q_{2}}+\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{q}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial L}{\partial x}-\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{x}}+\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\frac{\partial L}{\partial\ddot{x}}

(A.52)

We build the naive Hamiltonian as usual.

\displaystyle H_{c}=P_{1}q_{2}+\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{q}_{2}}\dot{q}_{2}-L(q_{1},q_{2},\dot{q}_{2})

(A.53)

We define

\displaystyle H_{2}(q_{1},q_{2},P_{2})\equiv\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{q}_{2}}\dot{q}_{2}-L(q_{1},q_{2},\dot{q}_{2})

(A.54)

This corresponds to the energy functional for
q_{2}
and
P_{2}
with the dynamics of
q_{1}
frozen. Note that
H_{2}
will not involve
\dot{q}_{2}
since those terms involving it will cancel in the subtraction above. Then the canonical Hamiltonian becomes

\displaystyle H_{c}=P_{1}q_{2}+H_{2}(q_{1},q_{2},P_{2})

(A.55)

Then using the primary constraints we can build the Dirac Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H_{D}=H_{c}+\eta(P_{1}-\lambda)+\gamma\pi+\omega\phi

(A.56)

The term
\gamma\pi
affecting only the evolution of
\lambda
can be ignored. The consistency condition of
\pi=0
reads

\displaystyle 0=\{\pi,H_{D}\}=\eta\{\pi,\lambda\}=\eta\approx 0

(A.57)

so we can set the second term
\eta(P_{1}-\lambda)
to zero as well. Hence, we have the simplified Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=H_{c}+\omega\phi(q_{1},q_{2},P_{2})

\displaystyle=P_{1}q_{2}+H_{2}(q_{1},q_{2},P_{2})+\omega\phi(q_{1},q_{2},P_{2})

(A.58)

The rest of the analysis is the standard application of Dirac’s method i.e. we check the consistency equations for the primary constraints to look for secondary constraints and build the extended Hamiltonian etc. We will skip that to avoid repetition.

Appendix B
Miscellaneous

B.1
Lightcone Coordinates

In this section we are going to introduce the conventions used in 2D fermionic theories. These mostly agree with the conventions in
[
WZNW
]
.

The lightcone coordinates (LCC) are defined through
x^{\pm}=(x^{0}\pm x^{1})/\sqrt{2}
. These are inverted to give
x^{0}=(x^{+}+x^{-})/\sqrt{2}
and
x^{1}=(x^{+}-x^{-})/\sqrt{2}
.
(Recall that the metric signature adopted in this thesis is
(+t,-x)
.) Using the transformation of the metric under a coordinate transformation
we get
g_{++}=0=g_{--}
and
g_{+-}=g_{-+}=1
. These imply
g^{++}=0=g^{--}
and
g^{+-}=1=g^{-+}
.

Using the metric in LCC we get
A_{\pm}=A^{\mp}
, and
A_{\mu}B^{\mu}=A^{+}B^{-}+A^{-}B^{+}=A_{+}B_{-}+A_{-}B_{+}
. If we normalize the Levi-Civita symbol
\varepsilon_{\mu\nu}
so that
\varepsilon_{01}=1
, we also get
\varepsilon_{+-}=-1=\epsilon^{-+}
.

The Dirac algebra is defined through
\{\gamma_{\mu},\gamma_{\nu}\}=2\eta_{\mu\nu}
, and we have
\overline{\psi}=\psi^{*}\gamma^{0}
. A convenient representation is given by

\displaystyle\gamma^{0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)\hskip 14.45377pt\gamma^{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)\hskip 14.45377pt\gamma_{c}=\gamma^{0}\gamma^{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right)

so that the chirality matrix satisfies
\gamma^{2}_{c}=+1
. In LCC we have

\displaystyle\gamma^{\pm}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\gamma^{0}\pm\gamma^{1})\ \ \ ,\ \ \ (\gamma^{+})^{2}=0=(\gamma^{-})^{2}\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \gamma^{+}\gamma^{-}+\gamma^{-}\gamma^{+}=2

(B.7)

Then for the representation (
B.1
) we find

\displaystyle\gamma^{+}=\sqrt{2}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
1&0\end{array}\right)\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \gamma^{-}=\sqrt{2}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
0&0\end{array}\right)

We define the chiral components
\psi_{\pm}
of
\psi
by requiring
\gamma_{c}\psi_{\pm}=\mp\psi_{\pm}
. Thus
\psi=(\psi_{-}\ \psi_{+})^{T}
.
We also have

\displaystyle\overline{\psi}=\psi^{\dagger}\gamma^{0}=\psi^{T}\gamma^{0}=(\psi_{-}\ \psi_{+})\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right)=(\psi_{+}\ \psi_{-})

The Dirac equation in flat spacetime reads
i\gamma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\psi=0
, which, in LCC, become

\displaystyle\partial_{\pm}\psi_{\mp}=0

(B.14)

Therefore,
\psi_{-}
represent left movers and
\psi_{+}
represent right movers.

For the massless Dirac particle, the vector current
\overline{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\psi
and the axial current
\overline{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma_{c}\psi
are both conserved. In 2D these are related by

\displaystyle\overline{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma_{c}\psi=\varepsilon^{\mu\nu}\overline{\psi}\gamma^{\nu}\psi

(B.15)

So, the current conservation equations combine, and can be written as

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}J^{\mu}=0=\varepsilon^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}J_{\nu}

(B.16)

In LCC these yield

\displaystyle\partial_{-}J_{+}=0=\partial_{+}J_{-}

(B.17)

B.2
Vanishing of the
\beta
term

Consider the generic form of the Virasoro algebra

\displaystyle[L_{m},L_{n}]=(m-n)L_{m+n}+(cm^{3}+hm)\delta_{m+n}

(B.18)

where
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
In particular this implies that

\displaystyle[L_{m},L_{-m}]=2mL_{0}+(cm^{3}+hm)=2m(L_{0}+h/2)+cm^{3}

(B.19)

Hence, if we redefine the generators as

\displaystyle L^{\prime}_{0}=L_{0}+h/2\ \ \ ,\ \ \ L^{\prime}_{m}=L_{m}\ ,\ m\neq 0

(B.20)

then the new generators satisfy

\displaystyle[L^{\prime}_{m},L^{\prime}_{n}]=(m-n)L^{\prime}_{m+n}+cm^{3}\delta_{m+n}

(B.21)

It is straightforward to show that the redefinition only affects the commutator (
B.19
). This is a standard trick in string theory and shows that only the cubic center has a geometric meaning (see
[
GSWsuperstring
]
, Section 3.2.2).

Then the pairing (
2.86
) between the Virasoro adjoint and coadjoint elements implies that the coadjoint transformation,

\displaystyle\delta D=\xi D^{\prime}+2\xi^{\prime}+\beta\xi^{\prime}+q\xi^{\prime\prime\prime}

(B.22)

of a Virasoro coadjoint element
D
can be simplified to

\displaystyle\delta D=\xi D^{\prime}+2\xi^{\prime}D+q\xi^{\prime\prime\prime}

(B.23)

Therefore, the
\beta
central extension term is at our disposal; it can be turned on when needed, and off when not needed.

B.3
Schwarzian Derivative

The Schwarzian derivative is defined as

\displaystyle Sf(x)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{d}{dx}\left(\log\left(\frac{df}{dx}\right)\right)-\frac{1}{2}\left(\log\left(\frac{df}{dx}\right)\right)^{2}

(B.24)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(x)}{f^{\prime}(x)}\right)^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(x)}{f^{\prime}(x)}\right)^{2}

(B.25)

\displaystyle=\frac{f^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)}{f^{\prime}(x)}-\frac{3}{2}\left(\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(x)}{f^{\prime}(x)}\right)^{2}

(B.26)

where prime denotes derivative with respect to
x
, and
x
may refer to a real or complex variable depending on the context.

For an infinitesimal map
f(x)=x-\xi(x)
the Schwarzian can be evaluated to be
Sf(x)=-\xi^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)
. We will frequently use the alternative notation
S(x,f)\equiv Sf(x)
. When we are not interested in the argument of the Schwarzian derivative, and need to consider compositions we also use the notation
S(f)\equiv Sf(x)
.

Schwarzian derivative satisfies the identity

\displaystyle S(g\circ f)=(f^{\prime})^{2}\ S(g)\circ f+S(f),

(B.27)

which we can explicitly write as

\displaystyle S(x,g\circ f)=(f^{\prime}(x))^{2}\ S(f(x),g\circ f)+S(x,f)

(B.28)

Evaluating this for
g=f^{-1}
, and denoting
v=x
,
f^{-1}(v)=u
we get another useful identity

\displaystyle(Sw)(v)=-\left(\frac{dw}{dv}\right)^{2}(Sv)(w)

(B.29)

Fractional linear transformations

\displaystyle x\mapsto\frac{ax+b}{cx+d}

(B.30)

with
ad-bc\neq 0
form the kernel of the Schwarzian derivative , i.e.

\displaystyle Sf(z)=0\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ f(z)=\frac{az+b}{cz+d}\ ,\ ad-bc\neq 0

(B.31)

This holds true for real or complex
x,a,b,c,d
. In the former case transformations (
B.30
) form
PGL(2,\mathbb{R})
and in the latter case
PGL(2,\mathbb{C})
.

As a consequence of (
B.31
), for an arbitrary fractional linear transformation
f
and an arbitrary smooth map
g
we have

\displaystyle S(f\circ g)=S(g)

(B.32)

So, Schwarzian is a projective invariant.

B.4
Invariance of
M^{(2)}[u]

Under the action of
\phi\in\text{Diff}_{0}(S^{1})
, a
-1/2
density
\psi
transforms as

\displaystyle\phi\circ\psi(x)=\psi(\phi(x))[\phi^{\prime}(x)]^{-1/2}

(B.33)

Since
\phi\in\text{Diff}_{0}(S^{1})
it satisfies

\displaystyle\phi(0)=0\ ,\ \phi^{\prime}(0)=1\ ,\ \phi^{\prime\prime}(0)=0

(B.34)

Since
\phi
is a diffeomorphism on
S^{1}
we also have

\displaystyle\phi(x+2\pi)=\phi(x)+2\pi

(B.35)

which implies

\displaystyle\phi^{(n)}(x+2\pi)=\phi^{(n)}(x)\ ,\ \text{for}\ n>0

(B.36)

In particular we have

\displaystyle\phi(2\pi)=\phi(0)+2\pi\ \ \ ,\ \ \ \phi^{(n)}(2\pi)=\phi^{(n)}(0)\ \text{for}\ n>0

(B.37)

The Wilson loop equation reads

\displaystyle\psi(2\pi)=M[u]\psi(0)\ \

(B.38)

Evaluating this for the transformed fields we get

\displaystyle(\phi\circ\psi)(2\pi)=M[u^{\phi}](\phi\circ\psi)(0)

(B.39)

We would like to show invariance of the Wilson loop i.e.

\displaystyle M[u^{\phi}]=M[u]

(B.40)

Evaluating (
B.33
) at
x=0
, and using (
B.34
) we get

\displaystyle\phi(\psi(0))

\displaystyle=\psi(\phi(0))[\phi^{\prime}(0)]^{-1/2}=\psi(0)

(B.41)

Next, evaluating (
B.33
) at
x=2\pi
, and using (
B.34
), (
B.35
), (
B.37
) we get

\displaystyle\phi(\psi(2\pi))

\displaystyle=\psi(\phi(2\pi))[\phi^{\prime}(2\pi)]^{-1/2}=\psi(2\pi)

(B.42)

Inserting (
B.41
) and (
B.42
) in (
B.39
) we get the desired result (
B.40
).
